fluid-mechanics.bigb
= Fluid mechanics
{wiki}

<Fluid mechanics> studies the motion and <forces> of <liquids> and <gases>. Its central simplification is the <continuum approximation>: replace individual <molecules> by bulk properties defined at every position and time. The averaging region must contain many <molecules> while remaining small compared with the distances over which those properties vary. A <fluid element> is such a small material parcel, rather than a single <molecule>. This connects <fluid mechanics> to <continuum mechanics> and makes <calculus> useful for describing flow. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/2-016-hydrodynamics-13-012-fall-2005/dc299d2ff05553b3515dccc23fc0f7d2_2005reading1.pdf[MIT, A. Techet, Hydrodynamics reading 1, pp. 2–3]{ref}

Three basic fields are the <velocity field> $\mathbf u(\mathbf x,t)$, the <mass density> $\rho(\mathbf x,t)$ and the <pressure> $p(\mathbf x,t)$. The first gives a parcel's instantaneous <velocity>; the second gives <mass> per unit <volume>. The <pressure> contributes an inward normal <traction> $-p\mathbf n$ across a surface with outward unit normal $\mathbf n$. Thus <pressure> is a <stress>, measured in $\mathrm{Pa}=\mathrm{N\,m^{-2}}$, and may vary with position even in <incompressible flow>. Additional <viscous stresses> can act tangentially. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/2-016-hydrodynamics-13-012-fall-2005/dc299d2ff05553b3515dccc23fc0f7d2_2005reading1.pdf[MIT, A. Techet, Hydrodynamics reading 1, p. 5]{ref}

The guiding questions are what must be conserved and which <forces> matter. <Mass conservation> constrains the <velocity field>; <momentum conservation> determines its response to <pressure gradients>, <viscosity> and body <forces>. An <incompressible flow> model, a <Reynolds number> estimate and <Bernoulli's principle> answer different parts of a problem. Their appeal is that a few carefully stated assumptions can determine useful quantities before the entire <velocity field> is known.

= Laminar flow
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

<Laminar flow> is a regime of <fluid flow> in which the motion remains organized, with neighboring fluid parcels following smooth, coherent paths rather than the irregular interacting fluctuations characteristic of <turbulence>. In a straight pipe, the simplest example consists of coaxial layers sliding past one another. This describes the character of the motion, not a requirement that every parcel have the same <velocity>. Nor does it require <steady flow>: a smooth profile can evolve in time as <viscosity> transmits motion from a newly moving wall into a resting <fluid>. The unsteady wall-driven solution provides an explicit example. https://notes.shreyasmandre.com/fluid-dynamics-notes/Ch3.html[Shreyas Mandre, Fluid Dynamics Lecture Notes, section 3.3]{ref}

The <Reynolds pipe-and-dye experiment> makes this distinction visible. A thin injected dye band can remain coherent in <laminar flow>, whereas irregular motion can fold it and carry dye across the pipe. However, <molecular diffusion> can spread dye even in <laminar flow>, and an inlet disturbance can move a coherent band without producing <turbulence>. A dye band is a <streakline>, not a measurement of the entire <velocity field>; in <steady flow> an ideal passive tracer's <streakline> coincides with a <streamline>. Reynolds explicitly distinguished a wandering band from one broken into eddies. https://archive.org/stream/philtrans06508925/06508925_djvu.txt[Osborne Reynolds, An Experimental Investigation (1883), section 11, pp. 941–943]{ref}

For a circular pipe of internal diameter $D$, define the mean axial <velocity> $U$ using the <volumetric flow rate> $Q$ and cross-sectional <area> $A$:
$$
A=\frac{\pi D^2}{4},\qquad U=\frac QA,\qquad
\mathrm{Re}_D=\frac{UD}{\nu}=\frac{\rho UD}{\mu}
=\frac{4Q}{\pi D\nu}.
$$
Here $\rho$ is <mass density>, $\mu$ is <dynamic viscosity> and $\nu=\mu/\rho$ is <kinematic viscosity>. This particular <Reynolds number> uses the diameter and mean <velocity>, not the radius or centreline <velocity>. Small <Reynolds number> generally favors <laminar flow>, but a single universal critical <Reynolds number> does not distinguish it from <turbulence> in all geometries and experiments. Even in a given pipe, inlet conditions and the magnitude of disturbances affect the observed onset. Reynolds demonstrated this dependence by deliberately inserting a wire coil. https://archive.org/stream/philtrans06508925/06508925_djvu.txt[Osborne Reynolds (1883), section 23, pp. 955–956]{ref}

\Image[laminar-pipe-velocity-profile.png]
{title=Ideal laminar pipe flow: zero velocity at the wall and twice the mean velocity at the centre}
{description=An illustrative fully developed Poiseuille solution, not measured data. The arrows and curve use a 10 mm diameter pipe with mean axial velocity 0.10 m/s. The dashed line marks the mean over the circular cross-section, not the mean along a diameter.}

The quantitative example above assumes <steady flow>, <incompressible flow>, a <Newtonian fluid> with constant <dynamic viscosity>, a straight horizontal circular pipe of radius $R=D/2$, stationary walls satisfying the <no-slip boundary condition>, and <fully developed flow>. The last assumption means that the axial <velocity> profile no longer changes downstream. A positive <pressure> drop $\Delta p=p_{\rm in}-p_{\rm out}$ over a developed length $L$ gives the <Hagen-Poiseuille equation>:
$$
u(r)=\frac{\Delta p}{4\mu L}(R^2-r^2)
=2U\left(1-\frac{r^2}{R^2}\right),\qquad
Q=\int_0^R u(r)\,2\pi r\,dr
=\frac{\pi R^4\Delta p}{8\mu L}.
$$
The <velocity> is zero at the wall and maximal at the centre. Averaging over the circular <area>, whose annuli have <area> $2\pi r\,dr$, gives $U=u(0)/2$; averaging along one diameter would give a different answer. These assumptions produce a <parabola>, rather than every <laminar flow> having a parabolic profile. https://notes.shreyasmandre.com/fluid-dynamics-notes/Ch3.html[Shreyas Mandre, Fluid Dynamics Lecture Notes, section 3.2.2, equations (3.9)–(3.11)]{ref}

As a stipulated calculation, take $D=0.010\,\mathrm m$, $U=0.10\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, $\rho=1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, $\mu=0.0010\,\mathrm{Pa\,s}$ and $L=1.0\,\mathrm m$, and assume the developed <laminar flow> above. Then $\nu=10^{-6}\,\mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}$, $\mathrm{Re}_D=1000$, $u(0)=0.20\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, $Q=7.85\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{m^3\,s^{-1}}$ (about $0.471\,\mathrm{L\,min^{-1}}$), and $\Delta p=32\mu LU/D^2=32\,\mathrm{Pa}$. These are predictions from chosen parameters, not measurements or a claim that $\mathrm{Re}_D=1000$ guarantees <laminar flow> in every apparatus.

The beauty of this example is that a spatially varying <velocity field> still gives an exact relation between two accessible measurements, <pressure> drop and <volumetric flow rate>. It predicts the <hydraulic resistance> $\Delta p/Q=8\mu L/(\pi R^4)$. Doubling the radius therefore multiplies $Q$ by sixteen at fixed <pressure> drop, length and <dynamic viscosity>, provided the model remains applicable. This makes the <laminar flow> model useful for sizing narrow tubes or determining <dynamic viscosity>. It also explains why a persistent narrow dye band can be informative without implying that the fluid is moving as a uniform plug.

= Reynolds pipe-and-dye experiment
{c}
{parent=Laminar flow}

In his 1883 paper, Osborne Reynolds investigated how the character of <pipe flow> depends jointly on <velocity>, pipe diameter and <viscosity>. His final dye apparatus drew water from a large glass tank through a horizontal glass tube with a smooth trumpet-shaped entrance. A separate reservoir supplied strongly colored water just upstream of the entrance; a valve below the tank controlled the outflow. The glass tube was immersed in the tank, so its internal dye band could be watched. Reynolds repeated the trials with different tube diameters and water <temperatures>. https://archive.org/stream/philtrans06508925/06508925_djvu.txt[Osborne Reynolds (1883), sections 11 and 21–22, pp. 941–943 and 951–953]{ref}

\Image[reynolds-pipe-dye-apparatus.png]
{title=Reynolds' pipe-and-dye experiment: observe the dye while measuring discharge and temperature}
{description=Original schematic of the historical operating principle, not a scale reconstruction. A falling tank level determines discharged volume; timing it gives flow rate. The lower dye patterns are conceptual sketches of a coherent band and irregular spreading, not measured trajectories or concentration records.}

The <volumetric flow rate> was measured independently of the appearance of the dye. Reynolds timed a one-inch fall of the tank's water level, corresponding to a calibrated discharged <volume>. In modern notation, if this volume is $V$ and the elapsed time is $\Delta t$, then $Q=V/\Delta t$ and $U=4V/(\pi D^2\Delta t)$. He determined the glass tubes' internal diameters by filling them with water. For a uniform bore, a known filled length $\ell$ and measured water <volume> $V_{\rm tube}$ determine $D=\sqrt{4V_{\rm tube}/(\pi\ell)}$; checking bore uniformity matters because the same <volume> would only give an average cross-section in a tapered tube. Thermometers at different tank levels checked both the <temperature> and its uniformity. Reynolds compared the <viscosity> at different <temperatures> using Poiseuille's results and checked his own water by separate experiments of that type. Thus the <Reynolds number> estimate did not come from judging the dye band or from assuming that transition occurred at a preset value. https://archive.org/stream/philtrans06508925/06508925_djvu.txt[Osborne Reynolds (1883), sections 21–22 and the reductions following Table I, pp. 951–955]{ref}

A practical repetition can instead collect a known outflow <volume> in a measuring vessel and time its collection, or weigh the collected water and divide its <mass> by <mass density>. Measure the internal diameter, rather than the external diameter, and obtain <kinematic viscosity> at the measured water <temperature> from independently established properties or a separate <viscosity> measurement. Record the dye appearance and observation position alongside $Q$, $D$, <temperature> and the resulting <Reynolds number>. These are alternative measurement procedures following $Q=V/\Delta t$, not additional historical claims about Reynolds' dye apparatus.

Begin with settled water and a small dye supply, then open the outlet valve in small steps. Reynolds saw a thin straight band at sufficiently low speeds. With a tank that had not fully settled, the band could wander while retaining its coherence. At higher discharge it could abruptly spread into the surrounding water downstream of the entrance. Short illumination by an electric spark resolved curls within what otherwise looked like a colored cloud. This connected the visible breakup with internal irregular motion rather than merely the appearance of an opaque pipe. https://archive.org/stream/philtrans06508925/06508925_djvu.txt[Osborne Reynolds (1883), sections 11 and 22, pp. 942 and 951–952]{ref}

The controls are part of the result. Reynolds used a smooth entrance and let the tank rest to reduce inlet disturbances. He found that a heated tank could retain slow circulation as it cooled, so measuring one <temperature> was insufficient: thermal uniformity and residual motion also mattered. His wire-coil trial broke up the band below the previous onset speed, but he explicitly cautioned that once the dye was mixed he could not tell from that dye alone whether the motion became orderly again farther along the tube. In a repetition, keep the dye supply small enough to act approximately as a <passive scalar>, avoid a strong injection jet, and record the inlet condition and downstream observation distance. If also testing the <Hagen-Poiseuille equation>, allow the profile to develop and measure <pressure> drop between positions within the developed region; do not treat the entrance and outlet losses as part of its uniform <pressure gradient>. The dye experiment by itself does not establish <fully developed flow>. https://archive.org/stream/philtrans06508925/06508925_djvu.txt[Osborne Reynolds (1883), sections 21–23 and 26, pp. 951–956 and 959]{ref}

The experiment's usefulness is that it compares a visible change in the motion with independently measured scales. Repeating it with several diameters and <temperatures>, while controlling disturbances, tests the organizing role of the <Reynolds number>. Its limitations are equally instructive: dye is a tracer of transport, <diffusion> still acts in <laminar flow>, and a local dye observation cannot by itself prove that <turbulence> persists throughout an arbitrarily long pipe.

= Void fraction
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{title2=$\phi=V_{\rm gas}/V_{\rm mixture}$}

The <void fraction> is the fraction of a mixture's local volume occupied by gas. For a horizontally uniform bubbly liquid it is the transported concentration in a one-dimensional gas-volume <continuity equation>. It is distinct from gas mass fraction because gas and liquid have different <mass densities>.

= Kinematic bubble transport in an exponentially narrowing vessel
{parent=Void fraction}
{title2=$\phi_t+V_s(1-2\phi)\phi_z=2\beta V_s\phi(1-\phi)$}

If the vessel radius is proportional to $e^{-\beta z}$, its area is proportional to $e^{-2\beta z}$. Gas-volume conservation gives the displayed equation. Along concentration <characteristic curves>, $\dot z=V_s(1-2\phi)$ and $\dot\phi=2\beta V_s\phi(1-\phi)$. Uniform initial concentration stays uniform in the uninterrupted bubbly region and follows this logistic law. Individual bubbles move at $V_s(1-\phi)$ instead: their rise distance there is $\eta=-(2\beta)^{-1}\log[\phi_0+(1-\phi_0)e^{-2\beta V_st}]$. Boundary interactions and dense-mixture backflow can invalidate the simplified closure.

= Bubble clearing in a cylindrical vessel
{parent=Void fraction}
{title2=$\Phi(t)=\phi_0[1-V_s(1-\phi_0)t/H]_+$}

For initially uniform bubbles with upward speed $V_s(1-\phi)$, no new gas production and free escape at the top, gas conservation gives flux $V_s\phi(1-\phi)$. A clearing <shock> rises from the impermeable bottom with speed $V_s(1-\phi_0)$ by the <Rankine-Hugoniot condition>. The displayed mean <void fraction> averages the remaining uniform region over the whole liquid depth $H$. This assumes the dilute regime and no retained foam layer.

= Strouhal number
{c}
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{title2=$St=\omega h/U$}

The Strouhal number compares the advective time $h/U$ with a prescribed forcing time $1/\omega$. Some conventions use ordinary frequency instead of angular frequency, introducing a factor $2\pi$. For a longitudinal disturbance extending over length $\lambda h$, the relevant ratio is $\lambda St$ rather than $St$ alone.

= Lift
{disambiguate=force}
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

= Lift
{synonym}

<Lift> is the component of the <fluid> <force> perpendicular to the far-field flow relative to the body. It need not point vertically: a tilted flow changes the lift direction, and a banked wing can produce a sideways component. In a planar problem choose a unit <vector> $\mathbf e_D$ along the incoming relative <velocity> $\mathbf U_\infty$ and a perpendicular unit <vector> $\mathbf e_L$ pointing towards the chosen positive-lift side. Then
$$
U_\infty=|\mathbf U_\infty|,\qquad
D=\mathbf F\cdot\mathbf e_D,\qquad
L=\mathbf F\cdot\mathbf e_L,\qquad
\mathbf F=D\mathbf e_D+L\mathbf e_L.
$$
Here $D$ is <drag> and $L$ is signed <lift>; $L<0$ means a <force> towards the opposite side. In three dimensions the plane perpendicular to $\mathbf U_\infty$ also admits a separate side-force direction, so a lift axis must be specified. <Lift> is also distinct from <buoyancy>, which can exist without a relative flow. https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/aerodynamic-forces/[NASA Glenn, Aerodynamic Forces: components relative to the flow]{ref}

The <force> comes from adding local surface <tractions>, rather than assigning a single <pressure> to each side. Let $\Sigma$ be the wetted body surface and $\mathbf n$ its unit <normal vector> pointing out of the body into the <fluid>. The <pressure> contribution pushes inwards; the <viscous stress tensor> also contributes, including tangential <shear stress>:
$$
\mathbf F=\int_\Sigma\left[-(p-p_\infty)\mathbf n+\boldsymbol\sigma_{\mathrm{visc}}\mathbf n\right]dA,
\qquad
L=\int_\Sigma\left[-(p-p_\infty)\mathbf n+\boldsymbol\sigma_{\mathrm{visc}}\mathbf n\right]\cdot\mathbf e_L\,dA.
$$
Using a constant reference <pressure> $p_\infty$ is legitimate on a closed surface because $\int_\Sigma\mathbf n\,dA=0$. This <surface integral> makes the direction explicit: a surface-normal <force> is generally neither pure <lift> nor pure <drag>. <Pressure> and skin <shear stress> both enter the resultant, although the <pressure> term usually dominates <lift> on an <airfoil>. https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/aerodynamic-forces/[NASA Glenn, Aerodynamic Forces: pressure and shear contributions]{ref}

\Image[lift-airfoils-pressure-geometry.png]
{title=Local <pressure> forces and <lift> relative to the far-field flow}
{description=Original toy geometry: a thin diamond-shaped section at $10\,{}^\circ$ <angle of attack>, with prescribed <pressure coefficients> $C_{p,u}=-0.8(1-x/c)$ above and $C_{p,l}=0.4(1-x/c)$ below. Arrows show gauge-pressure contributions: negative gauge pressure gives an outward contribution after the uniform ambient pressure has cancelled over the closed surface. The right panel resolves the integrated pressure force into flow axes. These prescribed pressures are neither measured data nor a solved flow; the corners and thickness are illustrative.}

The <lift coefficient> isolates a useful scale. For a finite wing of reference area $S$ (normally its projected planform area), and for a two-dimensional <airfoil> section of chord length $c$, respectively,
$$
q_\infty=\frac12\rho_\infty U_\infty^2,\qquad
C_L=\frac{L}{q_\infty S},\qquad
c_l=\frac{L'}{q_\infty c}.
$$
Here $q_\infty$ is <dynamic pressure>, $\rho_\infty$ is far-field <mass density>, and $L'$ is <lift> per unit span, with units $\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}}$. The signs of $C_L$ and $c_l$ follow the chosen lift axis. For stipulated values $\rho_\infty=1.20\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, $U_\infty=20\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, $S=0.50\,\mathrm{m^2}$ and $C_L=0.60$, $q_\infty=240\,\mathrm{Pa}$ and $L=72\,\mathrm N$. Doubling $U_\infty$ gives four times the <lift> only if $C_L$ stays fixed: changes in <Reynolds number>, <Mach number>, surface condition or <angle of attack> can change it. https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/lift-coefficient/[NASA Glenn, Lift Coefficient: normalization and similarity limits]{ref}

A motivating experiment is to compare two independently observed quantities: the <lift> registered by a force balance, and the <lift> inferred from surface <pressures>. A low-speed wind tunnel holds the model fixed while air passes it at adjustable speed; a calibrated balance measures <forces>, while small flush holes on both surfaces connect by tubing to differential-pressure sensors referenced to $p_\infty$. Iowa State's GA(W)-1 teaching model has $43$ taps around a $101\,\mathrm{mm}$ chord; its instructions give their coordinates and close the contour by repeating the first tap. A classroom comparison can combine this pressure-panel method with a balance on the same model; this is an experiment design, not a claim that those instructions report a combined measurement. https://www.aere.iastate.edu/~huhui/teaching/2026-08F/AerE344/lab-instruction/AerE344L-Lab-05-Pressure-Airfoil-instruction.pdf[Hui Hu, Iowa State, AerE344 Lab 05, p. 2: GA(W)-1 taps and pressure panels]{ref} https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/drag-measurement/[NASA Glenn, Drag Measurement: force balances and calibration]{ref}

\Image[lift-airfoils-wind-tunnel.png]
{title=Compare a force balance with integrated <airfoil> <pressure> measurements}
{description=Original schematic of a proposed low-speed experiment, not a drawing of a particular facility. The model has a rotatable mount, surface taps and two independent measurement routes. Only a few taps are drawn. A far-field total/static pressure probe supplies the speed scale; balance calibration uses known loads, and pressure calibration uses known pressure differences. No measured curves or results are shown.}

Before turning on the wind, apply known positive and negative loads to the balance through its resolving centre; use $mg$ for the known load from a mass. Record all balance channels during loading and unloading, including response to <drag> and <torque> loads, to detect cross coupling, hysteresis and zero drift. Record a wind-off tare at each model orientation so its <weight> does not become an aerodynamic <lift> reading. Map tunnel flow direction and uniformity, rather than using the fan setting as a speed measurement. Covert's MIT guide describes these calibration, tare and flow-survey requirements. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/16-621-experimental-projects-i-spring-2003/f38ecde8773fd36c0d8f9069e6aa1df5_student_wt_guide.pdf[Eugene E. Covert, A Student's Introduction to the Wright Brothers Wind Tunnel at MIT, pp. 3–8]{ref}

For each pressure channel, apply zero and several known <pressure> differences spanning the expected range; determine the voltage-to-pressure relation and recheck it after a run. Inspect tubing for leaks and blocked taps, and wait for readings to settle before averaging: tubing can delay a pressure signal. This calibration principle is used in NASA's electronically scanned pressure systems, where several applied calibration pressures determine each sensor's response and repeated calibration tracks drift. A simple pressure standard can instead use a liquid-column height difference and the <hydrostatic pressure> law. https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19890009044/downloads/19890009044.pdf[Patsy S. Fulton, NASA-TM-101531, p. 8: pressure-module calibration]{ref}

Measure far-field static and total <pressure> outside the model's <fluid wake>; in low-speed <incompressible flow> their difference gives $q_\infty$ by the <Bernoulli equation>, so $U_\infty=\sqrt{2q_\infty/\rho_\infty}$. Record <temperature> and ambient <pressure> to determine <mass density> using the <ideal gas law>. At fixed $q_\infty$, step the <angle of attack>, repeat selected settings, and sweep back down to check reproducibility. Record balance <lift>, each tap's mean <pressure>, and fluctuation sizes. NASA describes pressure probes connected to differential transducers and emphasizes recording their reference <pressure>. https://www.grc.nasa.gov/www/k-12/airplane/tunptm.html[NASA Glenn, Total Pressure Measurement: probes, tubing and pressure references]{ref}

Convert each tap to a <pressure coefficient> $C_{p,i}=(p_i-p_\infty)/q_\infty$. For consecutive taps bounding a panel of length $\Delta s_i$ with outward <normal vector> $\mathbf n_i$, a <trapezoidal rule> estimate of the pressure <force> per unit span is
$$
\mathbf F'_p\simeq-q_\infty\sum_i\frac{C_{p,i}+C_{p,i+1}}2\mathbf n_i\Delta s_i,\qquad
L'_p=\mathbf F'_p\cdot\mathbf e_L.
$$
Compare this with balance <lift> divided by span only when the spanwise flow is approximately uniform. Agreement tests the pressure-resultant picture; disagreement calls for checking unresolved leading-edge pressure variations, skin <shear stress>, support loads and three-dimensional end effects before rejecting it. A force balance senses the total load, while taps alone reconstruct the pressure part. Tunnel walls and model blockage alter the effective flow and require corrections; end plates or a model extending between walls approximate two-dimensional flow but do not guarantee it. Record the <Reynolds number> and <Mach number> with the result, and do not interpret tunnel <lift coefficients> as universal wing data. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/16-621-experimental-projects-i-spring-2003/f38ecde8773fd36c0d8f9069e6aa1df5_student_wt_guide.pdf[Covert, pp. 8–9: flow characterization and wall effects]{ref} https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/lift-coefficient/[NASA Glenn, Lift Coefficient: Reynolds and Mach number dependence]{ref}

= Lift coefficient
{parent=Lift (force)}
{title2=$C_L=L/(q_\infty S)$}

The signed <lift> divided by <dynamic pressure> times a stated reference area. For a two-dimensional <airfoil>, use $c_l=L'/(q_\infty c)$ instead. Reference area and experimental flow conditions are part of the definition of a reported coefficient; see <lift> for a worked scale and a pressure/balance experiment.

= Dynamic pressure
{parent=Lift coefficient}
{title2=$q=\rho U^2/2$}

<Dynamic pressure> is the bulk <kinetic energy> per unit <volume> associated with <fluid> motion at <speed> $U$ in a specified <reference frame>:
$$
q=\frac12\rho U^2,\qquad
\frac{\tfrac12mU^2}{V}=\frac12\rho U^2,\qquad
\mathrm{J\,m^{-3}}=\mathrm{Pa}.
$$
Here $\rho=m/V$ is <mass density>. The word "pressure" identifies its units and its use as a <pressure> scale; $q$ is not an extra isotropic <stress> to add to the local <static pressure> in every surface <traction>. A stationary <fluid> can have large <static pressure> with $q=0$. Conversely, knowing $U$ and $\rho$ fixes $q$ without fixing <static pressure>. The definition remains useful in <viscous fluid flow> and <compressible flow>, even when the simplest <Bernoulli equation> is inapplicable. <Dynamic Pressure by NASA Glenn Research Center>{ref}

For stipulated <mass density> $\rho_\infty=1.20\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$ and reference <speed> $U_\infty=20\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, $q_\infty=240\,\mathrm{Pa}$. At $40\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ with the same <mass density>, $q_\infty=960\,\mathrm{Pa}$. This quadratic scaling makes <dynamic pressure> a useful normalization for the <lift coefficient>, <drag coefficient> and <pressure coefficient>; it predicts four times a particular <force> only if its corresponding coefficient remains unchanged.

\Image[pitot-static-pressure-difference.png]
{title=A <Pitot-static tube> compares <stagnation pressure> with <static pressure>}
{description=Original schematic with separate <pressure> passages and a differential readout. The right panel uses stipulated values, not measurements: ideal stopping in <incompressible flow> at unchanged height raises <static pressure> by 240 Pa as the <kinetic energy> per unit <volume> falls by 240 Pa. The diagram is not a probe design or a claim that real <static pressure> ports are error free.}

The connection with <stagnation pressure> requires hypotheses. For <steady flow>, constant <mass density>, <inviscid flow>, and <gravity> as the only <body force>, the <Bernoulli equation> gives, along one <streamline>,
$$
p+\frac12\rho U^2+\rho gz=B.
$$
Here $p$ is <static pressure>, $g$ is <gravitational acceleration>, and $z$ is height. Bring that flow to rest without losses at the same height and the limiting <static pressure> is the <stagnation pressure> $p_0$:
$$
p_0=p+q,\qquad p_0-p=\frac12\rho U^2.
$$
There must be no intervening pump supplying <work> or other energy input. Constancy along one <streamline> does not assert equal $B$ on all <streamlines>; <irrotational flow> in a connected region supplies that additional conclusion. Changing height requires retaining $\rho gz$. In <compressible flow> the stopping <pressure> difference generally differs from $\rho U^2/2$, and <viscous dissipation> can reduce recovered <stagnation pressure>. <Bernoulli’s Equation by NASA Glenn Research Center>{ref}

A <Pitot-static tube> turns this relation into a measurement: its upstream-facing opening senses <stagnation pressure>, while separate side openings sample <static pressure>. Connect these to the positive and negative ports of a differential <pressure transducer> or <manometer>. Under the hypotheses above, the measured difference $\Delta p$ gives
$$
U=\sqrt{\frac{2\Delta p}{\rho}}.
$$
Thus the stipulated $240\,\mathrm{Pa}$ and $1.20\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$ imply $20\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, not an independently measured <speed>. Align the probe with the incoming flow and keep its passages clear: blockage and pinched tubing invalidate the comparison. At very low <speed>, the difference can be smaller than instrument error; at high <Mach number>, <compressibility> and possibly a <shock wave> require a different reduction. <Pitot – Static Tube – Speedometer by NASA Glenn Research Center>{ref}

A useful control is <differential-pressure calibration>. For a digital <manometer>, zeroing establishes the reading at equal port <pressures>; it does not establish the gain away from zero. The manufacturer's <Series 475 Handheld Digital Manometer: Specifications - Installation and Operating Instructions by Dwyer Instruments, Inc.> directs the user to vent both ports to the atmosphere before zeroing. Its stated accuracy is a fraction of full scale, not of the reading, and depends on instrument <temperature>. Select a suitable range and record the instrument variant and units; the manual covers several ranges.<Series 475 Handheld Digital Manometer: Specifications - Installation and Operating Instructions by Dwyer Instruments, Inc.>[p. 1: Specifications, Model Chart and ZERO instructions]{ref}

An independent physical check can use a liquid-column <manometer>: apply a stable <pressure> difference to both instruments and compare the digital reading with the <hydrostatic pressure> inferred from the vertical level difference. For equal-height connections carrying a gas of <mass density> $\rho_g$ above a manometric liquid of <mass density> $\rho_m$,
$$
|\Delta p|=(\rho_m-\rho_g)g|h|.
$$
This follows by balancing <hydrostatic pressure> down and up the two connected columns. Use vertical height, even if a tube is inclined. Known <mass densities>, measured height and <gravitational acceleration> set the comparison scale. Check several differences across the working range and reverse the port connections to check the sign. This is a proposed independent check, not a claim that the cited teaching experiment performed a full instrument calibration. Recheck zero after reconnecting the sealed tubing; a zero adjustment alone cannot detect a blocked passage or an incorrect scale.

= Pressure coefficient
{parent=Lift (force)}
{title2=$C_p=(p-p_\infty)/q_\infty$}

The <pressure coefficient> compares a local <static pressure> with a specified reference <static pressure>, using a fixed reference <dynamic pressure>:
$$
C_p(\mathbf x)=\frac{p(\mathbf x)-p_\infty}{q_\infty},\qquad
q_\infty=\frac12\rho_\infty U_\infty^2>0.
$$
The reference <mass density>, <speed>, <pressure> and location must accompany a reported <pressure coefficient>. Keep the same $q_\infty$ throughout a surface distribution; replacing it by local $\rho U^2/2$ gives a different quantity and fails at a <stagnation point>. The definition itself requires neither <Bernoulli's principle> nor <inviscid flow>, so measured <pressure coefficients> remain useful when those idealizations fail.

Positive $C_p$ means $p>p_\infty$; zero means equality; negative $C_p$ means $p<p_\infty$. This sign does not specify the sign of <absolute pressure>. For stipulated $p_\infty=101325\,\mathrm{Pa}$ absolute and $q_\infty=240\,\mathrm{Pa}$, $C_p=-3$ gives $p=100605\,\mathrm{Pa}$ absolute: a $720\,\mathrm{Pa}$ deficit, not a negative <absolute pressure>. If $p_\infty$ equals <atmospheric pressure>, the numerator is <gauge pressure>; a <wind tunnel>'s reference <static pressure> need not equal room <atmospheric pressure>. These are reference choices, not different physical <pressures>.

Under the same-height, constant-<mass density> $\rho=\rho_\infty$, <steady flow> and <inviscid flow> hypotheses of the <Bernoulli equation>, and with the same <Bernoulli invariant> as the reference flow,
$$
p+\frac12\rho U^2=p_\infty+\frac12\rho U_\infty^2,
\qquad C_p=1-\left(\frac{U}{U_\infty}\right)^2.
$$
The first equation immediately gives the second after subtracting $p_\infty$ and dividing by $q_\infty$. Hence a lossless <stagnation point> has $C_p=1$, $U=U_\infty$ gives $C_p=0$, and $U=2U_\infty$ gives $C_p=-3$. For a real stationary wall satisfying the <no-slip boundary condition>, the <velocity> is zero right at the wall; inserting that wall <velocity> in an <inviscid flow> formula would wrongly predict $C_p=1$ everywhere. A thin <boundary layer> approximation instead relates the wall <pressure> to the outer-flow <pressure> and uses the outer <velocity> where the <Bernoulli equation> applies.

\Image[cylinder-pressure-taps-ideal-coefficient.png]
{title=Ideal cylinder <pressure coefficients>, <pressure tapping> and cancellation of pressure <drag>}
{description=Original <potential flow around a circular cylinder> with zero <circulation>, not experimental data or a fit. <Angle> is measured counterclockwise from the downstream direction. Hollow circles mark sampling locations; arrows show only the reference-subtracted <pressure> <force> per unit <area>. A separate <pressure tapping> measures <static pressure>, rather than <stagnation pressure>. The front and rear ideal <pressure> <forces> cancel, so this model predicts zero <drag>.}

For <potential flow around a circular cylinder> of radius $a$ with zero <circulation>, the surface tangential <velocity> is $u_\theta=-2U_\infty\sin\theta$. Thus the ideal <pressure coefficient> is
$$
C_p(\theta)=1-4\sin^2\theta.
$$
With $\theta$ measured from downstream, the front is at $\pi$ and the rear at zero. Both ideal <stagnation points> have $C_p=1$, while the sides at $\pi/2$ and $3\pi/2$ have $C_p=-3$. This exact model is a useful baseline for an experiment; its complete rearward <pressure> recovery is not a prediction for a separated real <fluid wake>.

To obtain a <force>, integrate the local normal contributions with their directions. Let $\mathbf n$ be the unit <normal vector> from the body into the <fluid> and $\Sigma$ its closed wetted surface:
$$
\mathbf F_p=-\int_\Sigma p\mathbf n\,dA
=-q_\infty\int_\Sigma C_p\mathbf n\,dA,
\qquad \int_\Sigma\mathbf n\,dA=\mathbf0.
$$
The second equality removes a spatially constant reference <pressure> over the closed surface; it does not remove a nonuniform <hydrostatic pressure> or justify dropping the reference term on an isolated open patch. A negative $C_p$ contributes outwards in this reference-subtracted calculation, even though a positive <absolute pressure> still pushes inwards locally. Project $\mathbf F_p$ onto the chosen <lift> and <drag> directions. The full <fluid> <force> also includes $\int_\Sigma\boldsymbol\sigma_{\rm visc}\mathbf n\,dA$ from the <viscous stress tensor>, including tangential <shear stress>; <pressure tapping> does not measure that contribution.

For the two-dimensional cylinder, <force> per unit span follows from $dA=a\,d\theta$ per unit span and $\mathbf n=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)$:
$$
(D'_p,L'_p)=-q_\infty a\int_0^{2\pi}C_p(\theta)(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\,d\theta.
$$
Substituting $C_p=1-4\sin^2\theta$ gives $(0,0)$: opposite points have identical <pressure coefficients> and opposite <normal vectors>. Zero <drag> despite a strongly nonuniform <pressure> is the instructive limitation associated with <D'Alembert's paradox>. Real <viscosity>, <boundary layers> and <flow separation> can prevent rearward <pressure> recovery and produce <pressure drag>. Thus the measured curve can test the model and estimate a <pressure> contribution without proving that total <drag> vanishes.

A motivating measurement is Virginia Tech's <AOE 3054 laboratory manual/Experiment 3 - Flow Past a Circular Cylinder>. Its open-jet <wind tunnel> holds a rotatable $140\,\mathrm{mm}$ cylinder with end plates and 36 one-millimetre <pressure tappings> at ten-degree intervals. A reference <Pitot-static tube> supplies $p_{0\infty}$ and $p_\infty$ to one digital <manometer>; a second connects a surface tap to its positive port and reference <static pressure> to its negative port. Record the simultaneous readings:
$$
\Delta p_{\rm ref}=p_{0\infty}-p_\infty,\qquad
\Delta p_i=p_i-p_\infty,\qquad
C_{p,i}=\frac{\Delta p_i}{\Delta p_{\rm ref}}.
$$
The last equality uses the low-speed stopping relation, not an additional <speed> measurement. The manual requires sealed tubing and zero checks with the tunnel off. It also provides a water-column <manometer>: read each column's initial level, and subtract the independently measured reference-to-atmosphere <pressure> from the tap-to-atmosphere readings. Record tap positions after rotation. Compare the two instruments and check spanwise uniformity; end plates do not guarantee two-dimensional flow. <AOE 3054 laboratory manual/Experiment 3 - Flow Past a Circular Cylinder>{ref}

The inferential distinction matters: the <manometers> directly measure <pressure> differences, the ratio estimates <pressure coefficients>, and a <surface integral> estimates the pressure part of the <force>. Measure room <absolute pressure> and <temperature> if converting the reference difference to <speed> through the <ideal gas law> $\rho=p_{\rm abs}/(RT)$; use <thermodynamic temperature> in that equation. For <force> reconstruction, cover the complete contour and use <numerical integration> with the tap geometry. Finite tap spacing, unknown end effects, probe disturbance and fluctuations limit that reconstruction; tubes and instruments also average or delay changing <pressures>. Retain the signed raw readings, repeated readings and reference readings so that these limits can be assessed. A small reference difference magnifies any zero error in the ratio, rather than producing a reliably large $C_p$.

= Pressure tapping
{parent=Pressure coefficient}

A small flush opening communicating the local surface <static pressure> through tubing to a <manometer> or <pressure transducer>. An upstream-facing <Pitot tube> instead brings incident flow to rest to measure <stagnation pressure>.

= Static pressure
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}
{title2=$p$}

The local <fluid pressure>, distinguished from <dynamic pressure> and <stagnation pressure>. "Static" does not require the <fluid> to be stationary.

= Absolute pressure
{parent=Static pressure}
{title2=$p_{\rm abs}$}

<Pressure> referred to an ideal <vacuum>, rather than to <atmospheric pressure> or another chosen reference. <Gauge pressure> instead subtracts <atmospheric pressure>.

= Stagnation pressure
{parent=Static pressure}
{wiki}
{title2=$p_0$}

= Total pressure
{synonym}

The <pressure> obtained by bringing a flow to rest without losses; in <compressible flow> the usual definition specifies an <isentropic process> for stopping. For the constant-<mass density>, same-height <Bernoulli equation> discussed under <dynamic pressure>, $p_0=p+q$.

= Pitot tube
{c}
{parent=Stagnation pressure}
{wiki}

An upstream-facing probe that senses <stagnation pressure>. Determining <speed> also requires a separate <static pressure> and <mass density>.

= Pitot-static tube
{c}
{parent=Pitot tube}

A combined <Pitot tube> and <static pressure> probe with separate passages for the two <pressures>.

= Manometer
{parent=Fluid mechanics}

An instrument for measuring <pressure> relative to another <pressure>. A liquid-column <manometer> uses <hydrostatic pressure>; a digital <manometer> uses a <pressure transducer>.

= Pressure transducer
{parent=Fluid mechanics}

An instrument converting <pressure> or a <pressure> difference into a measurable signal. Its zero and scale must be checked separately, as illustrated by <differential-pressure calibration>.

= Differential-pressure calibration
{parent=Pressure transducer}

Checking the zero and scale of a differential <pressure transducer> against equal port <pressures> and independently known <pressure> differences. The <dynamic pressure> measurement discussion gives a liquid-column check.

= D'Alembert's paradox
{c}
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

Ideal <steady flow> with <incompressible flow>, <inviscid flow> and <irrotational flow> can predict zero <drag> on a body that has nonzero <drag> in a real <fluid>. The zero-<circulation> cylinder <pressure coefficient> distribution gives an explicit cancellation example; <viscosity> and <flow separation> are absent from that model.

= Wind tunnel
{parent=Fluid mechanics}

An apparatus that produces controlled <fluid flow> past a model for measuring quantities such as <pressure>, <lift> and <drag>.

= Airfoil
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

= Aerofoil
{synonym}

An <airfoil> (British spelling: <aerofoil>) is the cross-sectional profile of a lifting surface, such as a wing or blade. Its chord is the straight segment from leading edge to trailing edge, with length $c$; its mean camber line runs midway between its upper and lower surfaces, and its thickness measures their separation. Camber is the departure of that mean line from the chord. The <angle of attack> describes the profile's orientation relative to the incoming flow. These independent choices let a designer alter the distribution of <pressure>, <lift> and <drag>, rather than simply enlarging the surface. https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/wing-geometry/[NASA Glenn, Wing Geometry, Airfoil Geometry subsection]{ref}

The distinction between an <airfoil> and a finite wing matters. A two-dimensional section model assumes no variation along span and predicts <lift> per unit span $L'$. A wing of span $b$ has a spanwise distribution $L'(z)$ and total <lift> $L=\int_{-b/2}^{b/2}L'(z)\,dz$. Its tip flow and trailing <vortices> generate a downward induced <velocity>, called downwash, which changes the local flow seen by the sections. Thus one cannot generally multiply the isolated two-dimensional $L'$ by $b$. The wing aspect ratio $b^2/S$ measures its span relative to its reference area and helps characterize these three-dimensional effects. https://pressbooks.lib.vt.edu/aerodynamics/chapter/chapter-3-additional-aerodynamic-tools/[James F. Marchman III, Aerodynamics and Aircraft Performance, chapter 3.4: finite wings]{ref}

For a thin section with small camber slopes and small <angle of attack>, <thin airfoil theory> predicts
$$
c_l\simeq2\pi(\alpha-\alpha_{L=0}),\qquad
\frac{dc_l}{d\alpha}\simeq2\pi,
$$
where both <angles> are in <radians> and $\alpha_{L=0}$ is the zero-lift <angle of attack>. The model assumes two-dimensional, <steady flow>, <incompressible flow>, <inviscid flow>, an attached outer flow, and smooth departure at a sharp trailing edge (the Kutta condition). This last condition selects the <circulation> used by the <Kutta–Joukowski theorem>; with counterclockwise <circulation> $\Gamma$ positive and $\mathbf U_\infty=U_\infty\mathbf e_x$, that theorem gives $L'=-\rho_\infty U_\infty\Gamma$ for positive lift towards $+\mathbf e_y$. The beauty is that a detailed <velocity field> collapses to a simple force law, while the geometry enters through $\alpha_{L=0}$. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/16-01-unified-engineering-i-ii-iii-iv-fall-2005-spring-2006/d721171c42af48a056aaccec784a6d10_f03_0304.pdf[MIT Unified Engineering, F3 lecture notes, pp. 2–4: camber and lift slope]{ref} https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/16-01-unified-engineering-i-ii-iii-iv-fall-2005-spring-2006/cc3f20c40c9451bde6a595fdd96a7e9e_f02_mud_0304.pdf[MIT Unified Engineering, F2 questions 7 and 9: symmetric sections and Kutta condition]{ref}

A symmetric <airfoil> has $\alpha_{L=0}=0$ in this model: at $\alpha=4\,{}^\circ=\pi/45$ the predicted <lift coefficient> is $c_l\simeq0.439$, and reversing $\alpha$ reverses <lift>. A stipulated cambered section with $\alpha_{L=0}=-2\,{}^\circ$ instead predicts $c_l\simeq0.219$ at zero <angle of attack>. These are ideal calculations, not measurements or guarantees for a particular profile. Air parcels on the upper and lower surfaces have no obligation to arrive together at the trailing edge; the <pressure> and <velocity field> must satisfy the flow problem. A symmetric lifting section already makes an unequal-path-length explanation inadequate. https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/bernoulli-and-newton/[NASA Glenn, Bernoulli and Newton: the equal-transit explanation is incorrect]{ref}

Real <airfoils> have a viscous <boundary layer>, even when <inviscid flow> approximates the outer motion. At sufficiently demanding incidence, <flow separation> can become extensive and the <lift coefficient> departs from its approximately linear law, reaches a maximum and may fall: this is <aerodynamic stall>. <Lift> does not generally vanish at <aerodynamic stall>. The onset depends on profile, <Reynolds number>, surface condition and motion history; $2\pi(\alpha-\alpha_{L=0})$ predicts neither a universal stall angle nor post-stall behavior. The pressure/balance experiment in <lift> makes this limit observable by measuring changes in the surface <pressure> distribution alongside the total <force>. https://pressbooks.lib.vt.edu/aerodynamics/chapter/chapter-3-additional-aerodynamic-tools/[James F. Marchman, chapter 3.3: separation invalidates thin-airfoil assumptions]{ref}

= Angle of attack
{parent=Airfoil}
{title2=$\alpha$}

The signed <angle> between a lifting surface's chord and its incoming relative flow. Positive incidence in the planar convention used in <airfoil> raises the leading edge relative to the trailing edge in a horizontal left-to-right flow. It is distinct from orientation relative to the ground; an induced <velocity> can change the local effective incidence.

= Thin airfoil theory
{parent=Airfoil}
{title2=$c_l\simeq2\pi(\alpha-\alpha_{L=0})$}

A small-disturbance, two-dimensional <potential flow> model for a thin <airfoil>, using surface tangency and smooth flow departure at its trailing edge to determine <circulation>. Its <lift coefficient> slope is $2\pi$ per <radian>, with camber shifting the zero-lift <angle of attack>. See <airfoil> for assumptions, examples and the <aerodynamic stall> limit.

= Aerodynamic stall
{parent=Airfoil}

A loss of the approximately attached-flow relation between <angle of attack> and <lift coefficient>, associated with substantial <flow separation>. A stalled <airfoil> can still produce <lift>; its behavior must be measured or modeled beyond <thin airfoil theory>.

= Uniform flow
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{title2=$\mathbf u(\mathbf x)=\mathbf U$}

A uniform flow has the same <velocity> at every position. A constant velocity field has zero <divergence> and zero <vorticity>; a <velocity potential> is $\phi(\mathbf x)=\mathbf U\cdot\mathbf x$. It describes the remote ambient flow in idealized obstacle and <line vortex> problems.

= Volume viscosity
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{title2=$\zeta_B$}
{wiki}

= Bulk viscosity
{synonym}

Bulk viscosity is the dissipative response to a fluid's volume expansion or compression, distinct from the shear response. For a Newtonian isotropic fluid its isotropic dissipative stress includes $\zeta_B(\nabla\cdot\mathbf v)I$, and nonnegative $\zeta_B$ gives nonnegative compression-related dissipation. A condensate's relative-density-flow bulk coefficient must be identified from its particular hydrodynamic equation; it is not automatically numerically identical to a dimensional Newtonian viscosity.

= Hydraulic resistance
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{title2=$R=\Delta p/Q$}

<Hydraulic resistance> relates the <pressure> drop across a resistive fluid element to its <volumetric flow rate>. In a linear model, $\Delta p=RQ$ with $R>0$, so dissipated mechanical power is $\Delta p\,Q=RQ^2$. A peripheral circulatory bed can be represented by such a resistance when its detailed dynamics are not retained. This terminal <pressure>-to-flow resistance differs from the area-dependent friction rate multiplying <velocity> in a distributed tube momentum equation.

= Physiological fluid dynamics
{parent=Fluid mechanics}

Physiological fluid dynamics studies the motion of fluids in biological systems, including blood flow in elastic arteries, flow through deformable vessels, and liquid layers lining airways. Wall compliance couples <pressure> to geometry, so wave propagation and mechanical feedback are as important as <viscosity>.

= Lubrication model of a compliant translating plug
{parent=Physiological fluid dynamics}
{title2=$p_x=-6\mu U/h^2+12\mu Q/h^3$}

For a freely translating compliant body separated from a cylindrical wall by a thin <lubrication> gap $h$, the body frame has wall speed $-U$ and body surface speed zero. The local axial flow is $w=-U(1-y/h)+p_xy(y-h)/(2\mu)$, so its flux per circumference is $-Uh/2-p_xh^3/(12\mu)=-Q$. Rearranging proves the displayed pressure gradient. A linear compliance $p=p_0+\alpha(r_0-r)$ couples this flow to deformation. Specified end pressures and zero net axial body force close the problem.

= Force-free pressure drop of an annular lubrication plug
{parent=Lubrication model of a compliant translating plug}
{title2=$\Delta p=\frac{2\mu}{a}\int(4U/h-6Q/h^2)\,dx$}

The pressure force on a plug-and-film control volume is $\pi a^2\Delta p$. Its opposing wall traction is $2\pi a\mu\int w_y(0)\,dx$, where $w_y(0)=4U/h-6Q/h^2$. Equating them for a freely moving body proves the displayed formula. Writing $s=2Q/U$, $h=sH$ and $x=\sqrt{s/\kappa}X$ converts it into $H(-\widetilde L)-H(\widetilde L)=C\lambda\int[4/(3H)-1/H^2]dX$, with $C=s/a$ and $\lambda=6\mu U/(\alpha\sqrt\kappa s^{5/2})$.

= Small-speed compliant-plug lubrication asymptotics
{parent=Force-free pressure drop of an annular lubrication plug}
{title2=$b^{-2}=\tfrac23(1-C)+O(C^2),\quad\Delta p\sim8\pi\sqrt{2/3}\mu U/(a\sqrt{\kappa s})$}

For $H_X+\lambda(H^{-2}-H^{-3})=X$, small $\lambda$ gives $H_0=(b^2+X^2)/2$. In the large-domain limit its inverse-power integrals are $I_1=2\pi/b$, $I_2=2\pi/b^3$ and $I_3=3\pi/b^5$. The force-free condition is $I_2-I_3=C(4I_1/3-I_2)$, or $2b^2-3=C(8b^4/3-2b^2)$. Expanding the root near $b^{-2}=2/3$ proves the displayed minus sign and leading pressure drop. The absolute downstream pressure relative to the elastic reference pressure fixes $s$ and therefore closes the speed-pressure relation.

= Annular inviscid-core displacement
{parent=Physiological fluid dynamics}
{title2=$u_1=A u_0'/(R+y),\quad v_1=-A_xu_0/(R+y)$}

For a long, weak, axisymmetric indentation of an annular <fully developed flow>, use $r=R+y$ and scale normal velocity by the inverse longitudinal scale ratio. The leading <Euler equations for an inviscid fluid> give zero longitudinal pressure gradient and $u_0u_{1x}+v_1u_0'=0$. Together with $u_{1x}+(rv_1)_y/r=0$, this yields $\psi_1=A u_0$, hence the displayed displacement fields. Thin <viscous boundary layers> enforce tangential wall conditions separately.

= Forced annular displacement equation
{parent=Annular inviscid-core displacement}
{title2=$\sigma A_{xxx}-\beta\alpha_1A_t-\alpha_2AA_x=\beta\gamma_0F_t+\gamma_0^2FF_x+(\gamma_0^2/R)(AF)_x$}

A distinguished annular indentation limit has $\lambda^{-2}=O(\epsilon)$, $\lambda St=O(\epsilon)$ and $Re\gg\lambda\epsilon^{-3}$. Define $\beta=\lambda St/\epsilon$, $\sigma=(\lambda^2\epsilon)^{-1}\int_0^1u_0^2/(R+y)\,dy$, $\alpha_1=\gamma_0/R+\gamma_1/(R+1)$ and $\alpha_2=\gamma_0^2/R^2-\gamma_1^2/(R+1)^2$. Radial inertia supplies the pressure difference $\sigma A_{xx}$ between the walls. The axial momentum limits and the moving-wall condition supply its derivative, giving the displayed forced <KdV equation>. This reduction assumes the <viscous boundary layers> remain thin and their displaced flux is smaller than $O(\epsilon^2)$.

= Opposite propagation directions in the annular displacement equation
{parent=Forced annular displacement equation}
{title2=$\omega=\sigma k^3/(\beta\alpha_1),\quad A=-3\beta\alpha_1c\,\mathrm{sech}^2(k_s\xi)/\alpha_2$}

With no forcing, the linear <dispersion relation> has positive <phase velocity> $\sigma k^2/(\beta\alpha_1)$ and <group velocity> three times this. A decaying permanent wave with $\xi=x+ct$ instead obeys $\sigma(A')^2=A^2(\beta\alpha_1c+\alpha_2A/3)$. For positive coefficients a nonzero homoclinic wave requires $c>0$ and is negative, travelling upstream. Its inverse width is $k_s=\frac12\sqrt{\beta\alpha_1c/\sigma}$. The opposite directions reflect the nonlinear amplitude-dependent propagation speed.

= Peristaltic pumping
{parent=Physiological fluid dynamics}

<Peristaltic pumping> transports fluid by a travelling deformation or travelling external <pressure> acting on a tube. With wave coordinate $X=x-ct$, <mass conservation> makes the relative flux $Q=\alpha(u-c)$ constant. The laboratory flux is $\alpha u=c\alpha+Q$, so a negative relative flux does not rule out forward mean pumping.

= Weak peristaltic pumping of a collapsible tube
{parent=Peristaltic pumping}
{title2=$Q_2=\frac{c(n+1)}{2\alpha_0}\frac{P_e^2}{\widetilde P_0'^2+(R_0c/(k\alpha_0))^2}$}

For negligible inertia, periodic forcing $p_e=\epsilon P_e\sin kX$, resistance $R\propto\alpha^{-n}$ and prescribed mean area $\alpha_0$, write $Q=-c\alpha_0+\epsilon^2Q_2+\cdots$. First-order area obeys $\widetilde P'_0\alpha_1'+R_0c\alpha_1/\alpha_0=-kP_e\cos kX$. Its mean square is $P_e^2/[2(\widetilde P_0'^2+(R_0c/(k\alpha_0))^2)]$. Averaging the second-order friction balance gives $Q_2=c(n+1)\langle\alpha_1^2\rangle/\alpha_0$, proving the displayed forward pumping rate. Periodicity entails zero mean <pressure> drop; the mean-area convention fixes the expansion's otherwise free constant.

= Collapsible tube flow
{parent=Physiological fluid dynamics}

A one-dimensional collapsible tube model couples area $\alpha$, mean axial <velocity> $u$ and internal <pressure> $p$: $\alpha_t+(\alpha u)_x=0$ and $u_t+uu_x=-p_x-R(\alpha)u$. A wall relation specifies internal minus external <pressure> as a function of area. Decreasing area generally increases the friction coefficient $R$, while positive wall stiffness gives real small-amplitude <wave speed>.

= Choking by wall thickness in an elastic tube
{parent=Collapsible tube flow}
{title2=$h_c/R=1+\lambda/4-5\lambda^{1/5}/4$}

For steady uniform-density inviscid flow with pressure law $p=p_0+k(r-R)$ and a prescribed thickness $h$, mass conservation and the <Bernoulli equation> give $h/R=1-s^{-1/2}+\lambda(1-s^2)/4$, where $s=u/V$ and $\lambda=2\rho V^2/(kR)$. The function has a unique maximum at $s=\lambda^{-2/5}$, producing the displayed choking bound. Below it there are two positive roots; continuity from the upstream state selects the lower-speed root for $\lambda<1$ and higher-speed root for $\lambda>1$. At $\lambda=1$, the maximum is zero and any positive prescribed thickness is incompatible with this model.

= Subcritical tube flow
{parent=Collapsible tube flow}
{title2=$0<u<c$}

A positive <collapsible tube flow> is subcritical when its <characteristic speeds> $u+c$ and $u-c$ have opposite signs. A downstream boundary can then send information upstream. For a quadratic pressure-area law and fixed dimensionless flux $q$, this corresponds to $A/A_0>\sqrt q$.

= Supercritical tube flow
{parent=Collapsible tube flow}
{title2=$u>c$}

A positive <collapsible tube flow> is supercritical when both <characteristic speeds> $u\pm c$ are positive relative to the wall. Information then propagates downstream on both branches. For a quadratic pressure-area law and fixed dimensionless flux $q$, the condition is $A/A_0<\sqrt q$.

= Elastic jump in a quadratic pressure-area tube
{parent=Collapsible tube flow}
{title2=$q^2/\alpha+\alpha^3/3=\mathrm{constant}$}

An elastic jump is a short transition from a fast, narrow supercritical <collapsible tube flow> to a slower, wider subcritical flow. Neglecting body force and distributed friction across the jump conserves flux and the momentum flux shown above. Distinct upstream and downstream areas therefore obey $\alpha_2^3+\alpha_1\alpha_2^2+\alpha_1^2\alpha_2=3q^2/\alpha_1$. The flux function has its unique minimum at $\alpha=\sqrt q$, so an upstream state below this value has one conjugate state above it. The jump dissipates mechanical energy; it does not conserve a Bernoulli constant.

= Spatial attraction of a uniform downhill tube flow
{parent=Collapsible tube flow}
{title2=$(\alpha-q^2/\alpha^3)\alpha_x=(g\sin\theta/c_0^2)[1-qR(\alpha)/\beta]$}

For $p=P_0+\rho c_0^2\alpha^2/2$ and constant flux $c_0A_0q$, a downhill <collapsible tube flow> obeys the displayed steady equation. Uniform area satisfies $qR(\alpha_1)=\beta$. Since $R'<0$, linearizing about this root gives downstream exponential decay precisely when $\alpha_1^2<q$. This is spatial stability of a steady inlet-value problem, not a guarantee of temporal stability of the driven flow. The <wave speed> is $c_0\alpha$, so the stable spatial branch is supercritical.

= Friction-induced instability of a collapsible-tube flow
{parent=Collapsible tube flow}
{title2=$\nu^2+iR_0\nu-k^2c_0^2-inkR_0U=0$}

Let $R(\alpha)\propto\alpha^{-n}$, with $n>0$, and use disturbances $e^{i(kx-\omega t)}$ about uniform <velocity> $U$. The intrinsic <frequency> is $\nu=\omega-kU$. Linearized <mass conservation> and the decrease in friction as area grows give the displayed <dispersion relation>. For nonzero $k$ and positive flow, one branch grows exactly when $nU>c_0$. At $nU=c_0$, one branch is neutral, $\nu=kc_0$, while the other remains damped, $\nu=-kc_0-iR_0$. The maintained external-<pressure gradient> supplies the energy; positive friction alone does not imply linear stability of a driven flow.

= Pressure-area law of a collapsible tube
{parent=Collapsible tube flow}
{title2=$p-p_e=\widetilde P(\alpha),\quad c^2=\alpha\widetilde P'(\alpha)$}

The <pressure-area law of a collapsible tube> describes transverse mechanical equilibrium of a deformable tube wall. On a stable branch $\widetilde P'(\alpha)>0$. Linearizing mass and momentum conservation about a stationary uniform tube gives the displayed <wave speed>. External <pressure> is prescribed separately and is not part of the elastic wall stiffness.

= Tube wave speed
{parent=Pressure-area law of a collapsible tube}
{title2=$c^2=(A/\rho)\,dp/dA$}

Linearizing area conservation and the axial momentum equation of a <collapsible tube flow> gives waves travelling relative to the fluid at speeds $\pm c$, where the displayed relation uses the area derivative at fixed external pressure. For $p=P_0+\rho c_0^2(A/A_0)^2/2$, it gives $c=c_0A/A_0$.

= Arterial pressure wave
{parent=Physiological fluid dynamics}

A small <pressure> disturbance in an elastic artery propagates through the coupled inertia of blood and compliance of the wall. If $\mathcal A$ is the undisturbed area, $\rho$ the <mass density> and $c$ the <wave speed>, the linearized equations give $c^2=\mathcal A/\rho\,dp/d\mathcal A$. A travelling <pressure> amplitude $p_+$ carries flow amplitude $\mathcal A p_+/(\rho c)$; the oppositely travelling wave has the opposite flow sign.

= Bifurcation reflection of an arterial pressure wave
{parent=Arterial pressure wave}
{title2=$r=(Y_1-Y_d)/(Y_1+Y_d)$}

At a lossless vascular junction, <pressure> continuity and <volumetric flow rate> balance give $1+r=t$ and $Y_1(1-r)=Y_dt$, where $Y_d$ is the sum of daughter <characteristic admittances of an arterial wave>. Eliminating transmission amplitude $t$ gives the displayed <reflection coefficient>. A decrease in total area yields positive reflection if parent and daughter wave speeds are equal; differing stiffnesses require comparison of the admittances rather than areas alone.

= Pressure-flow phase lag from one arterial reflection
{parent=Bifurcation reflection of an arterial pressure wave}
{title2=$\omega\Delta t=\arg[1-r^2+2ir\sin\vartheta]$}

For incident and reflected harmonic <arterial pressure waves> with real $0<r<1$, the local <pressure> and flow phasors are proportional to $1+re^{-i\vartheta}$ and $1-re^{-i\vartheta}$. Their maximum-time difference is the displayed phase lag, on the branch $0<\vartheta<\pi$. Here $\vartheta=2\omega d/c$ is the round-trip phase to the reflector. Its derivative has the sign of $\cos\vartheta$. Raising the <wave speed> reduces the lag when $0<\vartheta<\pi/2$, but increases it when $\pi/2<\vartheta<\pi$. This conditional harmonic model does not establish an age trend for arbitrary vascular geometry or measurement stations beyond the sole reflector.

= Reflection of an arterial pressure wave
{parent=Arterial pressure wave}
{title2=$p_-/p_+=(RY-1)/(RY+1)$}

= Arterial wave reflection
{synonym}

A <hydraulic resistance> $R$ imposes $q=p/R$. Combining this with forward and backward <arterial pressure waves>, $p=p_++p_-$ and $q=Y(p_+-p_-)$, gives the displayed <reflection coefficient>. A matched load $RY=1$ has no reflected <pressure> wave, while a closed end has <reflection coefficient> one. At a network junction the combined downstream input admittance replaces $1/R$.

= Wave transfer matrix of an arterial segment
{parent=Arterial pressure wave}
{title2=$\begin{pmatrix}p_u\\q_u\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\beta&i\sin\beta/Y\\iY\sin\beta&\cos\beta\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}p_d\\q_d\end{pmatrix}$}

= Wave transfer matrices of an arterial segment
{synonym}

For time dependence $e^{i\omega t}$ and a segment of length $\ell$, let $\beta=\omega\ell/c$. Adding the forward and backward <arterial pressure waves> gives the displayed upstream-to-downstream matrix. The flow orientation is downstream at both ends. At a junction, <pressure> is continuous and incoming <volumetric flow rates> balance outgoing ones.

= Wave transmission through a resistively loaded arterial loop
{parent=Wave transfer matrix of an arterial segment}

Consider two identical two-segment paths from an inlet to a distal junction, with equal resistive beds at the two intermediate junctions and the distal junction. The segment transfer matrix and junction flow balances determine all <pressures>. If one inlet branch is removed and the beds are weakly loading the waves, the remaining segments form a three-segment chain with an effectively closed far end. The altered reflection pattern can raise peripheral oscillatory flow amplitudes even though a branch was lost; the incident wave amplitude, rather than total inlet <pressure> or flow, is the prescribed forcing.

= Characteristic admittance of an arterial wave
{parent=Arterial pressure wave}
{title2=$Y=\mathcal A/(\rho c)$}

= Characteristic admittances of an arterial wave
{synonym}

The <characteristic admittance of an arterial wave> relates the <volumetric flow rate> of a travelling <arterial pressure wave> to its <pressure>. For forward and backward amplitudes, $p=p_++p_-$ and $q=Y(p_+-p_-)$. It is the reciprocal of characteristic <pressure>-to-flow impedance, and should not be confused with the reciprocal <hydraulic resistance> of a steadily resistive segment.

= Fluid
{parent=Fluid mechanics}

A <fluid> is a material that flows under sustained <shear stress> and cannot maintain a static <shear stress> in its fluid state. <Liquids> and <gases> are the usual examples. A <Newtonian fluid> has <deviatoric stress> proportional to its <rate-of-strain tensor>, whereas more general fluid <constitutive equations> can be nonlinear or have memory. In a <porous medium>, the <fluid> occupies connected voids within a load-bearing <solid> framework. The important distinction is the response to a continuing tangential <force>: an ordinary elastic <solid> can settle into a fixed <deformation>, while an ordinary <fluid> continues to deform. A <fluid> can nevertheless be at rest when no unbalanced <shear stress> drives it. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/12-800-fluid-dynamics-of-the-atmosphere-and-ocean-fall-2004/7bb9e945405534b36776cbe404a8f839_lecture3.pdf[MIT, Fluid Dynamics of the Atmosphere and Ocean, lecture 3, pp. 4–5]{ref}

For a worked example, place a <Newtonian fluid> between parallel plates separated by $h=1\,\mathrm{mm}$, keep the lower plate fixed and move the upper one at $U=0.10\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. With <no-slip boundary conditions>, <steady flow>, no imposed <pressure gradient> and negligible edge effects, <Couette flow> has $u(y)=Uy/h$. For <dynamic viscosity> $\mu=0.001\,\mathrm{Pa\,s}$, the <shear stress> magnitude is
$$
\tau=\mu\frac{du}{dy}=\mu\frac{U}{h}=0.10\,\mathrm{Pa}.
$$
The <velocity gradient> describes neighbouring layers sliding at different speeds. Doubling $U$ doubles the required <shear stress>; maintaining the same <velocity> keeps producing <deformation>, rather than fixing a final shape. This gives <viscosity> a concrete meaning: resistance to a rate of motion, not a prohibition on motion.

= Buoyant plume
{parent=Fluid mechanics}

A buoyant plume rises because its density differs from the surrounding fluid and entrains ambient fluid as it travels.

= Pathline
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{title2=$\dot{\mathbf x}=\mathbf u(\mathbf x,t)$}

A <pathline> records the motion of one identified <fluid element>: keep its starting position $\mathbf a$ and starting time $t_0$ fixed, and let physical time $t$ advance. In a <velocity field> $\mathbf u(\mathbf x,t)$ its <Lagrangian trajectory> $\mathbf X(t;t_0,\mathbf a)$ solves the <initial value problem>
$$
\frac{d\mathbf X}{dt}=\mathbf u(\mathbf X(t),t),\qquad \mathbf X(t_0;t_0,\mathbf a)=\mathbf a.
$$
A <streamline> instead freezes the time argument of the <velocity field>; a <streakline> fixes the observation time and varies the release time of many <fluid elements>. A <pathline> is useful for answering where a particular parcel goes and how long it takes, rather than merely which way the <fluid> is moving now. https://web.mit.edu/13.021/demos/lectures/lecture2.pdf[MIT Marine Hydrodynamics, lecture 2, sections 1.1–1.2]{ref}

For a concrete analytical model, take spatial coordinates $x,y$ in metres, time $t\geq0$ in seconds, and the spatially uniform but time-dependent <velocity field>
$$
\mathbf u(x,y,t)=(U,at),\qquad U=1\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}},\qquad a=1\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}.
$$
Its numerical components in these units are $(1,t)$. A <fluid element> starting at the origin at $t_0=0$ has the <pathline>
$$
x(t)=Ut,\qquad y(t)=\frac{at^2}{2},\qquad y=\frac{a}{2U^2}x^2.
$$
At $t=1\ \mathrm s$ it is at $(1,0.5)\ \mathrm m$; at $t=2\ \mathrm s$ it is at $(2,2)\ \mathrm m$. The <parabola> records earlier, shallower <velocity> directions as well as the final direction. Differentiating gives $(\dot x,\dot y)=(U,at)$ and <acceleration> $(0,a)$, so this curved <pathline> needs no spatial variation of the <velocity field>. More generally the <chain rule> along a <pathline> gives $d f(\mathbf X(t),t)/dt=\partial_t f+\mathbf u\cdot\nabla f$, the <material derivative> of a transported <scalar field>.

A motivating tracer demonstration appears in S. J. Kline's film notes: water passes through a contracting channel, and <hydrogen> bubbles mark a small element at a wire at time $t_0$. The illustrated element is shown again $4.7\ \mathrm s$ later, with its accumulated <pathline> superposed. Following that same marked element across successive film frames records a history; the entire bubble pattern in one frame has a different meaning. https://web.mit.edu/hml/ncfmf/05FV.pdf[S. J. Kline, Flow Visualization film notes, figures 7a–7c]{ref}

For a modern version of the readout, use sparse small markers in a transparent water channel, illuminate the viewed plane, and keep an <optical camera> fixed with a ruler in that plane. Record timestamps and track the same isolated marker between frames; successive positions estimate its <pathline> and $[\mathbf X(t+\Delta t)-\mathbf X(t)]/\Delta t$ estimates its <velocity>. A long exposure records a <pathline> segment but loses the timing within it, so its total length divided by exposure time gives only mean <speed>. These are proposed measurement steps based on Jean Hertzberg's teaching guidance, rather than additional measurements from Kline's film. Markers leaving the viewed plane, overlapping tracks and perspective can break particle identification; repeat with a thinner viewed slice and a lower marker <concentration>. https://www.flowvis.org/Flow%20Vis%20Guide/particles-4-dilute/[Jean Hertzberg and Flow Vis course students, Particles 4: Dilute Particle Techniques]{ref}

= Control volume
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{title2=$V$}

A <control volume> is a chosen region over which fluid <mass conservation> and momentum balance are integrated. It may be fixed or move with a prescribed <velocity>. Boundary fluxes must use <velocity> relative to the moving boundary. In <Stokes flow>, negligible inertia reduces the momentum balance to the sum of body <forces> and boundary tractions, making a carefully chosen <control volume> useful for finding drag without integrating directly over a curved body.

= Navier slip boundary condition
{c}
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{title2=$u_{\mathrm{rel}}=b\partial_nu$}

= Navier slip
{c}
{synonym}

The <Navier slip boundary condition> allows a <fluid> to slide tangentially past a solid wall while resisting that motion through <wall friction in the Navier slip boundary condition>. For parallel flow past a flat, impermeable wall translating uniformly with tangential <velocity> $U_w$, write the fluid's tangential <velocity> as $u$ and its relative tangential <velocity> as $u_{\mathrm{rel}}=u-U_w$. Then
$$
u_{\mathrm{rel}}=b\partial_nu,\qquad b\ge0,
$$
where $b$ is the <slip length>, also written $\ell_s$. The <unit normal> $\mathbf n$ points from the wall into the <fluid>, and $\partial_nu=\mathbf n\cdot\nabla u$ is the <normal derivative>. The separate <no-penetration boundary condition> requires $(\mathbf u-\mathbf U_w)\cdot\mathbf n=0$: tangential sliding does not allow flow through the wall.

The sign convention matters. In a channel $0<z<H$, the lower wall has inward <unit normal> $+\mathbf e_z$ and the upper wall has inward <unit normal> $-\mathbf e_z$. If both walls obey the <Navier slip boundary condition>, their tangential <velocities> satisfy
$$
u(0)-U_0=b_0u'(0),\qquad
u(H)-U_H=-b_Hu'(H).
$$
Here the prime means the <derivative> with respect to $z$. Using $+b_Hu'(H)$ at the upper wall would reverse the friction law. The examples <Couette flow with a slipping lower wall> and <planar Poiseuille flow with equal wall slip> use different upper-wall <boundary conditions>.

This flat-wall formula is a local linear model. On a curved wall the <Newtonian fluid stress tensor> must supply the tangential <traction>; the <normal derivative> alone generally misses curvature terms. The author-written review by Lauga, Brenner and Stone states the full strain-based condition with the <unit normal> directed into the liquid. https://arxiv.org/pdf/cond-mat/0501557[Lauga, Brenner and Stone, Microfluidics: The No-Slip Boundary Condition (2005), §2.1, equation (2)]{ref}

The same review credits Navier's 1823 treatise with the linear slip law. Its history is a reminder that the <no-slip boundary condition> is an experimentally successful model, whose validity depends on the interface and scale. The history here follows that review rather than a new reading of Navier's original memoir. https://arxiv.org/pdf/cond-mat/0501557[Lauga, Brenner and Stone (2005), §2.1: the nineteenth-century boundary-condition debate]{ref}

= Slip length
{parent=Navier slip boundary condition}
{title2=$b=\ell_s$}

The <slip length> measures tangential wall mobility in the <Navier slip boundary condition>. Where the wall <shear rate> is nonzero,
$$
b=\frac{u_{\mathrm{rel}}}{\partial_nu}.
$$
The numerator is a <velocity> and the denominator has units $\mathrm{s^{-1}}$, so $b$ has units of length. Geometrically, take a local inward normal coordinate $s$, with the wall at $s=0$. The tangent line to the relative <velocity> profile is
$$
u_{\mathrm{rel,lin}}(s)=u_{\mathrm{rel}}(0)+s\partial_nu(0)
=(b+s)\partial_nu(0).
$$
It reaches zero at $s=-b$, a fictitious distance $b$ inside the solid. This extrapolation does not assert that <fluid> exists there. At a moving wall, extrapolate to the wall's <velocity>, rather than to zero laboratory <velocity>. If both the wall <shear rate> and relative <velocity> vanish, their ratio cannot determine $b$.

The useful comparison is $b/H$, where $H$ is the channel gap. A small absolute <slip length> can noticeably change <volumetric flow rate> in a micrometre-scale channel. Measured effective <slip length> may summarize unresolved interfacial structure; it need not mean literal molecular sliding at every point. The review distinguishes intrinsic, apparent and effective slip and discusses how surface conditions affect measurements. https://arxiv.org/pdf/cond-mat/0501557[Lauga, Brenner and Stone (2005), §§2.2 and 3.1: slip terminology and measurement]{ref}

= Couette flow with a slipping lower wall
{parent=Slip length}
{title2=$u(z)=U(z+b)/(H+b)$}

Consider <steady flow> of a <Newtonian fluid> in <incompressible flow> with constant <dynamic viscosity> $\mu>0$ between parallel plates at $z=0$ and $z=H$. The bottom plate is stationary and obeys the <Navier slip boundary condition> with <slip length> $b$. The top plate moves at <velocity> $U>0$ and obeys the <no-slip boundary condition>. There is no streamwise <pressure gradient> or body <force>. The <Couette flow> solution is
$$
u(z)=U\frac{z+b}{H+b},\qquad
u(0)=\frac{Ub}{H+b},\qquad
\tau=\mu u'(z)=\frac{\mu U}{H+b}.
$$
Here $\tau$ is the constant <shear stress> magnitude. The lower wall's <Navier slip boundary condition> holds exactly because $bu'(0)=bU/(H+b)=u(0)$. At the top, $u(H)=U$ is exactly the prescribed <no-slip boundary condition>; no finite upper-wall <slip length> is imposed in this example.

For a numerical example, stipulate $H=10\,\mathrm{\mu m}$, $b=2\,\mathrm{\mu m}$, $U=1\,\mathrm{mm\,s^{-1}}$ and $\mu=10^{-3}\,\mathrm{Pa\,s}$. The bottom <velocity> is $0.167\,\mathrm{mm\,s^{-1}}$ and the <shear stress> is $0.0833\,\mathrm{Pa}$, compared with $0.100\,\mathrm{Pa}$ when both plates obey the <no-slip boundary condition>. The extrapolated profile reaches zero at $z=-2\,\mathrm{\mu m}$.

\Image[navier-slip-couette.png]
{title=Couette profiles reveal slip length by extrapolation beneath the lower wall}
{description=The bottom wall is stationary with <slip length> $b$; the upper wall at $z=H$ moves at <velocity> $U$ with a <no-slip boundary condition>. Solid lines show <Couette flow> for $b/H=0$, $0.25$ and $0.75$. Dashed continuations inside the shaded solid reach zero at $z=-b$. Increasing <slip length> reduces the <shear rate> required for the same upper-wall <velocity>.}

For fixed $H$, $U$ and $\mu$, $b\to0$ recovers $u=Uz/H$. As $b\to\infty$, $u\to U$ throughout the gap and $\tau\to0$: the moving upper wall selects the limiting uniform <velocity>, while the lower wall approaches a <stress-free boundary condition>. The <dynamic viscosity> stays positive; the disappearing <shear stress> comes from the disappearing <velocity gradient>.

A short derivation uses <unidirectional flow>: the <convective acceleration> vanishes and the <Navier-Stokes equation> reduces to $\mu u''=0$. Write $u=Az+B$. The bottom condition gives $B=bA$ and the top condition gives $A(H+b)=U$.

= Planar Poiseuille flow with equal wall slip
{parent=Slip length}
{title2=$q=GH^3(1+6b/H)/(12\mu)$}

Now both plates at $z=0$ and $z=H$ are stationary and both obey the <Navier slip boundary condition> with the same <slip length> $b$. Drive <steady flow> of a <Newtonian fluid> in <incompressible flow> by the constant <pressure gradient> $G=-dp/dx>0$, with constant <dynamic viscosity> $\mu>0$ and no other streamwise body <force>. The planar <Poiseuille flow> profile and its <volumetric flow rate> per unit width are
$$
u(z)=\frac{G}{2\mu}\left[z(H-z)+bH\right],\qquad
q=\int_0^H u(z)\,dz
=\frac{GH^3}{12\mu}\left(1+\frac{6b}{H}\right).
$$
The full <volumetric flow rate> for channel width $W$ is $Q=Wq$ when side-wall effects are negligible. Thus $q$ has units $\mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}$, while $Q$ has units $\mathrm{m^3\,s^{-1}}$. Denote the value at $b=0$ by $q_0=GH^3/(12\mu)$.

The two wall checks expose the normal signs:
$$
u'(z)=\frac{G}{2\mu}(H-2z),\qquad
u(0)=u(H)=\frac{GbH}{2\mu},
$$
$$
bu'(0)=\frac{GbH}{2\mu}=u(0),\qquad
-bu'(H)=\frac{GbH}{2\mu}=u(H).
$$
Both <Navier slip boundary conditions> therefore hold exactly. The two walls exert backward tangential <tractions> of magnitude $GH/2$ on the <fluid>. Increasing $b$ at fixed $G$ adds a uniform <velocity> offset, leaving the <velocity gradient> and wall <shear stress> unchanged. This differs from <Couette flow with a slipping lower wall>, where upper-wall <velocity> is fixed and <shear stress> decreases.

For $H=10\,\mathrm{\mu m}$, $b=2\,\mathrm{\mu m}$, $G=10^3\,\mathrm{Pa\,m^{-1}}$ and $\mu=10^{-3}\,\mathrm{Pa\,s}$, the wall <velocity> is $10\,\mathrm{\mu m\,s^{-1}}$, the centre <velocity> is $22.5\,\mathrm{\mu m\,s^{-1}}$, and $q=1.83\times10^{-10}\,\mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}$. The enhancement is $q/q_0=2.2$. Equivalently, a prescribed <volumetric flow rate> needs only $1/2.2$ of the original <pressure gradient>. This makes <slip length> useful for estimating pumping requirements in small channels.

\Image[navier-slip-channel-flow.png]
{title=Equal slip at both channel walls increases pressure-driven flow}
{description=Both walls are stationary and obey the <Navier slip boundary condition>. Left: normalized planar <Poiseuille flow> profiles at fixed <pressure gradient> $G$, gap $H$ and <dynamic viscosity> $\mu$ for $b/H=0$, $0.10$ and $0.25$. Arrows show inward <unit normals> in opposite directions at the two walls. Right: <volumetric flow rate> per unit width increases by the factor $1+6b/H$ at fixed $G$.}

At fixed $H$, $G>0$ and $\mu$, $b\to0$ recovers ordinary planar <Poiseuille flow>. As $b\to\infty$, the <velocity> and $q$ grow without bound in this model, although the varying part of the profile stays the same. At exactly zero tangential wall <traction>, no steady solution can balance this nonzero <pressure gradient>: $u'(0)=u'(H)=0$ conflicts with $\mu[u'(H)-u'(0)]=-GH$. Unlimited enhancement is consequently not a prediction for a real channel; finite wall friction, finite driving capacity and the validity of the assumed <steady flow> must be assessed. At fixed finite $q$ instead, $G\to0$ and the profile approaches uniform <velocity> $q/H$.

A short derivation integrates the reduced <Navier-Stokes equation> $\mu u''=-G$ twice. Equal <slip lengths> and the opposite inward <unit normals> give $u=G[z(H-z)+bH]/(2\mu)$. Its <integral> over the gap supplies the enhancement factor $1+6b/H$.

= Wall friction in the Navier slip boundary condition
{parent=Navier slip boundary condition}
{title2=$\mathcal D_w=\mu u_{\mathrm{rel}}^2/b$}

For a <Newtonian fluid> and a flat wall with $0<b<\infty$, the <Navier slip boundary condition> is equivalent to a tangential wall-on-fluid <traction>
$$
t_{w,t}=-\frac{\mu}{b}u_{\mathrm{rel}}.
$$
The sign expresses resisting <friction>: $\partial_nu$ uses the inward <unit normal>, whereas the wall-on-fluid <traction> uses the outward <unit normal> of the fluid domain. The irreversible loss of mechanical <energy> per unit wall <area> and time is
$$
\mathcal D_w=-t_{w,t}u_{\mathrm{rel}}
=\frac{\mu}{b}u_{\mathrm{rel}}^2\ge0,
\qquad [\mathcal D_w]=\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}.
$$
For two tangential directions replace $u_{\mathrm{rel}}^2$ by the squared <Euclidean norm> of relative tangential <velocity>. At a moving wall this relative-<velocity> loss must be distinguished from the <power> supplied by the wall's motion. Bulk <viscous dissipation> also remains present. A finite <slip length> models reduced interfacial resistance, without declaring every boundary ideal or removing <dynamic viscosity>.

The limiting loss depends on how the flow is driven. In <Couette flow with a slipping lower wall>,
$$
\mathcal D_w=\frac{\mu U^2b}{(H+b)^2}.
$$
It tends to zero for both $b\to0$ and $b\to\infty$ at fixed $H$ and $U$. At $b=0$ impose the <no-slip boundary condition> directly, rather than dividing by zero.
