In <planar Poiseuille flow with equal wall slip> at fixed $G$, each wall instead dissipates $G^2bH^2/(4\mu)$, which grows with $b$ because relative <velocity> grows. The bulk loss per unit width is $G^2H^3/(12\mu)$; adding both wall losses gives $Gq$, the <pressure> work per unit channel length and width. This <energy> balance shows why small friction coefficients do not guarantee small losses under every driving constraint.

= Fluid free boundary
{parent=Fluid mechanics}

A <fluid free boundary> is an initially unknown material interface separating a fluid from its exterior. For a <polytropic elliptical patch in a shearing sheet> bounded by vacuum, it is the level set $p=\rho=Q=0$, and the velocity is tangent to it in a steady solution.

= Material surface
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{title2=$S(t)$}

A material surface is transported with its <fluid elements>, including its boundary when open. Its oriented <material surface element> evolves by tangent transport. <Magnetic flux freezing> conserves the flux through such an open surface.

= Material surface element
{parent=Material surface}
{title2=$D\mathbf A/Dt=(\nabla\cdot\mathbf u)\mathbf A-(\nabla\mathbf u)^T\mathbf A$}

If $\mathbf A=\mathbf t_a\times\mathbf t_b\,da\,db$ and each tangent satisfies $D\mathbf t/Dt=(\nabla\mathbf u)\mathbf t$, differentiation of the cross product gives $D\mathbf A/Dt=(\nabla\cdot\mathbf u)\mathbf A-(\nabla\mathbf u)^T\mathbf A$. Combining this with ideal induction proves pointwise conservation of $\mathbf B\cdot\mathbf A$.

= Material curve
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{title2=$\mathbf X(a,t)$}

= Material line
{synonym}

A material curve consists of the same <fluid elements> at all times. Differentiating their flow map gives $D\boldsymbol\ell/Dt=(\boldsymbol\ell\cdot\nabla)\mathbf u$ for its tangent. In <ideal magnetohydrodynamics> a magnetic-field tangent obeys this same transport law after division by <mass density>.

= Scalar transport
{parent=Fluid mechanics}

<Scalar transport> follows a material quantity through <advection> and <diffusion equation> terms, for example $\Theta_t+\mathbf U\cdot\nabla\Theta=\kappa\Delta\Theta$. A <passive scalar> does not affect the <velocity field>; an <active scalar> feeds back through forces such as <buoyancy>. With <incompressible flow> bounded by impermeable walls and homogeneous <Neumann boundary conditions>, the spatial mean is conserved.

= Active scalar
{parent=Scalar transport}

An <active scalar> is a transported <scalar field> that feeds back on the <velocity field>, for example a density anomaly producing <buoyancy>. In an <adjoint equations for Boussinesq scalar mixing> calculation its dynamical coupling must also be transposed; a passive-scalar sensitivity equation generally omits that feedback.

= Passive scalar
{parent=Scalar transport}

A <passive scalar> is an advected and diffusing <scalar field> whose value has no dynamical effect on the transporting <velocity field>. Concentration of a dilute nonreacting dye is a common example. Its transport can depend on the flow while the flow equations remain independent of it.

= Gaussian scalar packet in a linear incompressible flow
{c}
{parent=Passive scalar}
{title2=$\dot J=CJ+JC^T+2\kappa QI$}

For $\chi=f\exp(-\boldsymbol x^TB\boldsymbol x)$ in two dimensions, take $B$ positive definite, $\operatorname{tr}C=0$ and positive conserved mass $Q=\int\chi\,dA$. The <Gaussian integral> gives $Q=\pi f/\sqrt{\det B}$, $J=(Q/2)B^{-1}$ and $f=Q^2/(2\pi\sqrt{\det J})$. Substitution in the <advection-diffusion equation> gives $\dot B=-C^TB-BC-4\kappa B^2$ and $\dot f/f=-2\kappa\operatorname{tr}B$, equivalently the displayed moment equation. If $\dot F=CF$, its solution is $J=F[J_0+2\kappa Q\int_0^tF^{-1}(s)F^{-T}(s)ds]F^T$, with $\det F=1$.

= Diffusion arrest of Gaussian packet compression
{parent=Gaussian scalar packet in a linear incompressible flow}
{title2=$\ell_B\sim\sqrt{\kappa/\lambda}$}

For a chaotic incompressible planar flow with <Lyapunov exponent> pair $\lambda,-\lambda$, an initially broad <Gaussian scalar packet in a linear incompressible flow> stretches and compresses exponentially while diffusion is negligible. Its compressed variance stops decreasing around $\kappa/\lambda$, the <Batchelor scalar microscale> balance, while the larger variance continues to grow. In a constant principal strain, the normalized covariance eigenvalues obey $\dot j_\pm=\pm2\lambda j_\pm+2\kappa$, proving that the compressed one approaches $\kappa/\lambda$. Random finite-correlation strain instead gives a fluctuating compressed width; stationarity and integrability assumptions are required to turn this into exponential statistics of the packet amplitude.

= Large-deviation decay rates of a Gaussian scalar packet
{parent=Diffusion arrest of Gaussian packet compression}
{title2=$\gamma_\mu=\inf_{s\ge0}[\mathcal I(s)+\mu s]$}

After <diffusion arrest of Gaussian packet compression>, define the logarithmic area increase $A_t=\tfrac12\log[\det J(t)/\det J(0)]$. It is nonnegative. Assume $A_t/t$ has a <large deviation principle> with convex <rate function> $\mathcal I$, minimized at $\lambda>0$. The exact amplitude relation $f(t)=f(0)e^{-A_t}$ and the <Laplace principle for probability measures> give the displayed moment decay rate. The rate function refers to area, so rare width fluctuations are included rather than discarded without justification. For differentiable strictly convex $\mathcal I$, the interior minimizer satisfies $\mathcal I'(s_\mu)=-\mu$. If $\mu_*=-\mathcal I'(0)$ is finite, then $\gamma_\mu=\mathcal I(0)$ for $\mu\ge\mu_*$: rare nearly unstretched packets dominate high moments. Below the threshold, $d(\gamma_\mu/\mu)/d\mu=-\mathcal I(s_\mu)/\mu^2<0$. These statements concern a positive Gaussian packet with flow-only averaging; a homogeneous signed random initial scalar requires a different averaging kernel. Arbitrary random $C(t)$ alone does not imply this statistical model.

= Schmidt number
{disambiguate=fluid mechanics}
{parent=Passive scalar}
{c}
{title2=$\mathrm{Sc}=\nu/\kappa$}
{wiki=Schmidt_number}

= Molecular Schmidt number
{synonym}

The molecular Schmidt number is the ratio of <kinematic viscosity> to scalar <diffusivity>. A large value allows a <passive scalar> to develop structure below the viscous smoothing scale of the <velocity field>. It is distinct from the <turbulent Schmidt number>, which compares effective turbulent transport coefficients, and from the <Schmidt number> used for quantum entanglement.

= Covector evolution of a material scalar gradient
{parent=Passive scalar}
{title2=$D_t\nabla\theta=-(\nabla\boldsymbol u)^T\nabla\theta$}

Differentiate the <advection equation> componentwise: $D_t(\partial_i\theta)=-(\partial_i u_j)\partial_j\theta$. Along a <Lagrangian trajectory>, the scalar <gradient> therefore evolves by the negative transpose of the <velocity gradient tensor>. If $F$ is the <deformation gradient>, this says $\boldsymbol k(t)=F(t)^{-T}\boldsymbol k(0)$, as expected for a <covector>. Fluid material vectors and scalar gradients transform oppositely under stretching.

= Scalar-gradient inclination in a steady planar strain
{parent=Covector evolution of a material scalar gradient}
{title2=$\alpha\to |D|/\sqrt{a^2+b^2}$}

For horizontal strain matrix $S=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\b&-a\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$ and zero vertical velocity, horizontal scalar gradients obey $\dot k_h=-Sk_h$. Since $S^2=c^2I$ with $c=\sqrt{a^2+b^2}$, the growing projection is $(I-S/c)k_h(0)/2$. If nonzero it equals $k_0(\cos\phi,\sin\phi)$ and grows as $e^{ct}$. Fixed vertical shears give $\dot k_3=-\sigma_{13}k_1-\sigma_{23}k_2$, so $k_3\sim-k_0D e^{ct}/c$ with $D=\sigma_{13}\cos\phi+\sigma_{23}\sin\phi$. The ratio $\alpha=|k_3|/|k_h|$ tends to the displayed value. It measures scalar-isosurface orientation; zero growing projection or $D=0$ must not be described as generic exponential vertical-gradient growth.

= White-noise vertical shear gives an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck inclination
{parent=Scalar-gradient inclination in a steady planar strain}
{title2=$d\beta=-c\beta\,dt-g\,dW$}

In the long-time growing horizontal-gradient branch, independent vertical shear noises combine as $\cos\phi\,dW_1+\sin\phi\,dW_2=dW$. The signed inclination $\beta=k_3e^{-ct}/k_0$ satisfies the displayed <Ornstein-Uhlenbeck stochastic differential equation>. For $c>0$ its stationary density is $\sqrt{c/(\pi g^2)}e^{-cB^2/g^2}$, with variance $g^2/(2c)$. The <Fokker-Planck probability current> vanishes at infinity and gives this Gaussian by one integration. At $c=0$ and nonzero noise there is no normalizable stationary density.

= Gaussian-over-Rayleigh inclination distribution
{c}
{parent=Scalar-gradient inclination in a steady planar strain}
{title2=$F_\alpha(A)=\frac{\Gamma A}{\sqrt{\Gamma^2A^2+\Lambda^2}}$}

Let $c=\sqrt{a^2+b^2}$ with independent $a,b\sim N(0,\Gamma^2)$, and let $D\sim N(0,\Lambda^2)$ be independent of them. Then $c$ has <Rayleigh distribution> and $\alpha=|D|/c$ has the displayed <cumulative distribution function> for $A\ge0$ and density $\Lambda^2\Gamma(\Gamma^2A^2+\Lambda^2)^{-3/2}$. Indeed, conditioning on $D$ gives $\mathbb P(c\ge|D|/A)=\exp[-D^2/(2\Gamma^2A^2)]$, and a <Gaussian integral> completes the calculation. Independence is essential: choosing $D=(\Lambda/\Gamma)a$ has the same Gaussian marginal but makes the ratio bounded by $\Lambda/\Gamma$.

= Scalar structure function
{parent=Passive scalar}
{title2=$S_C(r)=\langle[C(\boldsymbol x+\boldsymbol r)-C(\boldsymbol x)]^2\rangle$}

For a homogeneous scalar, $S_C(r)=2[\langle C^2\rangle-\langle CC'\rangle]$. Decorrelation at large separation gives saturation at twice the variance. If the scalar is mean-square differentiable and isotropic, $S_C(r)=r^2\langle|\nabla C|^2\rangle/3+o(r^2)$ at small separation. Statistical isotropy and zero mean alone do not imply decorrelation at infinity.

= Obukhov-Corrsin theory
{c}
{parent=Passive scalar}
{title2=$S_C(r)\sim\epsilon_c\epsilon^{-1/3}r^{2/3}$}

= Kolmogorov-Obukhov-Corrsin theory
{c}
{synonym}

Obukhov-Corrsin theory concerns transfer of <scalar variance> across scales in turbulent mixing. In the inertial-convective range, a constant scalar-variance flux and an eddy turnover time $\epsilon^{-1/3}r^{2/3}$ predict the displayed second-order <scalar structure function>. It assumes a passive scalar, local transfer, approximate statistical equilibrium and separation from the molecular diffusion and forcing scales.

= Inertial-diffusive scalar range
{parent=Obukhov-Corrsin theory}
{title2=$S_C(r)\simeq\epsilon_cr^2/(3\alpha)$}

For scalar diffusivity much larger than <kinematic viscosity>, scalar diffusion dominates at lengths between the Kolmogorov and scalar cutoff scales. The velocity may still have inertial-range variations, but the scalar is smooth to leading order. The leading isotropic <scalar structure function> is quadratic, with coefficient fixed by the mean-square gradient and the <scalar dissipation rate>, rather than by a continuing inertial-convective two-thirds law.

= Corrsin scalar microscale
{c}
{parent=Inertial-diffusive scalar range}
{title2=$\eta_C=(\alpha^3/\epsilon)^{1/4}$}

In the large-diffusivity regime, equate diffusion rate $\alpha/r^2$ with inertial eddy rate $\epsilon^{1/3}r^{-2/3}$. This gives the Corrsin scalar microscale, which exceeds the <Kolmogorov length scale> when $\alpha\gg\nu$.

= Viscous-convective scalar range
{parent=Obukhov-Corrsin theory}
{title2=$S_C(r)\sim\epsilon_c\sqrt{\nu/\epsilon}\log(r/\eta_B)$}

For scalar diffusivity much smaller than <kinematic viscosity>, a scalar has structure below the <Kolmogorov length scale> while the velocity is smooth. The strain rate $s\sim(\epsilon/\nu)^{1/2}$ transfers scalar variance, giving $dS_C/dr\sim\epsilon_c/(sr)$. Integration produces a logarithm plus an additive matching constant.

= Batchelor scalar microscale
{c}
{parent=Viscous-convective scalar range}
{title2=$\eta_B=(\alpha^2\nu/\epsilon)^{1/4}$}

At the Batchelor scalar microscale, molecular diffusion rate $\alpha/r^2$ balances the smooth Kolmogorov strain rate $(\epsilon/\nu)^{1/2}$. For $\alpha\ll\nu$ this scale is much smaller than the <Kolmogorov length scale>.

= Inertial-convective scalar range
{parent=Obukhov-Corrsin theory}

This range lies above both the <Kolmogorov length scale> and the scalar diffusion cutoff, but below the forcing scale. Molecular viscosity and scalar diffusion have negligible direct influence on transfer at those scales. The <Obukhov-Corrsin theory> predicts a two-thirds <scalar structure function> there.

= Horizontally integrated plume concentration
{parent=Passive scalar}
{title2=$c(z,t)=\int C(x,z,t)\,dx$}

The horizontally integrated plume concentration is the <integral> of a local <concentration> $C$ across the plume. It measures scalar amount per unit vertical length and unit span, and its vertical <integral> is total scalar amount per unit span. Unlike a local <concentration>, its dimensions include one horizontal length. It must be distinguished from <horizontally averaged plume concentration> when a plume's width varies with height.

= Horizontally averaged plume concentration
{parent=Horizontally integrated plume concentration}
{title2=$\overline C=c/w$}

For a plume of full width $w(z)$, its horizontally averaged <concentration> is $\overline C(z,t)=c(z,t)/w(z)$, where $c$ is <horizontally integrated plume concentration>. In a <line plume> with $w=\mu z$, division by $z$ generally changes the location of maximum <concentration>. A gradient closure for $c$ differs from one for $\overline C$: $w\partial_z\overline C=c_z-c/z$, so integrated diffusive flux $-Kw\partial_z\overline C$ equals $-K(c_z-c/z)$.

= Adjoint equations for Boussinesq scalar mixing
{parent=Scalar transport}

For a smooth incompressible direct trajectory with total <velocity field> $\mathbf U$ and <active scalar> $\Theta$, the negative-constraint <Lagrangian function in constrained optimization> convention gives
$$
\partial_t\mathbf u^\dagger+\mathbf U\cdot\nabla\mathbf u^\dagger-(\nabla\mathbf U)^T\mathbf u^\dagger+\nabla p^\dagger+\nu\Delta\mathbf u^\dagger-\theta^\dagger\nabla\Theta=0,\qquad \nabla\cdot\mathbf u^\dagger=0,
$$
$$
\partial_t\theta^\dagger+\mathbf U\cdot\nabla\theta^\dagger+\kappa\Delta\theta^\dagger-\mathrm{Ri}_B u_y^\dagger=0.
$$
The transpose term is the <formal adjoint> of $\delta\mathbf u\cdot\nabla\mathbf U$; the two couplings transpose <scalar transport> by <advection> and <buoyancy>. For terminal cost $\int\Theta(T)^2$, the terminal data are $\mathbf u^\dagger(T)=0$, $\theta^\dagger(T)=2\Theta(T)$. They are integrated backward along the stored direct trajectory. <No-slip boundary conditions> and <Dirichlet boundary conditions> for the adjoint <velocity> and homogeneous <Neumann boundary conditions> for the adjoint scalar remove the spatial boundary terms.

= Scalar variance
{parent=Scalar transport}
{title2=$V=|\Omega|^{-1}\int_\Omega(\Theta-\overline\Theta)^2\,dV$}

<Scalar variance> measures spatial departure from the conserved mean. For zero mean, <incompressible flow> bounded by impermeable walls and homogeneous scalar <Neumann boundary conditions>, <integration by parts> gives $d\int\Theta^2/dt=-2\kappa\int|\nabla\Theta|^2$. <Advection> preserves the instantaneous quadratic integral but can sharpen <gradients>, allowing the <diffusion equation> to remove <scalar variance> faster.

= Scalar dissipation rate
{parent=Scalar variance}
{title2=$\epsilon_c=\alpha\langle|\nabla C|^2\rangle$}

With this half-variance convention, the unforced scalar equation gives $d\langle C^2/2\rangle/dt=-\epsilon_c$ in a homogeneous incompressible flow. Authors defining dissipation of the full variance instead use $2\alpha\langle|\nabla C|^2\rangle$. Stating the convention is necessary when relating the dissipation rate to a <scalar structure function>.

= Fluid flow
{parent=Fluid mechanics}

A <fluid flow> is motion described by a <velocity field> $\mathbf u(\mathbf x,t)$. The trajectories of <fluid elements> satisfy $d\mathbf x/dt=\mathbf u(\mathbf x,t)$; the <mass density> and other fields obey their <conservation laws> and <constitutive equations>. This physical motion differs from a <flow> on a <flow network>.

= Unidirectional flow
{parent=Fluid flow}
{title2=$\boldsymbol u=u(y)\boldsymbol e_x$}

A unidirectional flow has velocity parallel to a single fixed direction, for example $\boldsymbol u=u(y)\boldsymbol e_x$. If the speed is independent of the coordinate along that direction, the convective acceleration $(\boldsymbol u\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol u$ vanishes. The steady <Navier-Stokes equation> then reduces to a linear balance of the pressure gradient and viscosity, as in <Couette flow> and <Poiseuille flow>.

= Steady flow
{parent=Fluid flow}
{title2=$\partial_t\mathbf u=0$}

In steady flow, the velocity and other relevant flow fields are independent of time in the chosen reference frame. <Streamlines> then coincide with the paths of moving particles, while <convective acceleration> can still be nonzero.

= Inviscid flow
{parent=Fluid flow}

An inviscid flow neglects <viscosity> in the <fluid flow>. Its <momentum> balance is described by the <Euler equations for an inviscid fluid>.

= Inviscid falling jet and radial impact film
{parent=Inviscid flow}
{title2=$u^2=u_0^2+2g(H-z),\quad \pi R^2u=\pi a^2u_0$}

A steady incompressible jet at atmospheric <pressure> conserves <Bernoulli's equation> and <volume flux>. Its radius contracts as $R=a[u/u_0]^{-1/2}$. After impact, a thin nearly horizontal axisymmetric film has $2\pi rhv=\pi a^2u_0$. Evaluating <Bernoulli's equation> on its <free surface> gives $v^2=u_0^2+2g(H-h)$, hence $a^4/(4r^2h^2)=1+2g(H-h)/u_0^2$. This inviscid thin-film description applies away from the impact region and does not describe a dissipative hydraulic jump.

= Sediment transport
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

= Sediment resuspension
{parent=Sediment transport}

Sediment resuspension transfers deposited bed particles into a <particle suspension>. It can supply particle <buoyancy flux> to a <particle-laden gravity current> if erosion or upward mixing exceeds settling losses. Ambient fluid without particles dilutes concentration without supplying particle mass.

= Equilibrium settling-diffusion profile
{parent=Sediment resuspension}
{title2=$\phi(z)=\phi_1(z/z_1)^{-W_s/K}$}

For downward <settling velocity> magnitude $W_s>0$ and <eddy diffusivity> $\kappa_T=Kz$, the total upward particle flux is $J=-W_s\phi-Kz\phi_z$. A stationary profile tending to zero at infinity has $J=0$, giving the displayed power law. The exponent compares settling with turbulent mixing. It describes a region above the bed; entrainment from the bed and viscous near-wall effects determine the reference concentration separately.

= Sediment transport saturation
{parent=Sediment transport}

= Equilibrium sediment flux
{parent=Sediment transport saturation}
{title2=$q_{\mathrm{sat}}$}

= Saturated sediment flux
{synonym}

= Saturated sand flux
{synonym}

The <equilibrium sediment flux> is the transport supported locally by a prescribed bed <shear stress>. A common idealization is $q_{\mathrm{sat}}=\phi_b\chi(\tau-\tau_{\mathrm{th}})_+^\gamma$. Linearization above the <sediment entrainment threshold> requires perturbations small compared with the positive excess stress.

= Saturation length
{parent=Sediment transport saturation}
{title2=$L_{\mathrm{sat}}$}

The <saturation length> measures downstream relaxation of a transported grain population toward its equilibrium <sediment transport> flux. The elementary steady relaxation law is $L_{\mathrm{sat}}q_x=q_{\mathrm{sat}}-q$; it introduces a phase lag between forcing and actual transport.

= Exner equation
{c}
{parent=Sediment transport}
{title2=$\phi_b\eta_t=-q_x$}
{wiki}

The <Exner equation> is local sediment <mass conservation> expressed in terms of solid volume: accumulation raises the bed and flux divergence lowers it. Here $\phi_b$ is the solid volume fraction of the packed bed and $q$ is solid volume flux per unit width.

= Shields parameter
{c}
{parent=Sediment transport}
{title2=$\Theta$}
{wiki}

= Shields number
{c}
{synonym}

The <Shields parameter> is $\Theta=\tau/[(\rho_p-\rho_f)gd]$, comparing bed <shear stress> with a grain-scale <submerged weight> per area. Its critical value depends on the assumed <drag coefficient>, <static friction>, lift, and contact geometry.

= Sediment entrainment threshold
{parent=Shields parameter}
{title2=$\tau_{\mathrm{th}}$}

= Threshold shear stress
{synonym}

The <sediment entrainment threshold> is the bed <shear stress> at which resting grains begin moving. An elementary <force balance> using spherical grains, tangential <quadratic drag>, and <static friction> gives $\Theta_{\mathrm{th},0}=4\mu_s/(3C_D)$ when the <drag coefficient> is defined relative to the bed <shear velocity>.

= Inclined-bed sediment threshold
{parent=Sediment entrainment threshold}

For locally bed-tangent <drag force> and an upslope angle $a$, a spherical-grain <force balance> gives $\tau_{\mathrm{th}}(a)=\tau_{\mathrm{th},0}(\cos a+\sin a/\mu_s)$. The small-slope term is $\delta\tau_{\mathrm{th}}=(\tau_{\mathrm{th},0}/\mu_s)\eta_x$. A horizontal <drag force> instead changes the normal contact <force> and gives a different slope correction.

= Bedform
{parent=Sediment transport}
{wiki}

= Sediment-bed linear instability
{parent=Bedform}

Combining the <Exner equation>, <sediment transport saturation>, <bed shear response>, and <inclined-bed sediment threshold> gives a competition between upstream forcing and downstream relaxation. With $a=\tau_0A$, $b=\tau_0B-\tau_{\mathrm{th},0}/\mu_s$, $C=\chi\gamma(\tau_0-\tau_{\mathrm{th},0})^{\gamma-1}$, and positive <wavenumber> $k$, the <growth rate> is $\sigma=Ck^2(b-akL_{\mathrm{sat}})/(1+k^2L_{\mathrm{sat}}^2)$. Thus $b>0$ permits long-wave growth when $a>0$, while a finite <saturation length> stabilizes shorter waves.

= Bed shear response
{parent=Bedform}

For a small sinusoidal <bedform>, a fluid-dynamical closure can be written $\hat\tau=\tau_0k(A+iB)\hat\eta$ for $k>0$. The real coefficient $A$ is in phase with the bed height and $B$ describes an upstream phase lead. Neither coefficient follows from the <Exner equation> or sediment relaxation alone; they require a flow calculation or measurement.

= Ram pressure
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{title2=$P_{\rm ram}\sim\rho v^2$}
{wiki}

The momentum-flux <pressure> associated with a moving fluid. Some energy-density balances use $\rho v^2/2$ instead; the numerical convention must be stated when balancing <magnetic pressure>.

\Include[turbulence]{parent=Fluid mechanics}

= Acoustic wave
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

An acoustic wave is a propagating pressure and density disturbance in a material medium. Linear sound waves oscillate when pressure restores a displaced fluid element.

= Burgers reduction of weakly nonlinear rightgoing acoustics
{c}
{parent=Acoustic wave}
{title2=$p_X-\tfrac{\gamma+1}{2}p\,p_\theta=\tfrac{\beta}{2M}p_{\theta\theta}$}

For a pure rightgoing <acoustic wave>, retarded time $\theta=t-x$ and slow distance $X=Mx$ separate fast oscillations from nonlinear steepening. Expanding the <equation of state> gives $p=\rho+M(\gamma-1)\rho^2/2+O(M^2)$. The leading perturbations satisfy $u_0=\rho_0=p_0$. Adding the first-order <mass conservation> and <momentum conservation> equations cancels the unknown first corrections and yields the displayed <viscous Burgers equation>. Inviscid characteristics satisfy $\theta=s-(\gamma+1)F(s)X/2$, $p=F(s)$; loss of invertibility marks <shock> formation and the end of the smooth inviscid approximation.

= Adiabatic vertical modes of a Gaussian atmosphere
{parent=Acoustic wave}
{title2=$\sigma_n^2=\Omega^2[1+\gamma(2n+1)]$}

A <plane-parallel atmosphere> with <isothermal sound speed> $c_s$ in gravity $-\Omega^2z$ has <mass density> $\rho_0e^{-z^2/(2H^2)}$, $H=c_s/\Omega$. Above a rigid base, vertically directed <adiabatic fluid perturbations> satisfy $w''-zw'/H^2+(\sigma^2/\Omega^2-1)w/(\gamma H^2)=0$ and $w(0)=0$. Set $x=z/H$ and $\lambda=(\sigma^2/\Omega^2-1)/\gamma$. The substitution $y=e^{-x^2/4}w$ converts finite Gaussian-weighted <kinetic energy> into square integrability for $-y''+x^2y/4=(\lambda+1/2)y$. Odd extension across the base and the oscillator lowering operator force $\lambda$ to be an odd positive integer. Thus $w_n$ is proportional to the <probabilists' Hermite polynomial> $\mathrm{He}_{2n+1}(z/H)$. These are vertical <acoustic waves>; a general stably stratified atmosphere also admits <internal gravity waves> with horizontal structure.

= Acoustically rigid boundary
{parent=Acoustic wave}
{title2=$v_n=0$}

= Acoustically rigid
{synonym}

An acoustically rigid boundary has zero perturbation <normal velocity>. For a reflected <acoustic plane wave>, <pressure> amplitudes add in phase and the <reflection coefficient> is $+1$. It is the infinite-<surface acoustic impedance> limit. A <pressure-release boundary> instead fixes the <pressure> perturbation to zero and reflects with coefficient $-1$.

= Acoustic cutoff frequency
{parent=Acoustic wave}
{title2=$\omega_{\rm ac}\simeq c_s/(2H_\rho)$}

A stratified atmosphere can reflect low-frequency <acoustic waves> while permitting sufficiently high-frequency waves to escape. In a plane-parallel isothermal atmosphere, the angular acoustic cutoff is approximately $c_s/(2H_\rho)$, where $H_\rho$ is the density scale height. Its detailed form depends on the atmospheric profile and on the wave-variable approximation.

= Pressure-release boundary
{parent=Acoustic wave}
{title2=$\widetilde p=0$}

A pressure-release boundary imposes zero acoustic pressure disturbance, $\widetilde p=0$. The incident and reflected pressure amplitudes therefore obey $1+R=0$, so the pressure <reflection coefficient> is $R=-1$. It contrasts with a rigid inviscid boundary, where zero normal velocity gives $R=+1$. A much lower normal impedance on the far side approximates the pressure-release condition.

= Velocity field
{title2=$\mathbf u(\mathbf x,t)$}
{parent=Fluid mechanics}

A velocity field assigns a velocity vector to every position and time in a moving continuum. Its spatial derivatives describe local deformation, rotation, and volume change.

= Secondary flow
{parent=Velocity field}

A secondary flow is a usually smaller velocity component superposed on a primary flow selected by symmetry or a simplified force balance. Examples include meridional circulation around a rotating sphere, or transverse circulation in a curved channel. Its direction depends on the forcing mechanism: elastic <normal stresses> and inertial effects can produce opposite circulations.

= Velocity gradient tensor
{parent=Velocity field}
{title2=$A_{ij}=\partial_j u_i$}

The <velocity gradient tensor> is $A_{ij}=\partial_j u_i$. Its symmetric part is the <rate-of-strain tensor> $S$ and its antisymmetric part describes local rotation. For <incompressible flow>, $\operatorname{tr}A=\operatorname{tr}S=0$. The sign convention for a cubic invariant must be specified explicitly.

= Vertical velocity
{parent=Velocity field}
{title2=$w=\boldsymbol u\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol z}$}

The vertical velocity is the component of a fluid <velocity field> along the selected upward vertical direction. In <incompressible flow>, impermeable horizontal boundaries impose $w=0$. Eliminating <pressure> often yields a closed <hydrodynamic stability> equation for this component.

= Axisymmetric flow
{parent=Velocity field}

An axisymmetric flow is invariant under rotations about one fixed axis, so scalar fields and cylindrical-coordinate velocity components do not depend on the azimuthal angle.

= Advection
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

Advection transports a quantity with a <velocity field>. For a scalar field $f$, its local advective rate is $\mathbf u\mathbin\cdot\nabla f$, the spatial contribution to the <material derivative>. The same term transports the components of a vector field.

= Chaotic advection
{parent=Advection}

Chaotic advection is complicated, sensitive material motion generated by a smooth <velocity field>, often even when the Eulerian flow is periodic and simple. Transverse intersections of <stable manifolds> and <unstable manifolds> create stretching, folding and lobe transport across former <separatrices>. <Incompressible flow> preserves parcel area and advected scalar values in the absence of <diffusion>, so chaotic motion can create fine filaments without independently producing molecular homogenization.

= Ballistic transport
{parent=Advection}
{title2=$\operatorname{Var}X(t)\propto t^2$}

A trajectory with a persistent random velocity $V$ has $X(t)-X(0)=Vt$. An ensemble with finite velocity variance therefore has displacement variance $t^2\operatorname{Var}V$, contrasting with the linear-in-time variance of <diffusion>. In an <impulsively kicked sinusoidal shear dispersion> model with zero transverse diffusivity, the shear phase never changes and repeated identical kicks have this ballistic scaling. Transverse diffusion instead decorrelates the velocity and permits a long-time diffusive limit.

= Convection
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

Convection transports properties such as <energy> or composition through bulk fluid motion. <Thermal convection> is driven by temperature-dependent <buoyancy>.

= Convection cell
{parent=Convection}

An organized region of buoyant rising fluid and compensating descending flow that transports heat or composition. <Convection rolls> are a simple planar example; <solar granules> are evolving compressible examples. A cellular pattern does not require every material trajectory to form a closed streamline, especially near a radiating free surface.

= Convection zone
{parent=Convection}

A stellar <convection zone> is a region where buoyant fluid motion transports a significant part of the stellar heat flux. Rising and sinking parcels carry enthalpy, so heat transport is not entirely radiative. The <Sun>'s outer <convection zone> extends from near $0.7R_\odot$ to the surface and overlies its radiative interior. The <tachocline> lies near this transition, and the <convection> helps power the <solar dynamo>.

= Compositional convection
{parent=Convection}

Compositional convection is buoyancy-driven motion caused by composition-dependent density variations. In a planetary core, freezing can expel light elements into the surrounding liquid, making that liquid buoyant and supplying mechanical power for a <planetary dynamo>. It can operate alongside thermally driven <convection> and release of <latent heat>.

= Long-wave convection equation with broken Boussinesq symmetry
{parent=Convection}
{title2=$\Theta_t=-\Theta-\mu\Theta_{xx}-\Theta_{xxxx}-s(\Theta_x^2)_x+(\Theta_x^3)_x$}

A reduced long-wave <convection> model can combine linear damping, destabilizing second derivatives, stabilizing fourth derivatives and gradient nonlinearities. In the displayed normalization the quadratic coefficient $s$ breaks <Boussinesq symmetry>, while the cubic gradient term dissipates the mean-square temperature. The linear <dispersion relation> is $\lambda(k)=-1+\mu k^2-k^4$, with critical <wavenumber> $k=1$ at $\mu=2$. Smoothness and spatial averaging hypotheses must be specified before applying the <energy method> on an infinite interval.

= Second-harmonic feedback in a long-wave convection amplitude equation
{parent=Long-wave convection equation with broken Boussinesq symmetry}
{title2=$g=3-8s^2/9$}

In a <weakly nonlinear expansion> of the <long-wave convection equation with broken Boussinesq symmetry> at $\mu=2$, a critical <Fourier mode> $\Theta_1=Ae^{ix}+\mathrm{c.c.}$ generates $\Theta_2=(2is/9)A^2e^{2ix}+\mathrm{c.c.}$. The quadratic interaction of these first and second harmonics feeds back $8s^2|A|^2A/9$ into the critical <Fourier mode>, while the cubic gradient nonlinearity contributes $-3|A|^2A$. The <Fredholm solvability condition> is consequently $\mu_2A-g|A|^2A=0$. The sign of $g$ distinguishes supercritical and subcritical branches; $g=0$ requires a higher-order <amplitude equation>.

= Energy square completion for long-wave convection
{parent=Long-wave convection equation with broken Boussinesq symmetry}
{title2=$\mu_E=2-s^2/4$}

For a real smooth solution of the <long-wave convection equation with broken Boussinesq symmetry>, assume a periodic spatial average or an existing long-interval average with vanishing endpoint fluxes. <Integration by parts> gives the exact <energy method> identity
$$
\frac12\partial_t\langle\Theta^2\rangle
=-\langle(\Theta+\Theta_{xx})^2\rangle
-\langle\Theta_x^2(\Theta_x-s/2)^2\rangle
+(\mu-2+s^2/4)\langle\Theta_x^2\rangle.
$$
Thus $\mu<\mu_E$ prevents growth of the mean-square temperature at arbitrary amplitude in this averaging class. This sufficient nonlinear bound need not equal the linear instability threshold, and it does not imply pointwise monotonicity of the temperature.

= Thermal convection
{parent=Convection}

Thermal convection is fluid motion driven by temperature-dependent <buoyancy>. It transports <heat> by bulk motion in addition to <thermal conduction>.

= Horizontal convection
{parent=Thermal convection}
{wiki}

Horizontal convection is <buoyancy>-driven circulation forced by lateral variations of <temperature> along one horizontal boundary. Hot and cold surface regions drive a circulation even though all thermal forcing is applied at the same height. An insulated bottom and sides require zero net heat exchange through the remaining surface in steady state, but permit heat to enter its warmer region and leave its colder region.

= Shallow horizontal convection with an insulated bottom
{parent=Horizontal convection}
{title2=$\psi=-\operatorname{Ra}\epsilon^4y(1-y)^2(2y+1)/48$}

For downward depth $z=\epsilon y$, take $(u,w)=(\psi_z,-\psi_x)$, surface temperature $T=x$, a flat impermeable stress-free upper boundary and a no-slip insulated bottom. In the steady infinite-<Prandtl number> thin-layer interior, $\psi_{zzzz}=-\operatorname{Ra}$ with $\psi=\psi_{zz}=0$ at the surface and $\psi=\psi_z=0$ at the bottom. The displayed profile follows. Sidewall end layers close the circulation; the interior profile must not be imposed unchanged at those walls.

= Advection correction in shallow horizontal convection
{parent=Shallow horizontal convection with an insulated bottom}
{title2=$T-x=-\operatorname{Ra}\epsilon^5(2y^5/5-3y^4/4+y^2/2)/48$}

The interior first temperature correction obeys $\Theta_{zz}=u=\psi_z$, $\Theta(0)=0$ and $\Theta_z(\epsilon)=0$. Hence $\Theta_z=\psi$, which integrates to the displayed polynomial. Its contribution to depth-integrated horizontal advective <heat flux> is $\int u\Theta\,dz=-\int\psi^2dz=-19\operatorname{Ra}^2\epsilon^9/1451520$. The leading conductive flux is $-\epsilon$. These statements apply away from the sidewall end layers at fixed Rayleigh number as the layer becomes shallow.

= Forced convection
{parent=Thermal convection}
{wiki}

Heat-carrying fluid motion dominated by an externally imposed flow or shear. Buoyancy may still influence it; forced and free contributions can coexist. In surface-layer scaling, $z\ll|\ell_O|$ is predominantly shear-driven, while $z\gg|\ell_O|$ is predominantly buoyancy-driven for lower-boundary heating.

= Natural convection
{parent=Thermal convection}
{wiki}

= Free convection
{synonym}

Convection driven primarily by buoyancy generated by heating or cooling, rather than an externally imposed mean flow. In a constant-flux unstable surface layer, the local speed scales as $(B_0z)^{1/3}$.

= Free-convection surface-layer scaling
{parent=Natural convection}
{title2=$w_c\sim(B_0z)^{1/3},\quad\epsilon\sim B_0$}

For a positive approximately constant <vertical turbulent buoyancy flux>, dimensional turnover scaling gives $K_b\sim B_0^{1/3}z^{4/3}$ and $N^2\sim-B_0^{2/3}z^{-4/3}$. The negative squared frequency describes unstable overturning, not a real restoring oscillation. The leading dissipation is height-independent, unlike neutral wall turbulence.

= Hexagonal convection amplitude equations
{parent=Thermal convection}
{title2=$A_T=\mu_2KA+\gamma K\bar B\bar C-3K^2A\sum|A_j|^2$}

A finite-<wavenumber> convection instability with a <resonant three-wave triad> can yield three coupled <amplitude equations>. For the gradient nonlinearity $\nabla\cdot(|\nabla\Theta|^2\nabla\Theta)$ and weak quadratic asymmetry $-\epsilon\gamma\nabla\cdot(\Theta\nabla\Theta)$, projection onto a triad of length squared $K$ gives the stated equation and its cyclic copies. The quadratic coefficient is $+\gamma K$, while both self and cross cubic coefficients are $-3K^2$ for angles of $120$ degrees. The finite critical mode requires positive linear damping in the underlying long-wave equation.

= Rotational hexagon amplitude equations
{parent=Hexagonal convection amplitude equations}

For a <resonant three-wave triad> with rotational and translational <symmetry> but no spatial <reflection>, the cubic <amplitude equations> have the cyclic form $\dot A=\mu A+\overline B\,\overline C-\nu_1|A|^2A-(\nu_2+\delta)|B|^2A-(\nu_2-\delta)|C|^2A$. A half-turn induces <complex conjugation>, so all coefficients are real. The coefficient $\delta$ distinguishes the two cyclic cross-couplings that a <reflection> would interchange.

= Roll and hexagon spectra without reflection symmetry
{parent=Rotational hexagon amplitude equations}

For the <rotational hexagon amplitude equations>, put $d=\nu_2-\nu_1$ and $N=\nu_1+2\nu_2$. A roll with $r^2=\mu/\nu_1$ has transverse <eigenvalues> $-dr^2\pm\sqrt{\delta^2r^4+r^2}$, each twice, radial <eigenvalue> $-2\mu$, and one neutral translation. A hexagon with $\mu=Nh^2-h$ has real-amplitude <eigenvalues> $h-2Nh^2$ and $-2h+2dh^2\pm2i\sqrt3\delta h^2$; its imaginary-amplitude <eigenvalues> are $-3h,0,0$. These follow by diagonalizing the cyclic <Jacobian matrix>. Stability is understood modulo translations. If $\nu_2>\nu_1>0$ and $\delta\ne0$, the upper positive hexagon branch reaches a <Hopf bifurcation> at $h=1/d$, $\mu=(2\nu_1+\nu_2)/d^2$.

= Axial isotropy of rolls and hexagons
{parent=Rotational hexagon amplitude equations}

Write the translation <group action> as $(A,B,C)\mapsto(e^{i\theta_1}A,e^{i\theta_2}B,e^{-i(\theta_1+\theta_2)}C)$. Modulo lattice translations, the <stabilizer subgroup> of $(h,h,h)$, with nonzero real $h$, is the sixfold rotation group. Its <fixed-point subspace of a group action> is the real line $(a,a,a)$. The <stabilizer subgroup> of $(r,0,0)$, with nonzero real $r$, consists of the circle $\theta_1=0$ together with a half-turn; its <fixed-point subspace of a group action> is the real line $(a,0,0)$. The <equivariant branching lemma> therefore supplies generic branches of both axial types.

= Roll instability from broken up-down symmetry
{parent=Hexagonal convection amplitude equations}
{title2=$\lambda_\perp=\pm|\gamma|K|A|$}

In the <hexagonal convection amplitude equations>, a pure <convection roll> has $|A|^2=\mu_2/(3K)$ and $B=C=0$. The transverse diagonal linear growth cancels because self and cross cubic coefficients coincide. Broken up-down symmetry leaves the off-diagonal coupling $\gamma K A$, giving eigenvalues $\pm|\gamma|K|A|$ and an unstable orientation perturbation whenever $\gamma\ne0$. With zero quadratic coupling these directions are neutral at the retained order, so that degenerate case cannot be declared strictly unstable by this calculation.

= Convection roll
{parent=Thermal convection}

A <convection roll> is a circulating cell pattern whose ideal <velocity> and <temperature> fields are periodic across a chosen horizontal direction and independent of the coordinate along the <convection roll> axis. In a two-dimensional <stream function> representation, its <velocity field> is $(-\psi_z,0,\psi_x)$. <Rayleigh-Bénard convection> can select stationary or oscillatory <convection rolls>; a two-dimensional <convection roll> may be unstable to three-dimensional disturbances, a different <convection roll> orientation, or slow amplitude and phase modulations. Existence of a saturated <Landau amplitude equation> branch does not alone establish its stability.

= Oscillatory convection
{parent=Thermal convection}

<oscillatory convection> is time-dependent convection emerging through nonzero-frequency <overstability> or a <Hopf bifurcation>. At onset a complex-conjugate growth-rate pair crosses the imaginary axis. Travelling and standing <convection rolls> are distinct nonlinear possibilities, and their stability cannot be decided from the linear <angular frequency> alone.

= Travelling and standing convection rolls
{parent=Oscillatory convection}

= Traveling and standing convection rolls
{synonym}

At fixed orientation, opposite travelling amplitudes can obey $\dot Z_\pm=(r+i\omega_0)Z_\pm-(g|Z_\pm|^2+h|Z_\mp|^2)Z_\pm$. For $r>0$ and $g_r=\operatorname{Re}g>0$, a travelling branch has one nonzero intensity $r/g_r$ and is stable to its counter-propagating amplitude when $h_r>g_r$. A standing branch has both intensities $r/(g_r+h_r)$ and is stable within the pair when $g_r>h_r$ and $g_r+h_r>0$. These follow by linearizing the real intensity equations. Spatial modulation and other orientations remain separate stability tests.

= Stationary convection
{parent=Thermal convection}

= Steady convection
{synonym}

<stationary convection> has a time-independent <velocity> and <temperature> field, in the chosen fixed frame. Its onset from a conduction state corresponds to a real growth rate passing through zero. Stability of a resulting steady <convection roll> is a separate nonlinear question.

= Convective stability
{parent=Thermal convection}
{title2=$N^2>0$}

A stable stratification gives a restoring <buoyancy> force and positive squared <buoyancy frequency> for an adiabatically displaced parcel. An adverse <specific entropy> gradient can instead drive <convection>; other forces such as rotation can modify the full stability criterion.

= Convective overshoot
{parent=Thermal convection}
{wiki}

= Convective overshooting
{synonym}

Convective overshoot carries material from an unstable convecting region into an adjacent stably stratified region because its <momentum> does not vanish at the stability boundary. In stars this mixes material beyond the boundary determined by a local <stellar convective stability> criterion, changing stellar core masses and nuclear lifetimes.

= Double-diffusive convection
{parent=Thermal convection}
{wiki}

Double-diffusive convection occurs when two properties, such as <temperature> and composition, affect <buoyancy> but diffuse at different rates. A stabilizing composition gradient can oppose a destabilizing thermal gradient and change the form of heat transport.

= Thermosolutal convection
{parent=Double-diffusive convection}

<Thermosolutal convection> is <double-diffusive convection> driven by temperature and concentration gradients. Their buoyancy contributions can oppose one another, while unequal <thermal diffusivity> and <solutal diffusivity> allow steady, oscillatory and finite-amplitude states. For a layer heated from below with a stabilizing concentration gradient, thermal buoyancy is destabilizing and solutal buoyancy restoring.

= Double-diffusive overflow reservoir
{parent=Thermosolutal convection}
{title2=$\theta_s=C(1-\theta_s)^{10/3}/(1-\eta_s)^2$}

A steady well-mixed hot salty reservoir receives volume flux $Q$ and loses the same flux over a weir. Heat and salt balances are $Q(T_i-T)=AF_T$ and $Q(S_i-S)=AF_S$. If $\beta F_S/(\alpha F_T)=R_F$ and $\alpha F_T=b(\alpha\Delta T)^{4/3}/R_\rho^2$, define $R_0=\beta(S_i-S_\infty)/[\alpha(T_i-T_\infty)]$. Then $\eta_s=(R_F/R_0)\theta_s$ and $C=Ab[\alpha(T_i-T_\infty)]^{1/3}/(Q R_0^2)$. For $R_0>1$ and $0\leq R_F<1$ the interface is statically stable while preferential heat transfer can drive <double-diffusive convection>.

= Post-shutdown double-diffusive reservoir cooling
{parent=Double-diffusive overflow reservoir}
{title2=$\eta=q\theta,\quad q=R_F/R_0$}

After inflow and overflow cease, a fixed-volume reservoir initially in the preceding steady state obeys $\eta=q\theta$ and $\dot\theta=k(1-\theta)^{10/3}/(1-q\theta)^2$, with $k=Ab[\alpha(T_i-T_\infty)]^{1/3}/(V R_0^2)$. Integrating gives $k(t-t_0)=G(\theta)-G(\theta_s)$, where $G=3(1-q)^2(1-\theta)^{-7/3}/7+3q(1-q)(1-\theta)^{-4/3}/2+3q^2(1-\theta)^{-1/3}$. For $q<1$, heat contrast tends to zero only at infinite time, while a nonzero salt contrast remains.

= Lorenz model of thermosolutal convection
{c}
{parent=Thermosolutal convection}
{title2=$a,b,c,d,e$}

A five-mode <Galerkin method> model retains the roll velocity $a$, its correlated temperature and concentration modes $b,d$, and the mean-profile distortions $c,e$. Its parameters include the <Prandtl number> $\sigma$, diffusivity ratio $\tau$, thermal forcing $r$, stabilizing solutal forcing $r_s$, and a positive geometric decay ratio $\varpi$. The velocity equation is $\dot a=\sigma(-a+rb-r_sd)$, while the scalar equations are $\dot b=-b+a(1-c)$, $\dot c=\varpi(-c+ab)$, $\dot d=-\tau d+a(1-e)$ and $\dot e=\varpi(-\tau e+ad)$. The solutal forcing is normalized using the thermal diffusion scale, so its steady stabilizing effect is $r_s/\tau$.

= Oscillatory threshold of a thermosolutal Lorenz model
{parent=Lorenz model of thermosolutal convection}
{title2=$r_H=1+\tau(\sigma+1+\tau)/\sigma+r_s(\sigma+\tau)/(\sigma+1)$}

Linearization of the <Lorenz model of thermosolutal convection> at conduction yields $(\lambda+\sigma)(\lambda+1)(\lambda+\tau)-\sigma r(\lambda+\tau)+\sigma r_s(\lambda+1)=0$. A <Hopf bifurcation> requires the cubic coefficient identity $a_3=a_1a_2$ and positive frequency squared. Its threshold is the stated $r_H$, with $\omega^2=\sigma r_s(1-\tau)/(\sigma+1)-\tau^2>0$. If $r_s=\beta^2\tau$ and $\delta=\sigma/(1+\sigma)$, then $r_H=1+\tau/\delta+\beta^2\tau\delta+O(\tau^2)$. Comparing with the <steady fold of a thermosolutal Lorenz model> gives a leading nonnegative difference $\tau(\beta\delta-1)^2/\delta$ for $\beta\ge0$.

= Steady fold of a thermosolutal Lorenz model
{parent=Lorenz model of thermosolutal convection}
{title2=$r_{\rm min}=(\sqrt{r_s\tau}+\sqrt{1-\tau^2})^2$}

A nonzero steady <Lorenz model of thermosolutal convection> branch obeys $r(x)=(1+x)+r_s\tau(1+x)/(\tau^2+x)$, with $x=a^2$. For $0<\tau<1$, it has an interior <saddle-node bifurcation> precisely when $r_s>\tau^3/(1-\tau^2)$. Differentiating gives $x_*=-\tau^2+\sqrt{r_s\tau(1-\tau^2)}$ and the stated minimum. The weaker-versus-stronger threshold distinction matters: a sufficient bound need not be the sharp subcriticality condition. Steady-branch existence alone does not decide oscillatory stability.

= Layered convection
{parent=Double-diffusive convection}

Layered convection consists of mixed convecting layers separated by more weakly transporting interfaces. In a giant planet, composition gradients can support such interfaces and reduce heat loss, contributing to <delayed cooling of an inflated giant planet>.

= Buoyancy
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{title2=$F_b$}
{wiki}

= Buoyant force
{synonym}

<Buoyancy> is the resultant upward <force> exerted by a resting <fluid> on an immersed body in a downward <gravitational field>. The <fluid> pushes inward, perpendicular to each part of the body's surface. Because <hydrostatic pressure> increases with depth, the upward push from below exceeds the downward push from above. Opposite horizontal <forces> cancel; <buoyancy> is the remaining vertical <force>. A sinking body still receives this upward support. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/14-4-archimedes-principle-and-buoyancy[OpenStax, University Physics, §14.4: origin of buoyancy]{ref}

More precisely, <pressure> $p$ on a small surface of <area> $dA$ with outward unit <normal vector> $\mathbf n$ exerts
$$
d\mathbf F=-p\mathbf n\,dA.
$$
The minus sign says the <force> points into the body. On a <cube>, the top <force> points down, the bottom <force> points up, and the side <forces> cancel in pairs because opposite sides have the same <pressure> at each depth. This is a surface <traction>; <gravity> on the body itself is a separate <body force>. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/2-016-hydrodynamics-13-012-fall-2005/dc299d2ff05553b3515dccc23fc0f7d2_2005reading1.pdf[MIT, A. Techet, Hydrodynamics reading 1, p. 5: pressure traction]{ref}

For a computed example, stipulate resting <water> with constant <mass density> $\rho_f=1000\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$ and uniform <gravitational acceleration> $g=9.81\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$. A fully immersed <cube> has side $L=0.10\ \mathrm m$, top depth $h_t=0.20\ \mathrm m$ and bottom depth $h_b=0.30\ \mathrm m$. Each horizontal face has <area> $A=L^2=0.010\ \mathrm{m^2}$, and its <volume> is $V=L^3=0.001\ \mathrm{m^3}$. Its top and bottom <gauge pressures> are
$$
p_{g,t}=\rho_fgh_t=1962\ \mathrm{Pa},\qquad
p_{g,b}=\rho_fgh_b=2943\ \mathrm{Pa}.
$$
The corresponding <forces> relative to the common surface <pressure> are $p_{g,t}A=19.62\ \mathrm N$ downward and $p_{g,b}A=29.43\ \mathrm N$ upward. Therefore the net <buoyancy> is
$$
F_b=(p_{g,b}-p_{g,t})A=9.81\ \mathrm N.
$$
Using absolute <pressures> adds the same $p_{\mathrm{surface}}A$ to both face <forces>, leaving their difference unchanged. Zero <gauge pressure> at the surface does not mean zero absolute <pressure>. Increasing both depths by the same amount also leaves <buoyancy> unchanged in this constant-<mass density> model.

\Image[hydrostatic-pressure-and-buoyancy.png]
{title=How a hydrostatic pressure difference supports an immersed cube}
{description=Left: a fully immersed <cube> of side $0.10\ \mathrm m$ has its top at depth $0.20\ \mathrm m$ and bottom at $0.30\ \mathrm m$. The inward <pressure> <forces> on its sides cancel; its horizontal faces have <area> $0.010\ \mathrm{m^2}$. The displayed face <forces> use <gauge pressure>, whose common surface offset cancels in the net $9.81\ \mathrm N$ upward <buoyancy>. Right: <gauge pressure> increases linearly with depth, marking $1962\ \mathrm{Pa}$ at the top and $2943\ \mathrm{Pa}$ at the bottom.}

<Archimedes' principle> turns this calculation into a rule for bodies of any shape. It explains <floating equilibrium> and lets us infer an immersed body's <volume> from the difference between its <force> from <gravity> and the <force> needed to support it. In particular, <buoyancy> depends on the displaced <volume> and the surrounding <mass density>, whereas the body's own <mass density> decides how its <force> from <gravity> compares with that support.

= Buoyancy gradient
{parent=Buoyancy}
{title2=$\nabla b$}

The spatial <gradient> of <buoyancy> determines the buoyancy anomaly produced by a small parcel <displacement>: $b'=-\boldsymbol\xi\cdot\nabla\bar b$. Its vertical component is the squared <buoyancy frequency> in a stably stratified <Boussinesq approximation>. A horizontal component enters <thermal wind> and the propagation of a <boundary Rossby wave>.

= Magnetic buoyancy
{parent=Buoyancy}
{title2=$p_{\rm in}+B^2/(2\mu_0)=p_{\rm out}$}

<Magnetic buoyancy> is the tendency of magnetized fluid to rise when <magnetic pressure> support reduces its density relative to its surroundings. For a thin flux concentration in lateral balance, $p_{\rm in}+B^2/(2\mu_0)=p_{\rm out}$. At equal <temperature> and composition an ideal-gas equation of state then gives $\rho_{\rm in}<\rho_{\rm out}$, producing an upward buoyant force. Thermal exchange, <magnetic tension> and stable stratification modify this simple argument. In a <solar dynamo>, buoyant toroidal flux can emerge as tilted bipolar active regions.

= Archimedes' principle
{c}
{parent=Buoyancy}
{title2=$F_b=\rho_fgV_{\mathrm{displaced}}$}
{wiki}

<Archimedes' principle> states that <buoyancy> equals the downward <gravitational weight>, or weight, of the displaced <fluid>. In a resting <fluid> of constant <mass density> $\rho_f$ under uniform <gravitational acceleration> $g$, its magnitude is
$$
F_b=\rho_fgV_{\mathrm{displaced}}.
$$
Here $V_{\mathrm{displaced}}$ is the <volume> from which the body excludes the <fluid>: the whole external <volume> for a fully immersed solid body, and just the immersed <volume> for a partly immersed one. The body's own <mass> does not enter this <buoyancy> formula. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/14-4-archimedes-principle-and-buoyancy[OpenStax, University Physics, §14.4: Archimedes' principle]{ref}

A short proof outline for a fully immersed body is to imagine replacing it by exactly the <fluid> it displaces. The surrounding <hydrostatic pressure> is unchanged. That replacement <fluid> is at rest, so its upward <pressure> <force> must balance its downward <force> from <gravity>. The original body receives the same inward <pressure> <forces> on the same closed surface, hence the same upward <buoyancy>.

Equivalently, let $\Omega$ be the displaced region, $\partial\Omega$ its closed surface and $\mathbf e_z$ the upward unit direction. The <divergence theorem>, applied component by component to the <surface integral>, and <hydrostatic pressure> give
$$
\mathbf F_b=-\int_{\partial\Omega}p\mathbf n\,dA
=-\int_\Omega\nabla p\,dV
=\rho_fgV_{\mathrm{displaced}}\mathbf e_z.
$$
This also explains why horizontal <forces> cancel even for an irregular shape. A spatially constant surface <pressure> contributes zero to the closed-surface <integral>. If the <mass density> varies with depth, replace $\rho_fV_{\mathrm{displaced}}$ by the <mass> $\int_\Omega\rho_f\,dV$ of the displaced <fluid>.

The closed-surface argument assumes that the <fluid> applies <pressure> over the entire boundary of a fully immersed body. A body sealing against a tank bottom may have no <fluid> beneath it; then the lower-face <pressure> <force> used in the proof is absent, and a supporting contact <force> must be treated separately. For partial immersion, the usual <buoyancy> formula assumes the exposed surface receives the common atmospheric <pressure>, neglects the much smaller <buoyancy> from the air, and neglects <surface tension>.

For the $0.10\ \mathrm m$ <cube> in the stipulated <water>, full immersion displaces $0.001\ \mathrm{m^3}$, so $F_b=1000\times9.81\times0.001=9.81\ \mathrm N$. If its uniform <mass density> is $600\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, its <mass> is $m=600\times0.001=0.600\ \mathrm{kg}$ and its downward <force> from <gravity> is $mg=5.886\ \mathrm N$. To hold it fully immersed at rest requires an additional downward <force>
$$
F_{\mathrm{hold}}=F_b-mg=9.81-5.886=3.924\ \mathrm N.
$$
On release from rest it initially moves upward; its eventual <floating equilibrium> has a smaller displaced <volume>. A same-size <cube> of uniform <mass density> $1200\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$ has <mass> $1.200\ \mathrm{kg}$ and downward <force> from <gravity> $11.772\ \mathrm N$. Full immersion still gives $9.81\ \mathrm N$ of <buoyancy>, leaving an initial net downward <force> of $11.772-9.81=1.962\ \mathrm N$. Subsequent motion also involves <drag>.

= Floating equilibrium
{parent=Archimedes' principle}
{title2=$V_{\mathrm{displaced}}/V_{\mathrm{body}}=\rho_{\mathrm{body}}/\rho_f$}

A freely floating body at rest has no supporting contact or applied holding <force>. Neglecting <surface tension> and the <buoyancy> of air, its upward <buoyancy> balances its downward <force> from <gravity>:
$$
F_b=mg.
$$
For a body of uniform <mass density> $\rho_{\mathrm{body}}$ and total <volume> $V_{\mathrm{body}}$, its <mass> is $m=\rho_{\mathrm{body}}V_{\mathrm{body}}$. Substitution into <Archimedes' principle> gives
$$
\frac{V_{\mathrm{displaced}}}{V_{\mathrm{body}}}
=\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{body}}}{\rho_f}.
$$
Partial immersion is possible when $\rho_{\mathrm{body}}<\rho_f$. A nonuniform body uses its mean <mass density> $m/V_{\mathrm{body}}$ instead; for a hollow body this <volume> must count the enclosed space that excludes <water>. This explains how the amount of displaced <water> can support a vessel whose walls alone are denser than <water>. https://openstax.org/books/college-physics-2e/pages/11-7-archimedes-principle[OpenStax, College Physics, §11.7: floating fraction and mean density]{ref}

For the $600\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$ <cube> in <water> of <mass density> $1000\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, the displaced fraction is $600/1000=0.60$: sixty percent of its <volume> lies below the surface. With a horizontal top and bottom, the immersed depth $d$ satisfies $V_{\mathrm{displaced}}=Ad$, so
$$
V_{\mathrm{displaced}}=0.000600\ \mathrm{m^3},\qquad
d=\frac{0.000600}{0.010}=0.060\ \mathrm m.
$$
Its remaining height is $0.040\ \mathrm m$, and the <buoyancy> is $1000\times9.81\times0.000600=5.886\ \mathrm N$, exactly balancing its downward <force> from <gravity>.

\Image[buoyancy-floating-and-sinking.png]
{title=Floating, neutral buoyancy and sinking for three equal-size cubes}
{description=All three <cubes> have side $0.10\ \mathrm m$ in <water> with stipulated <mass density> $1000\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$ and <gravitational acceleration> $9.81\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$. Left: the $600\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$ body floats with $0.060\ \mathrm m$ immersed and equal upward and downward <forces> of $5.886\ \mathrm N$. Middle: a fully immersed $1000\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$ body has <neutral buoyancy>, with both <forces> equal to $9.81\ \mathrm N$. Right: a fully immersed $1200\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$ body has downward <force> from <gravity> $11.772\ \mathrm N$ against upward <buoyancy> $9.81\ \mathrm N$, so release from rest gives an initial net downward <force> $1.962\ \mathrm N$.}

= Neutral buoyancy
{parent=Buoyancy}
{title2=$\rho_{\mathrm{body}}=\rho_f$}
{wiki}

<Neutral buoyancy> means a fully immersed body's upward <buoyancy> equals its downward <force> from <gravity>. For a body of fixed <volume> in a constant-<mass density> <fluid> under a uniform <gravitational field>, this occurs when its mean <mass density> equals the surrounding <mass density>. A $0.10\ \mathrm m$ <cube> of <mass density> $1000\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$ in the stipulated <water> has <mass> $1.000\ \mathrm{kg}$, so both <forces> are $9.81\ \mathrm N$.

Ideally it can remain at rest at any depth for which it stays fully immersed and clear of boundaries. Raising or lowering it changes the absolute <pressure>, but neither its displaced <volume> nor the difference between the vertical <pressure> <forces>. There is therefore no vertical <restoring force> toward a preferred depth in this model. <Neutral buoyancy> is a vertical <force> balance, not a claim about resistance to tilting or about a compressible body whose <volume> changes with <pressure>.

= Submerged weight
{parent=Buoyancy}

For a grain of <mass density> $\rho_p$ and volume $\mathcal V$ in a fluid of <mass density> $\rho_f$, its effective downward <force> is $(\rho_p-\rho_f)g\mathcal V$, after subtracting <buoyancy> from weight.

= Buoyancy perturbation
{title2=$b'$}
{parent=Buoyancy}

A buoyancy perturbation is the departure $b'$ from a prescribed basic buoyancy field. In a fluid with constant <buoyancy frequency> $N$, vertical velocity $w'$ changes it according to $(\partial_t+\mathbf U\cdot\nabla)b'=-N^2w'$.

= Buoyancy displacement variable
{parent=Buoyancy perturbation}
{title2=$b_x=-N^2\theta$}

= Scaled potential-temperature perturbation
{synonym}

In a radially stratified <shearing sheet>, one can normalize the scalar <buoyancy perturbation> by writing $b_x=-N^2\theta$ and $D_t\theta=u_x+\xi\nabla^2\theta$. With this convention $\theta$ has dimensions of length, rather than <temperature>; $\xi$ is <thermal diffusivity>.

= Isopycnal
{parent=Buoyancy}
{wiki}

An isopycnal is a surface of constant <mass density>. In a <Boussinesq approximation>, it is equivalently a surface of constant buoyancy.

\Include[hydrodynamic-stability]{parent=Fluid mechanics}

= Barotropic fluid
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

= Barotropic
{synonym}

A barotropic fluid has pressure determined by density alone, $P=P(\rho)$, so pressure and density surfaces coincide in hydrostatic equilibrium.

= Barotropic cylindrical rotation theorem
{parent=Barotropic fluid}
{title2=$\partial_z\Omega^2=0$}

= Poincaré-Wavre theorem
{synonym}
{c}

In a steady axisymmetric <barotropic fluid> with purely azimuthal velocity $r\Omega\mathbf e_\phi$ and a conservative body force, the <barotropic enthalpy function> obeys $\nabla(w+\Phi)=r\Omega^2\mathbf e_r$. Taking its curl yields the displayed cylindrical rotation law. The first integral is $w+\Phi-\int r\Omega^2dr=C$ in a connected fluid region. Baroclinic pressure and density need not satisfy this conclusion.

= Barotropic spherical accretion equation
{parent=Barotropic fluid}
{title2=$(u-c_s^2/u)u^{\prime}=2c_s^2/r-\Phi^{\prime}$}

Steady spherical <continuity equation> gives $r^2\rho u=\text{constant}$. Combining this with <Euler equations for an inviscid fluid> and $c_s^2=dp/d\rho$ gives the displayed equation. Smooth transonic passage requires $u^2=c_s^2$ and $\Phi^{\prime}=2c_s^2/r$ simultaneously. The <barotropic enthalpy function> gives the integral $u^2/2+h+\Phi=\text{constant}$.

= Polytropic accretion in a mixed inverse-power potential
{parent=Barotropic spherical accretion equation}
{title2=$m c_\infty^2R_s^2-\frac{2m-3}{4}\mu R_s-\frac{2m-1}{2}\lambda=0$}

For $p=K\rho^{1+1/m}$, the <barotropic enthalpy function> is $mc_s^2$. In $\Phi=-\lambda/R^2-\mu/R$, a regular <sonic point> obeys $c_{s,s}^2=\lambda/R_s^2+\mu/(2R_s)$, and its Bernoulli integral yields the displayed quadratic. For $K,\lambda,\mu>0$ and the usual positive polytropic index, its unique positive root exists precisely when $m>1/2$. The <mass accretion rate> is $4\pi R_s^2\rho_\infty c_{s,s}(c_{s,s}^2/c_\infty^2)^m$. If indices with $0<1+1/m<1$ are admitted, $m<-1$ also permits a positive sonic root; negative compressibility indices do not.

= Critical-point slope of barotropic spherical accretion
{parent=Barotropic spherical accretion equation}
{title2=$C=\rho\,d(c_s^2)/d\rho$}

At a regular <sonic point>, differentiating the vanishing coefficient and numerator yields $(2+C/c_s^2)(u^{\prime})^2+4Cu^{\prime}/(ru)+(4C+2c_s^2)/r^2+\Phi^{\prime\prime}=0$. A real slope compatible with the desired branch is needed in addition to the sonic equalities. For constant <sound speed> this reduces to $(u^{\prime})^2=-c_s^2/r^2-\Phi^{\prime\prime}/2$.

= Barotropic enthalpy function
{parent=Barotropic fluid}
{title2=$h(\rho)=\int_{\rho_1}^\rho \frac{dp/d\rho^{\prime}}{\rho^{\prime}}d\rho^{\prime}$}

= Barotropic pressure potential
{synonym}

This <barotropic pressure potential> obeys $\nabla h=\nabla p/\rho$ for a <barotropic fluid>. It gives the <pressure> contribution to a steady <Bernoulli equation>. In isentropic thermodynamics it agrees with <specific enthalpy> up to a constant; for an externally maintained isothermal ideal gas it is $c_s^2\log\rho$ and is not the constant thermodynamic enthalpy at fixed temperature.

= Centrifugal potential of cylindrical rotation
{parent=Barotropic fluid}
{title2=$\Psi=\Phi-\int_0^R R'\Omega^2(R')\,dR'$}

For <angular velocity> depending only on cylindrical radius, the centrifugal acceleration is a <gradient>. Steady barotropic balance is $\nabla p/\rho=-\nabla\Psi$, hence <specific enthalpy> satisfies $h(\rho)+\Psi=\text{constant}$. On an invertible equation-of-state branch, <mass density> and <pressure> are functions of $\Psi$. Cylindrical rotation is essential to this scalar centrifugal-potential representation.

= Barotropic energy density
{parent=Barotropic fluid}
{title2=$\rho U^{\prime}-U=P$}

For a <barotropic fluid> with <pressure> $P=P(\rho)$, choose an energy density $U$ satisfying $\rho U'(\rho)-U=P$. The <continuity equation> then gives $\partial_tU+\nabla\cdot(U\mathbf u)=-P\nabla\cdot\mathbf u$. For an <isothermal equation of state> $P=c_s^2\rho$, one choice is $U=c_s^2\rho\ln(\rho/\rho_{\rm ref})$. A fixed reference density changes $U$ only by a conserved mass term. The same construction holds with <surface density> and vertically integrated <pressure>. This closure energy need not equal the microscopic internal energy of a gas maintained at fixed <temperature>.

= Barotropic magnetic energy equation
{parent=Barotropic fluid}

For a <barotropic fluid>, an energy per mass $e$ can be defined by $de/d\rho=p/\rho^2$. For the <isothermal equation of state>, $e=c_s^2\ln(\rho/\rho_0)$. Adding kinetic, gravitational and <magnetic energy> gives the ideal magnetic energy flux $\mathbf u(E+p+B^2/(2\mu_0))-\mathbf B(\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf B)/\mu_0$. This is a mechanical barotropic energy identity; it does not say that an isothermal gas is thermally isolated.

= Barotropic vorticity transport
{parent=Barotropic fluid}
{title2=$\partial_t\boldsymbol\omega=\nabla\times(\mathbf u\times\boldsymbol\omega)$}

For an inviscid <barotropic fluid> with only potential body forces, the <vorticity> $\boldsymbol\omega=\nabla\times\mathbf u$ obeys
$$
\partial_t\boldsymbol\omega=\nabla\times(\mathbf u\times\boldsymbol\omega).
$$
The pressure force is a <gradient> because $\rho^{-1}\nabla p=\nabla\int^\rho p'(s)\,ds/s$. A spatially uniform <specific entropy> makes a fixed-composition fluid barotropic. Merely having $Ds/Dt=0$ does not exclude <baroclinic vorticity generation> from spatial entropy gradients.

= Shear flow
{parent=Fluid mechanics}

A shear flow has a <velocity field> varying across adjacent fluid layers, giving a nonzero <shear rate>. A parallel example is $\mathbf u=U(z)\mathbf e_x$, with vertical shear $U'$. <Linear shear flow> has constant shear, while a <shear layer> concentrates the velocity change in a narrow region.

= Renovating shear flow
{parent=Shear flow}
{title2=$S(a)=\begin{pmatrix}1&a\\0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad a=\Lambda T$}

A renovating shear flow holds a planar <shear flow> fixed for an interval $T$, then chooses a fresh orientation independently of preceding intervals. Each interval acts on a <material line> by a rotated copy of the displayed <deformation gradient>. The orientation law must be specified: independent uniformly distributed orientations define the isotropic model, whereas a biased orientation law generally gives another <Lyapunov exponent>.

= Mean logarithmic stretching in an isotropic renovating shear
{parent=Renovating shear flow}
{title2=$\mu=\log(1+a^2/4)/(2T)$}

For independent uniform orientations in a <renovating shear flow>, the incident angle relative to each new shear is uniform even after earlier deformations. One interval stretches a unit <material line> by $\ell(\theta)^2=1+a\sin2\theta+a^2\sin^2\theta$. Write this as $A+B\cos(2\theta-\delta)$, where $A=1+a^2/2$ and $A^2-B^2=1$. The angular logarithmic integral gives $\langle\log\ell\rangle=\tfrac12\log[(A+1)/2]$. Add these independent logarithmic increments to get the displayed rate. At fixed shear rate, small $T$ gives $\mu\sim\Lambda^2T/8$ and large $T$ gives $\mu\sim\log(|\Lambda|T/2)/T$. The unique positive maximum is at $|\Lambda|T\approx3.9605826$, obtained from $\log z=2(1-z^{-1})$ with $z=1+a^2/4$.

= Log-stretch fluctuations in an isotropic renovating shear
{parent=Mean logarithmic stretching in an isotropic renovating shear}
{title2=$\operatorname{Var}(\log\lambda_N)=\frac N2\sum_{n\geq1}q^{2n}/n^2,\quad q^2=a^2/(a^2+4)$}

The logarithm of the stretch after $N$ independent uniform renovations is a sum of independent identically distributed bounded increments. For singular stretching $e^\gamma$, $\gamma=\operatorname{arsinh}(|a|/2)$, the increment has Fourier expansion $r+\sum_{n\geq1}(-1)^{n+1}q^n\cos(2n\theta)/n$, with $q=\tanh\gamma$ and $r=\tfrac12\log(1+a^2/4)$. Orthogonality proves the displayed variance. The <central limit theorem> gives an approximate central <log-normal distribution> of $\lambda_N$, not an exact description of extreme tails. For $|a|\ll1$, both the mean and variance of $\log\lambda_N$ are $Na^2/8$ to leading order. Direct expansion of fixed-power moments gives $\langle\lambda_N^p\rangle=\exp[Np(p+2)a^2/16+O(Na^4)]$; in particular mean stretch grows faster than typical stretch.

= Linear shear flow
{parent=Shear flow}

A linear shear flow has velocity that varies linearly in a transverse coordinate, such as $\mathbf u=-Sx\mathbf e_y$. Its velocity gradient and shear rate are spatially constant.

= Lagrangian displacement
{title2=$\boldsymbol\xi$}
{disambiguate=fluid mechanics}
{c}
{parent=Fluid mechanics}

= Fluid displacement
{synonym}

= Fluid Lagrangian displacement
{synonym}

A Lagrangian displacement maps each equilibrium fluid element to its perturbed position. Its time derivative is the velocity perturbation when the equilibrium is static.

= Eulerian perturbation of a fluid variable
{parent=Lagrangian displacement (fluid mechanics)}
{c}
{title2=$\delta q=q_{\rm perturbed}(x,t)-q_{\rm background}(x)$}

= Fluid Eulerian perturbation
{synonym}

The Eulerian perturbation compares field values at the same spatial location, rather than following a fluid element. To first order it satisfies $\delta q=\Delta q-\xi\cdot\nabla q$. Thus a field advected unchanged by individual elements can still have a nonzero Eulerian perturbation in an inhomogeneous background.

= Lagrangian perturbation of a fluid variable
{parent=Lagrangian displacement (fluid mechanics)}
{c}
{title2=$\Delta q=\delta q+\xi\cdot\nabla q$}

= Fluid Lagrangian perturbation
{synonym}

The Lagrangian perturbation compares a variable on the same moving fluid element before and after a displacement. Taylor expansion of the background value at the displaced position gives the displayed relation to an Eulerian perturbation. For an adiabatic <ideal gas>, $\Delta(p/\rho^\gamma)=0$ even when the equilibrium <entropy> varies spatially.

= Adiabatic fluid perturbation
{parent=Lagrangian perturbation of a fluid variable}
{title2=$\Delta s=0,\quad\Delta p=\gamma p\,\Delta\rho/\rho$}

An adiabatic fluid perturbation preserves the <specific entropy> of each displaced parcel. For a <perfect gas>, <mass conservation> gives $\Delta\rho=-\rho\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol\xi$ and the <adiabatic equation of state> gives $\Delta p=\gamma p\Delta\rho/\rho$. The corresponding <Eulerian perturbation of a fluid variable> is $\delta p=-\gamma p\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol\xi-\boldsymbol\xi\cdot\nabla p$. An isothermal equilibrium may still have adiabatic perturbations, because the thermodynamic constraint on displaced parcels differs from the equilibrium temperature profile.

= Eulerian and Lagrangian fluid perturbations
{parent=Fluid mechanics}

For a background scalar field $f$ and fluid displacement $\boldsymbol\xi$, the Lagrangian perturbation follows the displaced fluid element while the Eulerian perturbation compares fields at one fixed position. To first order,
$$
\Delta_L f=\delta f+\boldsymbol\xi\mathbin\cdot\nabla f.
$$

= Eulerian fluid perturbation
{c}
{parent=Eulerian and Lagrangian fluid perturbations}
{title2=$f_1=\Delta f-\boldsymbol\xi\cdot\nabla f$}

An Eulerian fluid perturbation compares a perturbed field with its equilibrium value at one fixed spatial point. It differs from the perturbation following a displaced material element: $\Delta f=f_1+\boldsymbol\xi\cdot\nabla f$. Linearizing a static fluid velocity gives $\mathbf v_1=\partial_t\boldsymbol\xi$.

= Surface density perturbation of a displaced uniform interface
{parent=Eulerian and Lagrangian fluid perturbations}
{title2=$\rho^{\prime}=\rho_0\eta_+\delta(z-H)-\rho_0\eta_-\delta(z+H)$}

Moving a uniform-density boundary produces a delta-function sheet in the Eulerian <mass density> perturbation. For a slab displaced vertically by $\eta_\pm$, $\rho^{\prime}=\rho_0\eta_+\delta(z-H)-\rho_0\eta_-\delta(z+H)$. <Poisson equation> then gives a continuous gravitational potential with derivative jumps fixed by those sheets.

= Lagrangian pressure perturbation
{parent=Eulerian and Lagrangian fluid perturbations}
{c}
{title2=$\Delta p$}

The <Lagrangian pressure perturbation> is the change in <pressure> experienced by a displaced <fluid element>: $\Delta p=\delta p+\boldsymbol\xi\cdot\nabla p_0$. For a free surface against fixed external <pressure>, $\Delta p=0$, rather than $\delta p=0$ at the original surface.

= Torricelli's law
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{c}
{wiki}

An ideal fluid issuing from a small hole a vertical depth $h$ below a free surface has speed
$$
v=\sqrt{2gh}.
$$

= Drainage time of a conical tank
{parent=Torricelli's law}
{title2=$T\sim\frac{2h_0^{5/2}}{5\epsilon^2\sqrt{2g}}$}

For a cone of half angle $\pi/4$ draining through a small circular aperture of radius $\epsilon$, the horizontal area at height $h$ is $\pi h^2$. <Torricelli's law> gives speed $\sqrt{2gh}$, so <conservation of mass> gives $h^2\dot h=-\epsilon^2\sqrt{2gh}$. Integration yields the displayed leading drainage time when $h_0\gg\epsilon$. The final layer $h=O(\epsilon)$ is outside the quasi-steady approximation and contributes only order $\sqrt{\epsilon/g}$, compared with the leading time of order $h_0^{5/2}/(\epsilon^2\sqrt g)$. This is an ideal inviscid estimate without a discharge coefficient.

= Density
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

= Mass density
{synonym}

Mass density is mass per unit volume.

= Linear mass density
{parent=Density}
{title2=$\mu=dm/d\ell$}

Linear mass density is <mass> per unit <length>, measured in $\mathrm{kg\,m^{-1}}$. A uniform string of mass $m$ and length $L$ has $\mu=m/L$; with constant <tension> $T$, its transverse <wave> speed is $\sqrt{T/\mu}$. <Mass density> instead measures mass per unit <volume>.

= Gas clumping factor
{parent=Density}
{title2=$C_e=\langle n_e^2\rangle/\langle n_e\rangle^2$}

The <gas clumping factor> measures unresolved inhomogeneity in electron <number density> within a chosen averaging region. Nonnegative <variance> gives $C_e\ge1$. At a uniform <temperature>, <thermal bremsstrahlung> scales with $\langle n_e^2\rangle$, whereas the <thermal Sunyaev-Zeldovich effect> scales with $\langle n_e\rangle$. A homogeneous <cluster distance from Sunyaev-Zeldovich and X-ray signals> inference then gives $d_{A,\mathrm{inferred}}=d_{A,\mathrm{true}}/C_e$, for the same geometry and temperature. Correlated temperature variations require additional weighted averages.

= Projected surface mass density
{parent=Density}
{title2=$\Sigma(\boldsymbol\xi)=\int\rho(\boldsymbol\xi,l)\,dl$}

= Surface mass density
{synonym}

Projected surface mass density is mass per unit projected area, obtained by integrating <mass density> along the line of sight. It determines the enclosed cylindrical mass used by a <thin gravitational lens equation>. It need not equal a physical disk's density, and its aperture mass differs from a spherical three-dimensional enclosed mass.

= Fluid pressure
{parent=Fluid mechanics}

Fluid pressure is the isotropic normal compressive stress in a fluid at rest.

= Cavitation
{parent=Fluid pressure}

Cavitation is the formation of gas or vapour cavities when a liquid's <pressure> becomes sufficiently low. A <journal bearing> model with <cavitation> must specify how a partly filled film and its boundaries behave, so periodic <pressure> alone cannot determine the flow there.

= Pressure gradient
{title2=$\nabla p$}
{parent=Fluid pressure}

The pressure gradient is the vector $\nabla p$ pointing in the direction of fastest pressure increase. The force density exerted by pressure on a continuum is $-\nabla p$.

= Drag
{disambiguate=physics}
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

= Drag force
{synonym}

= Drag
{synonym}

A drag force opposes an object's motion relative to a surrounding fluid and transfers momentum between them.

= Linear drag
{parent=Drag (physics)}
{title2=$\mathbf F_d=-\alpha\mathbf v$}

Linear drag opposes the <velocity> relative to the surrounding medium with magnitude proportional to its <speed>, where $\alpha>0$. Its <power> is $\mathbf F_d\cdot\mathbf v=-\alpha|\mathbf v|^2$, so it dissipates <kinetic energy>. Under a constant external <force>, the resulting <linear ordinary differential equation> relaxes exponentially on the <time> scale $m/\alpha$.

= Overdamped particle dynamics
{parent=Linear drag}

When momentum relaxes much faster than the imposed motion, <force> balance replaces the inertial equation: $\zeta\dot x=F(x,t)$ in a deterministic one-dimensional model. The <linear friction coefficient> sets the time scale. Thermal forcing, if included, gives <overdamped Langevin dynamics>.

= Linear friction coefficient
{parent=Linear drag}
{title2=$\zeta$}

The dimensional linear friction coefficient relates <drag force> and <velocity>, $F_d=-\zeta v$, and has units of <force> divided by speed. It differs from the dimensionless aerodynamic <drag coefficient>. For an isolated sphere in <Stokes flow>, the <Stokes drag law> gives $\zeta=6\pi\eta a$.

= Vertical ascent under linear drag
{parent=Linear drag}
{title2=$T=(m/\alpha)\log(1+\alpha u_0/(mg))$}

For constant downward gravitational <acceleration> $g$ and <linear drag> $-\alpha v$, the upward <velocity> solves $m\dot v=-mg-\alpha v$ with $v(0)=u_0\ge0$. Its first zero occurs at the displayed <time>. The dimensionless control variable is $\alpha u_0/(mg)$, and the limit $\alpha\to0$ gives $u_0/g$.

= Drag coefficient
{parent=Drag (physics)}
{title2=$C_D$}
{wiki}

The <drag coefficient> measures a <drag force> against a specified scale of <force>. Let $\mathbf U=\mathbf v_{\rm body}-\mathbf u_\infty$ be the body's <velocity> relative to the distant, undisturbed <fluid>, and let $U=|\mathbf U|>0$ be its relative <speed>. For <fluid> <density> $\rho$, <drag force> magnitude $F_D$ and reference <area> $A>0$, define
$$
C_D=\frac{F_D}{\tfrac12\rho U^2A},\qquad F_D=\tfrac12\rho U^2AC_D.
$$
The <drag coefficient> is a <dimensionless quantity>: the denominator has units of <force>. The reference <area> must be reported with it. For a <sphere> of radius $a$, the usual choice is the frontal <area> $A=\pi a^2$. Choosing its entire surface <area> $4\pi a^2$ instead divides the reported <drag coefficient> by four for the same physical <drag force>. Comparing coefficients without comparing their reference <areas> can therefore be misleading. https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/drag-coefficient/[NASA Glenn Research Center, Drag Coefficient, definition and Determining Value for Drag Coefficient]{ref}

For a rigid <sphere> in <steady flow> through an unbounded <Newtonian fluid> with constant <dynamic viscosity> $\mu$, <incompressible flow> and the <no-slip boundary condition>, the creeping-flow limit requires the diameter-based <particle Reynolds number> $\mathrm{Re}_d=2a\rho U/\mu\ll1$. The <Stokes drag law> gives $F_D=6\pi\mu aU$. Substitution into the definition with $A=\pi a^2$ gives the short derivation
$$
C_D=\frac{6\pi\mu aU}{\tfrac12\rho U^2\pi a^2}=\frac{12\mu}{\rho aU}=\frac{24}{\mathrm{Re}_d}.
$$
Thus the <drag coefficient> is neither necessarily constant nor bounded above by one. The formula containing $U^2$ still describes <linear drag> here, because the <drag coefficient> varies as $1/U$. The relation $C_D=24/\mathrm{Re}_d$ is the leading small-<Reynolds number> approximation, not a measured curve valid at every <Reynolds number>. https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/336L/Fluid/node142.html[Richard Fitzpatrick, Axisymmetric Stokes Flow Around a Solid Sphere, equations 10.126 and 10.129]{ref}

For a worked example, stipulate <dynamic viscosity> $\mu=0.10\,\mathrm{Pa\,s}$, <density> $\rho=1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, radius $a=10^{-4}\,\mathrm m$ and relative <speed> $U=10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. Within the stated <Stokes drag law> assumptions,
$$
\mathrm{Re}_d=0.002,\qquad C_D=12000,\qquad F_D=6\pi\times10^{-8}\,\mathrm N\approx1.88495559\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm N.
$$
Doubling the relative <speed> with all other parameters fixed gives $\mathrm{Re}_d=0.004$ and $C_D=6000$: the predicted <drag force> doubles to $3.76991118\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm N$.
