Falsely holding the <drag coefficient> at $12000$ would instead quadruple the <drag force>. Both <particle Reynolds numbers> remain small, so the comparison is within the creeping-flow approximation.

\Image[sphere-drag-coefficient-stokes-limit.png]
{title=Sphere drag coefficient in the creeping-flow approximation}
{description=The mathematical approximation $C_D=24/\mathrm{Re}_d$ is plotted only for small diameter-based <particle Reynolds number>, using frontal reference <area> $A=\pi a^2$. The two dots mark the worked example and its doubled relative <speed>. This is a creeping-limit curve, not empirical data or an extension of the <Stokes drag law> to all <Reynolds numbers>.}

The useful idea is to separate the <force> scale from the dependence on geometry and flow conditions. A <drag coefficient> can help compare experiments, but changing <Reynolds number> can change the coefficient itself; NASA Glenn explicitly stresses matching the relevant flow conditions. The elegant cancellation above also exposes a common mistake: a <drag coefficient> definition does not by itself impose a <quadratic drag> law.

= Quadratic drag
{parent=Drag (physics)}

Quadratic drag has magnitude proportional to the square of the relative speed and points opposite to the relative velocity.

For an <isotropic> model of a body of fixed shape, let $\mathbf U=\mathbf v_{\rm body}-\mathbf u_\infty$ denote its <relative velocity> through the distant <fluid>, with $U=|\mathbf U|$. A constant positive resistance $k$ gives
$$
\mathbf F_D=-k|\mathbf U|\mathbf U,\qquad F_D=kU^2,\qquad k=\tfrac12\rho AC_D.
$$
Here <density> $\rho$, reference <area> $A$ and <drag coefficient> $C_D$ are held fixed over the modeled range of relative <speeds>. The vector formula makes the direction explicit: the <drag force> on the body opposes its motion relative to the <fluid>, and vanishes at $U=0$. For a general body, orientation and other flow conditions can affect the <drag coefficient>; treating it as a constant requires a regime in which that is an adequate approximation. https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/drag-coefficient/[NASA Glenn Research Center, Drag Coefficient, Air Viscosity and Compressibility Effects]{ref}

Compare two distinct models at a chosen relative <speed> $U_0>0$. A <linear drag> model has $F_D(U)/F_D(U_0)=U/U_0$, while a constant-coefficient <quadratic drag> model has $F_D(U)/F_D(U_0)=(U/U_0)^2$. At $U=2U_0$ their <drag force> ratios are two and four respectively.

\Image[linear-versus-quadratic-drag-scaling.png]
{title=Normalized linear and quadratic drag models: doubling speed gives force ratios two and four}
{description=Each model is divided by its own <drag force> at $U_0$. The <linear drag> curve applies, for example, to a rigid <sphere> under the <Stokes drag law> assumptions while its <particle Reynolds number> remains small. The <quadratic drag> curve assumes constant <density>, reference <area> and <drag coefficient> over its modeled range. The comparison specifies neither an exact transition nor a single set of physical parameters that realizes both laws throughout the plotted range.}

This distinction matters when estimating <terminal velocity> or trajectories such as <descent from rest under quadratic drag>: doubling relative <speed> increases the resistance much more strongly in the <quadratic drag> model. An approximately constant <drag coefficient> can occur in another <Reynolds number> regime; its use must be justified there. Conversely, substituting the creeping-flow <sphere> coefficient $C_D=24/\mathrm{Re}_d$ into $F_D=\tfrac12\rho U^2AC_D$ recovers <linear drag>, so writing this general formula does not make the resistance quadratic. Neither approximation should be extended across all <Reynolds numbers>.

The distinction already appears in the original investigation underlying the <Stokes drag law>, read on 9 December 1850: article 42 identifies resistance proportional to relative <speed> and distinguishes it from a contribution proportional to the square of relative <speed>. https://mural.uv.es/daroig/documentos/stokes1850.pdf[George Gabriel Stokes, On the Effect of the Internal Friction of Fluids on the Motion of Pendulums, article 42, collected-paper reprint]{ref}

= Descent from rest under quadratic drag
{parent=Quadratic drag}
{title2=$v(t)=\sqrt{mg/\alpha}\,\tanh(\sqrt{\alpha g/m}\,t)$}

For positive downward speed, gravity and <quadratic drag> give $m\dot v=mg-\alpha v^2$, $v(0)=0$. Separation and the integral of $(1-s^2)^{-1}$ give the displayed approach to <terminal velocity>. The fallen distance is $(m/\alpha)\log\cosh(\sqrt{\alpha g/m}\,t)$. The limit as $\alpha\to0$ recovers $v=gt$ and distance $gt^2/2$.

= Quadratic-drag turning angle
{parent=Quadratic drag}
{title2=$\tan\theta=\mu|f|/(\rho_wC_wU)$}

For an ice mass in still water, approximate the wind stress by a fixed vector $a\mathbf e_x$ while retaining <quadratic drag> in the water. With mass per water-drag area $\mu=m/A_w$, water coefficient $b=\rho_wC_w$ and $f=2\Omega\sin\phi$, the steady balance is $a\mathbf e_x=bU\mathbf u+\mu f\widehat{\mathbf z}\times\mathbf u$. Resolving along the drift and perpendicular to it gives $a\cos\theta=bU^2$, $a\sin\theta=\mu|f|U$. Thus $b^2U^4+(\mu f)^2U^2=a^2$ and $\tan\theta=\mu|f|/(bU)$. The direction is right of the wind in the northern hemisphere and left in the southern hemisphere. This angle neglects intrinsic boundary-layer turning, ocean currents and contact forces; it is not a universal observed sea-ice turning angle.

= Mass per drag area
{parent=Quadratic-drag turning angle}
{title2=$\mu=m/A_w$}

The normalization $\mu=m/A_w$ has units $\mathrm{kg\,m^{-2}}$. It converts the <Coriolis force> into a force per water-drag area and allows comparison with wind and water stresses. <Icebergs> generally have much larger $\mu$ than thin <ice floes>, but their effective air-to-water drag-area ratio also matters. A geometric depth becomes a mass per area only after multiplication by density.

= Quadratic damping
{parent=Quadratic drag}
{title2=$\dot\omega\supset-c\omega|\omega|$}

A resistive velocity term proportional to the speed squared removes energy at rate proportional to $|\omega|^3$. Its derivative at zero velocity vanishes, so it does not appear in the <linearization of a dynamical system> at rest. Nonlinear energy arguments can still prove <asymptotic stability>.

= Pressure drag
{parent=Drag (physics)}

Pressure drag is the component of force produced by integrating pressure over a body's surface. For flow over topography $z=h(x)$, the horizontal force on the fluid is $-\overline{p h_x}$ to leading order.

= Stokes number
{title2=$\operatorname{St}$}
{parent=Drag (physics)}
{c}
{wiki}

The Stokes number compares a particle's drag stopping time $\tau$ with a characteristic flow time $T$: $\operatorname{St}=\tau/T$. In an orbiting disk, the natural choice is $\operatorname{St}=\tau\Omega$.

= Gas drag
{parent=Drag (physics)}

Gas drag is a <drag force> on a solid body due to its motion relative to surrounding gas. With <aerodynamic stopping time> $t_s$, the linear approximation gives $\dot{\mathbf v}|_{\rm drag}=-(\mathbf v-\mathbf v_g)/t_s$.

= Aerodynamic stopping time
{title2=$\tau$}
{parent=Drag (physics)}

The aerodynamic stopping time is the characteristic time on which <drag force> damps a solid particle's velocity relative to the surrounding fluid. Linear drag gives relative acceleration $-\mathbf v_{\rm rel}/\tau$.

= Suspension
{disambiguate=chemistry}
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

= Particle suspension
{synonym}

A particle suspension is a dispersed population of solid particles carried by a fluid. Its dynamics couple particle transport, <drag force>, gravity, and the compensating motion of the carrier fluid.

= Density inversion in a settling suspension
{parent=Suspension (chemistry)}

More suspended heavy particles above fewer particles puts denser fluid above lighter fluid. Such a <particle suspension> may develop <Rayleigh-Taylor instability> and overturning in addition to one-dimensional <kinematic sedimentation>. A rarefaction solution alone does not establish hydrodynamic stability.

= Heated particle-laden layer
{parent=Suspension (chemistry)}

A dilute <particle suspension> can supply both excess <mass density> and heat. To first order its <equation of state> is $\rho/\rho_0=1+\gamma\phi-\theta$, where $\phi$ is the <particle volume fraction> and $\theta$ is fractional <thermal expansion>. <Particle deposition flux> reduces $\phi$, while particle heating increases $\theta$. The competition can reverse the <reduced gravity> and create <unstable density stratification>.

= Particle volume fraction
{title2=$\phi$}
{parent=Suspension (chemistry)}

The particle volume fraction is the fraction of a representative mixture volume occupied by particles. Several particle species have fractions $\phi_i$, while the carrier-fluid fraction is $1-\sum_i\phi_i$.

= Bidisperse particle suspension
{parent=Suspension (chemistry)}

A bidisperse particle suspension contains two distinguishable particle populations, commonly with different sizes, densities, or <settling velocities>.

= Composition wave in a bidisperse suspension
{parent=Bidisperse particle suspension}

When two species share the same local settling velocity, their total <particle volume fraction> supports a nonlinear <kinematic wave>, while their relative composition is transported as a contact-like wave at the common particle speed.

= Settling velocity
{title2=$W$}
{parent=Suspension (chemistry)}

The settling velocity is a particle's vertical velocity relative to a stated frame. With height increasing upward, a sedimenting particle has $W<0$.

= Settling-velocity radius scaling
{parent=Settling velocity}
{title2=$W_s\propto r^2\ \text{or}\ r^{1/2}$}

For spheres with the same density contrast, balance submerged weight, proportional to $r^3$, against <Stokes drag law>, proportional to $rW_s$, to obtain $W_s\propto r^2$. With constant <drag coefficient>, <quadratic drag> is proportional to $r^2W_s^2$, giving $W_s\propto r^{1/2}$. The two radius laws require their corresponding <particle Reynolds number> limits.

= Particle Reynolds number
{parent=Settling velocity}
{title2=$\operatorname{Re}_p=2r\rho_fW_s/\mu$}

A <Reynolds number> based on particle diameter and relative speed in the carrier fluid. Small values justify <Stokes drag law>; a high-Reynolds-number regime with nearly constant <drag coefficient> instead gives <quadratic drag>. The drag law must be evaluated in the appropriate regime for every species.

= Particle deposition flux
{parent=Settling velocity}
{title2=$J_s=W_s\phi_b$}

= Deposition flux
{synonym}

For downward <settling velocity> magnitude $W_s$ and near-bed <particle volume fraction> $\phi_b$, a horizontal absorbing bed receives particle volume per area per time $J_s=W_s\phi_b$. A bed inclined at angle $\vartheta$ to the horizontal receives $W_s\phi_b\cos\vartheta$ per unit actual area, in the absence of resuspension or additional normal fluid motion.

= Stokes settling velocity
{c}
{parent=Settling velocity}
{title2=$U_s=2(\rho_p-\rho_f)ga^2/(9\mu)$}

= Stokes settling speed
{c}
{synonym}

\Image[stokes-settling-size-scaling.png]
{title=Sphere settling in water: terminal speed grows as radius squared while Reynolds number grows as radius cubed}

Take downward as positive and let <gravity> have magnitude $g$. For a rigid <sphere> of <density> $\rho_p$ in otherwise stationary <fluid> of <density> $\rho_f$, its weight minus <buoyancy> is $\tfrac43\pi a^3(\rho_p-\rho_f)g$. At <terminal velocity>, this balances the <Stokes drag law>:
$$
6\pi\mu aU_s=\frac43\pi a^3(\rho_p-\rho_f)g,\qquad
U_s=\frac{2(\rho_p-\rho_f)ga^2}{9\mu}.
$$
This signed downward <settling velocity> is negative for a buoyantly rising <sphere> and zero for equal <densities>. For fixed <density> contrast and <dynamic viscosity>, the submerged weight grows as $a^3$ while <linear drag> at fixed <speed> grows as $a$; their balance gives the <settling-velocity radius scaling> $U_s\propto a^2$. Meanwhile,
$$
\mathrm{Re}_d=\frac{2a\rho_f|U_s|}{\mu}
=\frac{4\rho_f|\rho_p-\rho_f|ga^3}{9\mu^2}.
$$
Thus doubling the radius multiplies the predicted <Stokes settling speed> by four and the <particle Reynolds number> by eight. This makes the formula useful for separating particles by size, while also providing its own check against extrapolation to large particles.

For a computed water example, take $\rho_f=1000\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, $\mu=1.00\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{Pa\,s}$, $\rho_p=2500\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, $g=9.81\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$, and $a=10.0\ \mathrm{\mu m}$. These are specified rounded inputs rather than a claim of exact water properties at every <temperature>. They give
$$
U_s=3.27\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}=0.327\ \mathrm{mm\,s^{-1}},\qquad
\mathrm{Re}_d=\frac{1000(3.27\times10^{-4})(20.0\times10^{-6})}{10^{-3}}=0.00654.
$$
The diameter is $20.0\ \mathrm{\mu m}$, and both the net downward weight and the upward <drag force> are $6.16\times10^{-11}\ \mathrm N$. At this <terminal velocity>, falling $1.00\ \mathrm{mm}$ takes $3.06\ \mathrm s$. At $a=20.0\ \mathrm{\mu m}$ the prediction is $1.308\ \mathrm{mm\,s^{-1}}$ with $\mathrm{Re}_d=0.05232$; at $a=50.0\ \mathrm{\mu m}$ it is $8.175\ \mathrm{mm\,s^{-1}}$ with $\mathrm{Re}_d=0.8175$, which is no longer a convincing small-<Reynolds number> case. The figure labels these as predictions of the <Stokes settling velocity> formula and shows where its <inertia> check fails.

The calculation describes the eventual <terminal velocity>, not the release transient governed by particle <acceleration> and possibly the <unsteady Stokes equation>. Walls, <hydrodynamic interactions>, departures from a rigid <sphere>, or a different surface condition require a different resistance. For sufficiently small particles, <Brownian motion> can obscure individual settling trajectories; the formula still specifies the gravitational drift within its assumptions. The balancing argument already appears in article 43 of https://mural.uv.es/daroig/documentos/stokes1850.pdf[Stokes's original investigation, collected-paper reprint]{ref}.

= Hindered settling
{parent=Settling velocity}

Hindered settling is the reduction or modification of particle settling by the displaced carrier-fluid backflow and by interactions with other particles. A simple dilute mixture model gives every species the same volume-averaged backflow.

= Kinematic sedimentation
{parent=Hindered settling}
{title2=$\phi_t+\partial_zj(\phi)=0$}

= Kinematic sedimentation theory
{synonym}

A one-dimensional <particle suspension> settling without particle diffusion or overturning obeys the <scalar conservation law> $\phi_t+\partial_zj(\phi)=0$. The upward flux is negative for downward settling. <Characteristic curves>, compressive <shocks> and <rarefaction waves> describe concentration transport rather than individual particle paths.

= Hindered-settling flux inflection
{parent=Kinematic sedimentation}
{title2=$f''(s)=V_0\alpha(1-s)^{\alpha-2}[2-(\alpha+1)s]$}

For <hindered settling> with normalized <particle volume fraction> $s$, upward coordinate and downward isolated <settling velocity> magnitude $V_0$, the <scalar conservation law> has <conservation law flux> $f(s)=-V_0s(1-s)^\alpha$. For $\alpha>0$, its <convexity> changes at $s=2/(\alpha+1)$ when this lies in the physical interval. This change distinguishes spreading <rarefaction waves> from compressing <characteristic curves>; a flux with an inflection can produce both a <shock wave> and a <rarefaction wave> in one transition. At $\alpha=0$ the flux is linear and the profile simply translates.

= Bidisperse kinematic sedimentation
{parent=Kinematic sedimentation}

A <particle suspension> contains two particle species with separate <particle volume fractions>. In an independent-settling model, each species obeys its own flux conservation equation, while a shared stationary deposit can couple the jump conditions through its maximum total <packing fraction>. Such independence is an imposed model; general <hindered settling> need not decouple species.

= Two-stage bidisperse batch sedimentation
{parent=Bidisperse kinematic sedimentation}

Initially, separate clearing fronts of fast and slow species coexist with a mixed deposition front. After the fast clearing front meets that deposit, the remaining suspension contains only the slow species. Its later <sedimentation shock> builds a pure-slow layer above the earlier mixed deposit. Final layer thicknesses and composition follow from separate particle-volume conservation.

= Deposit composition from sedimentation jump conditions
{parent=Bidisperse kinematic sedimentation}
{title2=$s(p_i-q_i)=W_iq_i(1-q_i)$}

For a <bidisperse kinematic sedimentation> model with normalized suspension concentrations $q_i$, isolated speeds $W_i$, and a stationary deposit with $p_1+p_2=1$, the upward deposit-front speed magnitude obeys $s(p_i-q_i)=W_iq_i(1-q_i)$. Adding gives $s=\sum_iW_iq_i(1-q_i)/(1-\sum_iq_i)$; substitution determines each deposited fraction. These are <Rankine-Hugoniot conditions> with a stationary compacted branch.

= Sedimentation shock
{parent=Kinematic sedimentation}
{title2=$U=[j]/[\phi]$}

A moving discontinuity of <particle volume fraction> in <kinematic sedimentation>. Conservation of particle <volume> gives the <Rankine-Hugoniot condition> $U[\phi]=[j]$ in a consistently oriented coordinate. A clearing front and a deposit front can both be <sedimentation shocks>. A stationary deposit uses zero particle flux; its compacted branch need not obey the suspension flux law.

= Sediment mass determines final deposit thickness
{parent=Kinematic sedimentation}
{title2=$h_c=[\phi_1(H-z_m)+\phi_2z_m]/\phi_{\max}$}

Particle <mass conservation> gives $h_c=[\phi_1(H-z_m)+\phi_2z_m]/\phi_{\max}$. Averaging concentration over the entire original container gives $\bar\phi=\phi_{\max}h_c/H$. This average differs from the concentration inside the deposit itself.

= Two-layer sedimentation with a compression shock
{parent=Kinematic sedimentation}

With upper concentration $a$ and lower concentration $2a$, the clearing, internal and deposit fronts have speeds $-u_s(1-a)$, $-u_s(1-3a)$ and $2u_sa$. If the lower two merge, the resulting deposit/upper-suspension front has speed $u_sa$. Final deposit thickness is $a(H+z_m)$ by <mass conservation>.

= Triple-shock sedimentation point
{parent=Two-layer sedimentation with a compression shock}
{title2=$(z_m,t_c,h_c)=\left(\frac{H(1-a)}{1+a},\frac{H}{u_s(1+a)},\frac{2aH}{1+a}\right)$}

For initial concentrations $a$ above $z_m$ and $2a$ below it, all three straight <shocks> meet when $z_m=H(1-a)/(1+a)$. The common time is $H/[u_s(1+a)]$ and height is $2aH/(1+a)$. Afterwards only a stationary deposit/clear-fluid boundary remains.

= Quadratic hindered-settling flux
{parent=Kinematic sedimentation}
{title2=$j(\phi)=-u_s\phi(1-\phi)$}

For downward settling speed $u_s(1-\phi)$ in upward coordinate $z$, particle flux is $j=-u_s\phi(1-\phi)$. The concentration-characteristic speed is $j^{\prime}=u_s(2\phi-1)$ and differs from particle speed. Since $j^{\prime\prime}=2u_s>0$, decreasing concentration with height creates a compressive <shock>, while increasing concentration creates a <rarefaction wave>.

= Parabolic-profile sedimentation shock
{parent=Quadratic hindered-settling flux}

For $\phi_0(z)=\phi_{\max}(1-z^2/h^2)/3$ on $0\le z\le h$, with clear fluid above and no imposed bed discontinuity, the first compression of the smooth profile occurs at $t=3h/(4V_s)$ and $z=h/4$. The nascent shock enters clear fluid with initial signed speed $-V_s$. Its downward speed subsequently decreases as the state behind it becomes more concentrated, until a boundary interaction changes the problem. This is characteristic breaking, not the time of initiation of a separately imposed packed-bed front.

= Settling rarefaction fan
{parent=Quadratic hindered-settling flux}
{title2=$\phi=\tfrac12[1+(z-z_m)/(u_st)]$}

If lower initial concentration $b$ is less than upper concentration $a$, the <entropy solution> expands into a fan with $\phi=[1+(z-z_m)/(u_st)]/2$. Its characteristic speeds range from $u_s(2b-1)$ to $u_s(2a-1)$. The fan can be clipped by a clearing or deposition <shock>.

= Early extinction of a settling rarefaction fan
{parent=Settling rarefaction fan}
{title2=$a_*=1-\sqrt{2/3}$}

For upper concentration $a$, lower $a/2$ and interface height $4H/5$, both curved fronts meet inside the fan only if $a\geq1-\sqrt{2/3}$. At smaller $a$, the deposition front reaches the fan upper edge first, then advances through uniform concentration $a$. The clearing front remains straight and final time is $H(1-3a/5)/[u_s(1-a)]$.

= Curved settling fronts within a rarefaction fan
{parent=Settling rarefaction fan}
{title2=$z_u,z_d$}

For upper concentration $a$, lower concentration $b<a$ and $L=H-z_m$, define $A=aL$ and $D=(1-b)z_m$. Once inside the <settling rarefaction fan>, clearing and deposition <shocks> obey $z_u=z_m-u_st+2\sqrt{u_sAt}$ and $z_d=z_m+u_st-2\sqrt{u_sDt}$. These curves apply only while their neighboring concentration remains within the fan.

= Last surviving characteristic of a settling fan
{parent=Curved settling fronts within a rarefaction fan}
{title2=$\phi_*=\sqrt A/(\sqrt A+\sqrt D)$}

If both <shocks> meet inside a <settling rarefaction fan>, $t_c=(\sqrt A+\sqrt D)^2/u_s$ and the last characteristic has concentration $\phi_*=\sqrt A/(\sqrt A+\sqrt D)$. It runs from the initial internal interface to the final deposit point. This construction requires $b\leq\phi_*\leq a$; otherwise a front exits the fan before final deposition.

= Settling shock speed
{parent=Quadratic hindered-settling flux}
{title2=$V=u_s(\phi_L+\phi_R-1)$}

The <Rankine-Hugoniot condition> for the <quadratic hindered-settling flux> gives $V=u_s(\phi_L+\phi_R-1)$, with lower and upper states labelled $L,R$. It includes a downward clearing front ($\phi_R=0$), an upward deposition front ($\phi_L=1$), and a stationary final deposit/clear-fluid interface.

= Dynamic viscosity
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{title2=$\mu$}

= Viscosity
{synonym}

= Shear viscosity
{synonym}

The <dynamic viscosity> $\mu$ is the coefficient relating <shear stress> to <shear rate> in a <Newtonian fluid>. For a <simple shear flow> $\mathbf u=(u(y),0,0)$,
$$
\tau_{xy}=\mu\frac{du}{dy}=\mu\dot\gamma,\qquad
[\mu]=\mathrm{Pa\,s}=\mathrm{kg\,m^{-1}\,s^{-1}}.
$$
Here $\tau_{xy}$ is the $x$ component of viscous <traction> on a plane with <normal vector> $+\mathbf e_y$. The <shear rate> $\dot\gamma=du/dy$ has units $\mathrm{s^{-1}}$. Thus <dynamic viscosity> measures the <stress> required to sustain a given rate of deformation. It is the shear coefficient; the separate coefficient for <volume> change is <volume viscosity>. https://www.homepages.ucl.ac.uk/~uceseug/Fluids2/Slides_1.pdf[UCL, Fluid Mechanics II, Forces in Fluids, slides 4–5]{ref}

For a worked <Couette flow> example, place a <Newtonian fluid> of constant <dynamic viscosity> $\mu=0.10\,\mathrm{Pa\,s}$ between parallel plates separated by $h=0.0010\,\mathrm m$. Hold the lower plate fixed and move the upper plate at $U=0.020\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. Assume <steady flow>, no streamwise <pressure gradient>, and the <no-slip boundary condition>. Then
$$
u(y)=\frac{Uy}{h},\qquad
\dot\gamma=\frac Uh=20\,\mathrm{s^{-1}},\qquad
\tau_{xy}=\frac{\mu U}{h}=2.0\,\mathrm{Pa}.
$$
The fluid pulls backward on the moving plate with <force> magnitude $F=\tau_{xy}A$, where $A$ is its wetted <area>. For $A=0.010\,\mathrm{m^2}$ this is $0.020\,\mathrm N$. Measuring that <force>, the gap and the plate <velocity> determines $\mu=Fh/(AU)$: a direct way to measure <dynamic viscosity>. The linear profile and opposing wall <tractions> are the standard plane <Couette flow> solution. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/2-25-advanced-fluid-mechanics-fall-2013/1a114d602956fa0dd328155f9b45f93d_MIT2_25F13_Couet_and_Pois.pdf[MIT, Couette and Poiseuille Flows, p. 1]{ref}

\Image[viscosity-couette-shear.png]
{title=Couette flow: a constant velocity gradient produces a constant shear stress}
{description=Blue arrows show the linear velocity profile between the plates. Orange arrows show viscous traction on the upper and lower faces of a fluid slice; reversing the surface normal reverses the traction. The slice has zero net viscous force although each face carries stress.}

The <velocity gradient> matters, rather than the <velocity> itself. Adding the same constant <velocity> to both plates and the fluid leaves $du/dy$, and hence <shear stress>, unchanged. In <uniform flow>, every layer translates together and $du/dy=0$. More generally, even a nonzero <velocity gradient> need not generate <viscous stress>: <solid-body rotation> has zero <strain-rate tensor>, as the <viscous stress tensor> example shows. Thin gaps amplify <shear stress> for a fixed plate-speed difference, making <dynamic viscosity> useful for predicting the <force> needed to move lubricated surfaces.

= Zero-shear viscosity
{parent=Dynamic viscosity}
{title2=$\mu=\int_0^\infty G(s)\,ds$}

For an integrable <relaxation modulus>, a small constant shear rate sustained from the remote past gives stress equal to that rate times the displayed integral, including any instantaneous solvent mass. For the linear <Oldroyd-B model> it is $\mu_0+G_0\tau$. A nondecaying elastic modulus would make this integral infinite rather than describe a fluid with finite steady viscosity.

= Kinematic viscosity
{parent=Dynamic viscosity}
{title2=$\nu=\mu/\rho$}

The <kinematic viscosity> is <dynamic viscosity> divided by <mass density>:
$$
\nu=\frac\mu\rho,\qquad [\nu]=\mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}.
$$
It measures the rate at which viscous transport smooths differences in <velocity>. For constant $\mu$ and $\rho$ in <incompressible flow>, dividing the <Navier-Stokes equation> by <mass density> makes the viscous acceleration $\nu\nabla^2\mathbf u$, where $\nabla^2$ is the <Laplacian>. https://www.damtp.cam.ac.uk/user/tong/fluids/fluids.pdf[David Tong, Fluid Mechanics, section 3.1.1, p. 61]{ref}

For example, stipulate two <Newtonian fluids> with the same <dynamic viscosity> $0.10\,\mathrm{Pa\,s}$ but different <mass densities>. In the same steady <Couette flow> as above, both have <shear stress> $2.0\,\mathrm{Pa}$, but
$$
\begin{aligned}
\rho_1&=1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}},&\nu_1&=1.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}},\\
\rho_2&=500\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}},&\nu_2&=2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}.
\end{aligned}
$$
The same <viscous stress> transports <momentum> through less <mass> in the second fluid, so its <velocity> responds faster to a given nonuniform viscous <force>.

To see the <diffusion equation> directly, consider an unsteady parallel <velocity field> $\mathbf u=(u(y,t),0,0)$ with constant material coefficients and no streamwise <pressure gradient> or body <force>. Its <convective acceleration> vanishes, and the <Navier-Stokes equation> reduces to
$$
\partial_tu=\nu\partial_y^2u.
$$
Balancing $u/t$ against $\nu u/h^2$ gives the <viscous diffusion time> $t_\nu\sim h^2/\nu$. For the $1.0\,\mathrm{mm}$ gap, the two fluids have scales $0.010\,\mathrm s$ and $0.0050\,\mathrm s$. This is a characteristic scale, not an exact settling time. For example, a perturbation of the <Couette flow> profile satisfying zero perturbation <velocity> at both plates is
$$
\delta u(y,t)=V\sin\left(\frac{\pi y}{h}\right)
\exp\left(-\frac{\pi^2\nu t}{h^2}\right).
$$
Substituting verifies the <diffusion equation>; its amplitude falls by a factor $e$ in $h^2/(\pi^2\nu)$. <Kinematic viscosity> is therefore useful for predicting how quickly a moving wall influences the interior, while <dynamic viscosity> gives the <shear stress> at a prescribed <shear rate>.

= Density-weighted mean kinematic viscosity
{parent=Kinematic viscosity}
{title2=$\bar\nu=\Sigma^{-1}\int\rho\nu\,dz$}

For a vertically stratified <accretion disk> with <surface density> $\Sigma=\int\rho\,dz$, the density-weighted mean <kinematic viscosity> is $\bar\nu=\Sigma^{-1}\int\rho\nu\,dz$. In an <alpha disk> with $\nu=\alpha p/(\rho\Omega)$, this becomes $\bar\nu=\alpha(\Sigma\Omega)^{-1}\int p\,dz$.

// Target: astrophysics.bigb

= Density-weighted viscosity of a disk
{parent=Kinematic viscosity}
{title2=$\bar\nu=\Sigma^{-1}\int\rho\nu\,dz$}

If angular <velocity> is independent of height, integrating the viscous stress $\rho\nu r\Omega'$ gives $\Sigma\bar\nu r\Omega'$. Thus the <surface density> weighted average, rather than an unweighted height average, enters <vertically averaged viscous disk equations>.

= Viscous stress tensor
{parent=Dynamic viscosity}
{title2=$\boldsymbol\sigma_{\mathrm{visc}}=2\mu D$}

= Viscous stress
{synonym}

The <viscous stress tensor> is the part of the <Cauchy stress tensor> associated with deformation rates. For an isotropic <Newtonian fluid> in <incompressible flow>,
$$
\boldsymbol\sigma=-pI+\boldsymbol\sigma_{\mathrm{visc}},\qquad
\boldsymbol\sigma_{\mathrm{visc}}=2\mu D,\qquad
D=\frac12\left[\nabla\mathbf u+(\nabla\mathbf u)^T\right],\qquad
\operatorname{tr}D=\nabla\cdot\mathbf u=0.
$$
Here $p$ is <pressure>, $I$ is the <identity matrix>, $\mu$ is <dynamic viscosity>, and $D$ is the <strain-rate tensor>. The convention is $(\nabla\mathbf u)_{ij}=\partial_j u_i$; $T$ denotes <transpose> and $\operatorname{tr}$ denotes <trace>. Across a plane with unit <normal vector> $\mathbf n$, the viscous <traction> is $\boldsymbol\sigma_{\mathrm{visc}}\mathbf n$. The <tensor> makes this rule work for any plane orientation. https://www.damtp.cam.ac.uk/user/tong/fluids/fluids.pdf[David Tong, Fluid Mechanics, section 3.1.1, equations (3.5)–(3.6)]{ref}

In the worked <Couette flow>, $\mathbf u=(Uy/h,0,0)$, so
$$
D=\frac{U}{2h}\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\1&0&0\\0&0&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
\boldsymbol\sigma_{\mathrm{visc}}
=\begin{pmatrix}0&2&0\\2&0&0\\0&0&0\end{pmatrix}\,\mathrm{Pa}.
$$
In particular, $D_{xy}=10\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$ while the <shear rate> is $20\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$; the factor $2$ in $2\mu D$ recovers $\tau_{xy}=2\,\mathrm{Pa}$. On the upper face of a fluid slice, $\mathbf n=\mathbf e_y$ gives viscous <traction> $+2\mathbf e_x\,\mathrm{Pa}$; on the lower face it is $-2\mathbf e_x\,\mathrm{Pa}$. Constant <stress> can therefore transmit a <force> between the plates even though its <divergence>, and the net viscous <force> on each slice, vanish.

Compare planar extension $\mathbf u=(ax,-ay,0)$ with <solid-body rotation> $\mathbf u=(-\Omega y,\Omega x,0)$:
$$
D_{\mathrm{extension}}=\begin{pmatrix}a&0&0\\0&-a&0\\0&0&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
\nabla\mathbf u_{\mathrm{rotation}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\Omega&0\\\Omega&0&0\\0&0&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
D_{\mathrm{rotation}}=0.
$$
Extension changes lengths and produces viscous <normal stresses> $\pm2\mu a$. Rotation preserves distances and gives zero <viscous stress>, despite its nonzero <velocity gradient> and <vorticity> $2\Omega\mathbf e_z$. For $a=\Omega=1\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$ and $\mu=0.10\,\mathrm{Pa\,s}$, extension gives $\sigma_{\mathrm{visc},xx}=0.20\,\mathrm{Pa}$ and $\sigma_{\mathrm{visc},yy}=-0.20\,\mathrm{Pa}$; rotation gives zero. A rotating fluid may still have a <pressure gradient> supplying its centripetal <acceleration>.

\Image[viscosity-strain-versus-rotation.png]
{title=Deformation produces Newtonian viscous stress; rigid rotation does not}
{description=Blue arrows show instantaneous velocity. The dashed square is a material patch at $t=0$; its orange outline at $t=0.65\,\mathrm s$ is obtained from the exact trajectories of planar extension and solid-body rotation, with $a=\Omega=1\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$. The marked corner makes rotation visible. Both motions preserve area, but only extension changes the patch's shape.}

For an isotropic <Newtonian fluid> in three-dimensional <compressible flow>, separate shape change from <volume> change:
$$
\boldsymbol\sigma_{\mathrm{visc}}
=2\mu\left(D-\frac{\nabla\cdot\mathbf u}{3}I\right)
+\zeta(\nabla\cdot\mathbf u)I.
$$
The first term is <deviatoric stress>, with zero <trace>; $\zeta$ is <volume viscosity>, also called <bulk viscosity>. For uniform expansion $\mathbf u=b\mathbf x$, $D=bI$ and $\nabla\cdot\mathbf u=3b$, so the shear contribution vanishes and the viscous <stress> is $3\zeta bI$. Thus <dynamic viscosity> and <volume viscosity> describe distinct responses, and <incompressible flow> removes only the volume-change term. https://notes.shreyasmandre.com/fluid-dynamics-notes/Ch2.html[Shreyas Mandre, Fluid Dynamics Lecture Notes, section 2.11.3, equation (2.49)]{ref}

= Reynolds number
{parent=Dynamic viscosity}
{c}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{Re}=\rho UL/\mu=UL/\nu$}

The <Reynolds number> $\operatorname{Re}=UL/\nu$ compares inertial and viscous effects. Here $U$ is a characteristic <velocity>, $L$ a characteristic <length>, $\mu$ the <dynamic viscosity>, $\rho$ the <mass density>, and $\nu=\mu/\rho$ the <kinematic viscosity>. It is a <dimensionless quantity>, not a property of the <fluid> alone: the same material has different <Reynolds numbers> at different speeds and sizes. Specify the chosen scales when reporting it.

The comparison comes from the <Navier-Stokes equation>. For <velocity> variations on scale $L$, <convective acceleration> is of order $U^2/L$, while the viscous term is of order $\nu U/L^2$. Their ratio is $UL/\nu$. Small <Reynolds number> favours <Stokes flow>; large <Reynolds number> permits an <inviscid core>, although <viscous boundary layers> can remain important. Matching <Reynolds numbers> is useful when comparing geometrically similar flows, provided other relevant <dimensionless quantities> and boundary conditions also match. https://uclnatsci.github.io/2021/Electromagnetism-Fluids-and-Waves/fluids/scaling.html[UCL, Scaling arguments, section 34.1.1]{ref}

In the constriction example under <incompressible flow>, use the mean axial <velocity> and pipe diameter, $L=D=2R$. Taking $\rho=1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$ and a stipulated <dynamic viscosity> $\mu=0.001\,\mathrm{Pa\,s}$ gives
$$
\mathrm{Re}_1=\frac{1000(0.20)(0.020)}{0.001}=4000,\qquad
\mathrm{Re}_2=\frac{1000(0.80)(0.010)}{0.001}=8000.
$$
Halving the radius at fixed <volumetric flow rate> doubles this diameter-based <Reynolds number>, because mean <velocity> increases fourfold. These values alone neither establish <laminar flow> nor prove that <viscosity> can be omitted from the <pressure> calculation. Increasing $\mu$ to $0.10\,\mathrm{Pa\,s}$ with the same imposed flow gives $40$ and $80$, but strengthens viscous effects; small <Reynolds number> does not justify <Bernoulli's principle>.

The name recalls Osborne Reynolds's 1883 experimental investigation of water motion and resistance in pipes. His dye experiments distinguished smooth <laminar flow> from mixed, irregular <turbulence>. https://cir.nii.ac.jp/crid/1362544419765899904[Reynolds, 1883, original paper abstract and bibliographic record]{ref} https://uclnatsci.github.io/2021/Electromagnetism-Fluids-and-Waves/fluids/scaling.html[UCL, Scaling arguments, section 34.1.4]{ref} There is no universal transition value applicable to every geometry and disturbance environment. https://www.damtp.cam.ac.uk/user/hkm2/PDFs/Moffatt_1977_GordonandBreach_Slogfdatohdt_149.pdf[H. K. Moffatt, transition discussion, p. 208]{ref}

= Mass diffusivity
{title2=$\kappa$}
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

Mass diffusivity is the coefficient multiplying the Laplacian in a diffusion law for a concentration or density anomaly.

= Vorticity
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}
{title2=$\boldsymbol\omega=\nabla\times\mathbf u$}

Use <vorticity> to distinguish the local spin of a <fluid element> from the motion of its center along a <pathline>. Circular <streamlines> can occur both with and without <vorticity>: the two <velocity fields> below even have the same <circulation> around the unit <circle>.

\Image[vorticity-and-circulation-fields.png]{title=Solid-body rotation and a potential line vortex: equal unit-circle circulation, different local vorticity}

For a continuously differentiable <velocity field> $\mathbf u=(u_x,u_y,u_z)$, <vorticity> is the <curl> vector
$$
\boldsymbol\omega=\nabla\times\mathbf u=\left(\frac{\partial u_z}{\partial y}-\frac{\partial u_y}{\partial z},\frac{\partial u_x}{\partial z}-\frac{\partial u_z}{\partial x},\frac{\partial u_y}{\partial x}-\frac{\partial u_x}{\partial y}\right).
$$
Each <partial derivative> divides <velocity> by distance, so <vorticity> has units $\mathrm{s^{-1}}$. In a planar <velocity field> independent of $z$, only $\omega_z=\partial_xu_y-\partial_yu_x$ survives. Positive $\omega_z$ points along $+\mathbf e_z$: the <right-hand rule> associates it with counterclockwise spin viewed from positive $z$ toward the plane. https://davidtong.org/pdfs/teaching/fluid-mechanics/fluids1.pdf[David Tong, Fluid Mechanics, section 2.2]{ref}

The local rotational <angular velocity> is half the <vorticity>, $\boldsymbol\Omega_{\rm spin}=\boldsymbol\omega/2$. To see the factor, split the <velocity gradient> $L_{ij}=\partial_ju_i$ into its symmetric <rate-of-strain tensor> $E=(L+L^T)/2$ and antisymmetric <spin tensor> $W=(L-L^T)/2$. A nearby displacement $\delta\mathbf x$ has relative <velocity> $L\delta\mathbf x$; its rotational part satisfies $W\delta\mathbf x=(\boldsymbol\omega/2)\times\delta\mathbf x$. This is a proof outline of the local spin interpretation. The <rate-of-strain tensor> can also change the angle of a particular <material line>, so that line's <angular velocity> need not equal $\boldsymbol\omega/2$.

For <solid-body rotation>, $\mathbf u=(-\Omega y,\Omega x,0)$ gives $\boldsymbol\omega=(0,0,2\Omega)$ and $\boldsymbol\Omega_{\rm spin}=(0,0,\Omega)$. Taking $\Omega=1\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$, the <vorticity> is $2\mathbf e_z\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$, although the <angular velocity> is only $1\mathbf e_z\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$. For the potential <line vortex>, $\mathbf u=K\mathbf e_\theta/r$, the planar <curl> is $r^{-1}\partial_r(ru_\theta)=r^{-1}\partial_rK=0$ wherever $r>0$. Its circular <pathlines> therefore coexist with zero local spin.

This distinction makes <vorticity> useful for recognizing <shear flow> as well as swirling motion; taking the <curl> of the <Euler equations> also removes a <pressure gradient> and leads to the <vorticity equation>. A historical landmark is Hermann von Helmholtz's 1858 paper on <fluid vortex> motion, an early study devoted to <vortex dynamics>. https://zenodo.org/records/1448878[H. Helmholtz, Über Integrale der hydrodynamischen Gleichungen, welche den Wirbelbewegungen entsprechen (1858)]{ref}

= Vorticity of a linear shear flow
{parent=Vorticity}
{title2=$\omega_z=-\gamma,\quad\Omega_{{\rm spin},z}=-\gamma/2$}

Use a <linear shear flow> to see why straight <pathlines> can still carry local spin. Faster layers pull past slower ones, and a small symmetric paddle can turn clockwise while its center travels straight ahead.

\Image[shear-flow-vorticity.png]{title=Straight particle paths in linear shear coexist with clockwise local spin and material-line deformation}

For the planar <simple shear flow> $\mathbf u=(\gamma y,0,0)$ with constant signed <shear rate> $\gamma$, direct <differentiation> gives
$$
\omega_z=\partial_xu_y-\partial_yu_x=-\gamma,\qquad\Omega_{{\rm spin},z}=-\frac\gamma2.
$$
With $\gamma=1\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$, these are $-1\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$ and $-0.5\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$: both signs indicate clockwise orientation under the <right-hand rule>. Yet a particle initially at $(x_0,y_0)$ follows the straight <pathline> $x(t)=x_0+\gamma y_0t$, $y(t)=y_0$. The <curl> measures differences of <velocity> between neighboring particles, rather than bending of one particle's <pathline>.

The <velocity gradient> has $L_{xy}=\gamma$ as its only nonzero entry. Its <spin tensor> supplies clockwise rotation, while its <rate-of-strain tensor> supplies simultaneous deformation. For a <material line> making angle $\alpha$ to the horizontal, the equation $\dot{\delta\mathbf x}=L\delta\mathbf x$ gives $\dot\alpha=-\gamma\sin^2\alpha$. Initially horizontal and vertical <material lines> therefore have <angular velocities> $0$ and $-\gamma$; their mean is $-\gamma/2$. At $t=0.5\ \mathrm{s}$ with $\gamma=1\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$, an initially vertical displacement becomes $(0.5,1)$ times its original length and has angle $\arctan 2\approx63.435^\circ$, a clockwise change of $26.565^\circ$. The plotted cross deforms; an actual paddle's response also depends on its shape and interaction with the fluid. The exact local spin statement concerns the <spin tensor>, rather than every tracer's orientation.

= Vortex line
{parent=Vorticity}

A <vortex line> is an <integral> curve of the <vorticity> vector field: its tangent is parallel to $\boldsymbol\omega=\nabla\times\mathbf u$. In a two-dimensional <incompressible flow>, these lines are perpendicular to the flow plane and the <vorticity equation> gives $D\omega/Dt=0$. Since material cross-sectional area is preserved, the flux of <vorticity> through a material <vortex tube>, equal to its <circulation>, is constant.

= Vortex
{disambiguate=fluid mechanics}
{parent=Vorticity}
{wiki=Vortex}

= Fluid vortex
{synonym}

A fluid vortex is a region in which <velocity field> circulates about an axis or center. <Vorticity> measures local rotation, while <circulation> measures the integral of velocity around a closed path. A <phase vortex> is instead a <topological defect> defined by winding of an order parameter; superfluid examples connect the two notions.

= Vortex dynamics
{parent=Vortex (fluid mechanics)}

The evolution of a <vorticity> distribution together with the <velocity field> it induces. A transport equation advances <vorticity>; diagnostic inversion recovers <velocity> from it and the specified <boundary conditions>.

= Nonrotating layerwise-two-dimensional vortex dynamics
{parent=Vortex dynamics}
{title2=$\partial_t\zeta+J(\psi,\zeta)=0,\quad\nabla_h^2\psi=\zeta$}

In the limiting model of infinitely strong stable <density stratification>, small <Froude number> and fixed vertical scales suppress vertical displacement. Each horizontal level evolves by <two-dimensional vortex dynamics>; $z$ labels independent copies of the horizontal <Poisson equation>. The leading normalized <Ertel potential vorticity> is $Q/N^2=\zeta$. This is a constrained asymptotic model: finite <buoyancy frequency> restores vertical motion and coupling, and arbitrarily short vertical scales invalidate the independent-layer limit. https://pordlabs.ucsd.edu/wryoung/theorySeminar/pdf14/McIntyrePV.pdf[McIntyre's discussion of potential-vorticity inversion] explains the limiting independent-layer picture.

= Two-dimensional vortex dynamics
{parent=Vortex dynamics}
{title2=$\zeta_t+J(\psi,\zeta)=0,\quad\nabla^2\psi=\zeta$}

For inviscid planar <incompressible flow>, write <velocity> as $(-\psi_y,\psi_x)$ and <vorticity> as $\zeta=\nabla^2\psi$. Taking <curl> of the <Euler equations for an inviscid fluid> removes <pressure> and gives the displayed transport equation, with $J(\psi,\zeta)=\psi_x\zeta_y-\psi_y\zeta_x$. The <Poisson equation> then recovers the <streamfunction> at each instant. A velocity tangent to the plane cannot stretch its perpendicular <vorticity> vector.

= Steady planar vorticity as a local function of stream function
{parent=Two-dimensional vortex dynamics}
{title2=$\Delta\psi=F(\psi)\quad\text{locally where }\nabla\psi\ne0$}

In a steady inviscid planar <incompressible flow>, <vorticity> is constant along <streamlines>. A regular <stream function> supplies a local transverse coordinate, so locally $\Delta\psi$ depends only on $\psi$. A global single-valued function needs the same value on every component of each level set. This can fail: $\psi=x^3-x$ gives the steady Euler shear $(u,v)=(0,1-3x^2)$ with constant pressure, but $\psi(0)=\psi(1)=0$ while $\Delta\psi$ is respectively $0$ and $6$.

= Line vortex
{parent=Vortex (fluid mechanics)}
{title2=$\mathbf u=\frac{\Gamma}{2\pi}\frac{\mathbf e_z\times(\mathbf r-\mathbf r_0)}{|\mathbf r-\mathbf r_0|^2}$}

= Point vortex
{synonym}

= Point vortices
{synonym}

An ideal straight <line vortex> in a two-dimensional <incompressible flow> has <circulation> $\Gamma$ and induces velocity
$$
\mathbf u(\mathbf r)=\frac{\Gamma}{2\pi}\frac{\mathbf e_z\times(\mathbf r-\mathbf r_0)}{|\mathbf r-\mathbf r_0|^2}.
$$
Its <stream function>, with $\mathbf u=(\psi_y,-\psi_x)$, is $\psi=-\Gamma\log|\mathbf r-\mathbf r_0|/(2\pi)$. Positive $\Gamma$ means counterclockwise <circulation>. In an ideal point-vortex description, the vortex position is advected by the regular velocity produced by the other vortices and the boundaries; its own singular velocity is excluded.

= Motion of two point vortices
{parent=Line vortex}
{title2=$\dot{\mathbf d}=(\kappa_1+\kappa_2)J\mathbf d/(2\pi|\mathbf d|^2)$}

Take vortex strength to mean circulation and $J(x,y)=(-y,x)$. Each <point vortex> moves with the <velocity> induced by the other, so for $\mathbf d=\mathbf x_1-\mathbf x_2$ the displayed equation holds. Since $\mathbf d\cdot J\mathbf d=0$, separation is constant; weighting the two velocities also gives $d(\kappa_1\mathbf x_1+\kappa_2\mathbf x_2)/dt=0$. Equal strengths at opposite points of a circle of radius $a$ rotate with angular speed $\kappa/(4\pi a^2)$.

= Image vortex at a plane wall
{parent=Line vortex}
{title2=$U=\frac{\Gamma}{4\pi b}$}

The <method of images> enforces impermeability at a plane wall by reflecting a <line vortex> across it and reversing its <circulation>. The two logarithmic <stream functions> cancel on the wall, which is consequently a <streamline>. For a vortex of <circulation> $\Gamma$ at $(0,b)$ above $y=0$, the image of <circulation> $-\Gamma$ at $(0,-b)$ induces velocity $(\Gamma/(4\pi b),0)$ at the real vortex. The signed drift parallel to the wall is therefore $\Gamma/(4\pi b)$.

= Line-vortex trajectory in a quarter-plane
{parent=Image vortex at a plane wall}
{title2=$r\sin2\theta=\text{constant}$}

In the quadrant $x,y>0$, a <line vortex> of <circulation> $\Gamma$ at $(x,y)$ has three images: circulations $-\Gamma$ at $(-x,y)$ and $(x,-y)$, and $+\Gamma$ at $(-x,-y)$. Their induced velocity is
$$
\dot x=\frac{\Gamma x^2}{4\pi y(x^2+y^2)},\qquad\dot y=-\frac{\Gamma y^2}{4\pi x(x^2+y^2)}.
$$
In <polar coordinates>, these equations give $\dot r=\Gamma\cot(2\theta)/(2\pi r)$ and $\dot\theta=-\Gamma/(4\pi r^2)$. Direct <differentiation> yields $\frac d{dt}(r\sin2\theta)=0$, so the trajectory obeys $r\sin2\theta=\text{constant}$.

= Hydrodynamic Biot-Savart kernel
{parent=Vorticity}
{title2=$\mathbf u(\mathbf x)=\frac1{4\pi}\int\frac{\boldsymbol\omega(\mathbf y)\times(\mathbf x-\mathbf y)}{|\mathbf x-\mathbf y|^3}\,d^3y$}

In an unbounded incompressible fluid with localized <vorticity> and no added harmonic background, velocity is reconstructed by $\mathbf u(\mathbf x)=(4\pi)^{-1}\int\boldsymbol\omega(\mathbf y)\times(\mathbf x-\mathbf y)/|\mathbf x-\mathbf y|^3\,d^3y$. Its multipole expansion relates <hydrodynamic impulse> to a dipolar far-field velocity.

= Hydrodynamic impulse
{parent=Vorticity}
{title2=$\mathbf I=\frac12\int\mathbf x\times\boldsymbol\omega\,dV$}

<Hydrodynamic impulse> per unit density is $\mathbf I=\tfrac12\int\mathbf x\times\boldsymbol\omega\,dV$ for localized <vorticity>. In unforced all-space <incompressible flow> its derivative vanishes when far-field fluxes vanish. For a spherical all-space limit, $\mathbf I=(3/2)\lim_R\int_{V_R}\mathbf u\,dV$; a dipolar velocity tail makes the distinction from a bare <momentum> integral important.

= Dipolar velocity field of a localized eddy
{parent=Hydrodynamic impulse}
{title2=$\mathbf u_{\rm far}=[3\mathbf n(\mathbf I\cdot\mathbf n)-\mathbf I]/(4\pi r^3)$}

A localized <vorticity> distribution with nonzero <hydrodynamic impulse> induces $\mathbf u\sim[3\mathbf n(\mathbf I\cdot\mathbf n)-\mathbf I]/(4\pi r^3)$. This <curl>-free exterior velocity can remain long-ranged even when the <vorticity> is localized. In a random ensemble it produces dipolar longitudinal and transverse correlation tails.

= Vortex tube
{parent=Vorticity}

A <vortex tube> is a bundle of vortex lines bounded by a surface to which <vorticity> is tangent. <Circulation> measures its <vorticity> flux. Stretching a nearly incompressible tube reduces its cross-sectional area and increases its <vorticity>. The <Burgers vortex> is a local model in which this concentration balances viscous diffusion.

= Enstrophy
{parent=Vorticity}
{wiki}
{title2=$Z=\langle\omega^2\rangle/2$}

<Enstrophy> measures squared <vorticity>: a local density is $\omega^2/2$, and its spatial integral or ensemble mean gives a global measure. In homogeneous <incompressible flow>, <viscous dissipation> satisfies $\epsilon=\nu\langle\omega^2\rangle$. <Vortex stretching> can produce <enstrophy> in three dimensions; <kinematic viscosity> destroys it.

= Mean enstrophy balance
{parent=Enstrophy}
{title2=$\dot Z=\langle\omega_i\omega_jS_{ij}\rangle-\nu\langle|\nabla\times\boldsymbol\omega|^2\rangle$}

For unforced homogeneous <incompressible flow>, the <vorticity equation> gives $\dot Z=\langle\omega_i\omega_jS_{ij}\rangle-\nu\langle|\nabla\times\boldsymbol\omega|^2\rangle$. <Curl> and full-gradient squared averages agree because the <vorticity> is divergence free and boundary divergences average to zero. In established high-Reynolds-number <turbulence>, slow integral-time evolution makes production and destruction nearly balance.

= Planar vorticity velocity kernel
{parent=Vorticity}
{title2=$K(z)=\pm z^\perp/(2\pi|z|^2)$}

= Planar Biot-Savart kernel
{synonym}

The <velocity> induced by planar <vorticity> is a <convolution> with this kernel, whose sign depends on the <stream function> and vorticity conventions. Its magnitude is $(2\pi|z|)^{-1}$ and its derivative is bounded by $C|z|^{-2}$. Splitting the convolution at radius $R$ gives $\|K*\omega\|_\infty\leq R\|\omega\|_\infty+\|\omega\|_1/(2\pi R)$. The same singularity yields a <log-Lipschitz modulus>, enough for a <Yudovich characteristic flow>.

= Uniform-vorticity circular flow in an annulus
{parent=Vorticity}
{title2=$u_\phi=V(4a^2/r-r)/(3a)$}

Circular streamlines and constant <vorticity> imply $u_\phi=\omega r/2+C/r$. Zero velocity at $2a$ and positive azimuthal velocity $V$ at $a$ give
$$
u_\phi=\frac V{3a}(4a^2/r-r),\qquad \omega=-\frac{2V}{3a}.
$$
The radial <Euler equations for an inviscid fluid> give $p_r=\rho u_\phi^2/r$, yielding outer-minus-inner pressure $(15-16\log2)\rho V^2/18$. The pressure difference is independent of circulation orientation.

= Vorticity cross-product identity
{parent=Vorticity}
{title2=$(\nabla\times U)\times v=(v\cdot\nabla)U-(\nabla U)^Tv$}

For differentiable vector fields, the displayed identity relates <vorticity> to the antisymmetric part of the <velocity gradient>. Together with the <curl of a cross product>, it converts the <adjoint linearized Navier-Stokes evolution> between component and rotational forms.

= Convective acceleration identity
{parent=Vorticity cross-product identity}
{title2=$(\mathbf u\cdot\nabla)\mathbf u=\nabla(|\mathbf u|^2/2)-\mathbf u\times\boldsymbol\omega$}

The local vector-calculus identity
$$
(\mathbf u\cdot\nabla)\mathbf u=\nabla(\tfrac12|\mathbf u|^2)-\mathbf u\times(\nabla\times\mathbf u)
$$
separates the convective <acceleration> into a kinetic-energy gradient and a <vorticity> cross-product contribution. Substitution into the steady <Euler equations for an inviscid fluid> yields the vorticity form of the <Bernoulli equation>.

= Hydrodynamical helicity
{parent=Vorticity}
{title2=$H_k=\int\mathbf u\cdot\boldsymbol\omega\,dV$}
{wiki}

= Kinetic helicity
{synonym}

= Hydrodynamic helicity
{synonym}

Kinetic helicity measures the alignment and linking of a <velocity field> and its <vorticity>. It is the volume integral of $\mathbf u\cdot\boldsymbol\omega$, with boundary conditions needed for its conservation.

The integral measures linkage and twisting of <vortex lines> through the <velocity> and <vorticity> fields.

= Kinetic helicity density
{parent=Hydrodynamical helicity}
{title2=$q=\mathbf u\cdot\boldsymbol\omega$}

The local density of <kinetic helicity> is $q=\mathbf u\cdot\boldsymbol\omega$. Conservation of its volume integral does not imply that $q$ is a materially advected scalar.

= Helicity vector of a solenoidal Fourier mode
{parent=Kinetic helicity density}
{title2=$\tfrac12\operatorname{Re}(i\hat{\mathbf u}^*\times\hat{\mathbf u})=H\mathbf k$}

For a transverse complex velocity amplitude $\hat{\mathbf u}$ and real nonzero <wavevector> $\mathbf k$, write $\hat{\mathbf u}=\mathbf a+i\mathbf c$. Solenoidality makes both real vectors perpendicular to $\mathbf k$, so $\tfrac12\operatorname{Re}(i\hat{\mathbf u}^*\times\hat{\mathbf u})=-\mathbf a\times\mathbf c=H\mathbf k$. The corresponding spatial average of <kinetic helicity density> is $-Hk^2$. Cross-product order determines this sign.

= Kinetic helicity conservation law
{parent=Kinetic helicity density}

For smooth inviscid <barotropic fluid> motion without gravity or magnetic forces, let $w$ be the <specific enthalpy> with $\nabla w=\rho^{-1}\nabla p$, and set $q=\mathbf u\cdot\boldsymbol\omega$. Then
$$
\partial_tq+\nabla\cdot\left[q\mathbf u+\left(w-\frac{u^2}{2}\right)\boldsymbol\omega\right]=0.
$$
This follows by dotting the <barotropic vorticity transport> equation with $\mathbf u$ and using the <divergence of a cross product>. A volume integral is constant when the resulting boundary flux vanishes.

= Material conservation of kinetic helicity density
{parent=Kinetic helicity conservation law}

The <kinetic helicity conservation law> implies
$$
\frac{Dq}{Dt}=\boldsymbol\omega\cdot\nabla\left(\frac{u^2}{2}-w\right)-q\nabla\cdot\mathbf u.
$$
Therefore $q$ is materially constant exactly when the right side vanishes. <Incompressible flow> together with constancy of $w-u^2/2$ along vortex lines is sufficient. In compressible flow, the density-normalized quantity satisfies $D(q/\rho)/Dt=-(\boldsymbol\omega/\rho)\cdot\nabla(w-u^2/2)$.

= Absolute vorticity
{parent=Vorticity}
{wiki}

Absolute vorticity is the vorticity measured in an inertial frame. In a frame rotating with angular velocity $\boldsymbol\Omega$, it is $\boldsymbol\omega+2\boldsymbol\Omega$.

= Planetary vorticity
{parent=Absolute vorticity}
{title2=$f=2\Omega\sin\phi$}

The planetary contribution to absolute <vorticity> is supplied by the rotating frame. Its local vertical component is the <Coriolis parameter> $f=2\Omega\sin\phi$. On a <beta plane>, motion with northward velocity $v$ changes this contribution at the rate $\beta v$.

= Absolute-vorticity flux tensor
{parent=Absolute vorticity}
{title2=$T_{ij}=u_i\omega_j-\omega_i u_j$}

For a flow satisfying <incompressibility>, this antisymmetric tensor writes advection and <vortex stretching> in conservative form. With its first index as transport direction, $(\nabla\cdot T)_j=\partial_iT_{ij}$.

= Radial vorticity conservation in a shearing sheet
{parent=Absolute-vorticity flux tensor}
{title2=$\partial_t\omega_x+\partial_iT_{ix}=0$}

When the only nonpotential force points radially, its <curl> has no radial component. The radial <absolute vorticity> therefore obeys a local flux <conservation law>, although <vortex stretching> can change it along a fluid trajectory.

= Relative vorticity
{title2=$\boldsymbol\omega$}
{parent=Vorticity}

Relative vorticity is the curl of velocity measured in the chosen rotating frame. Adding the planetary vorticity gives <absolute vorticity>.

= Circulation
{disambiguate=physics}
{parent=Vorticity}
{wiki}
{title2=$\Gamma_C=\oint_C\mathbf u\cdot d\mathbf l$}

= Circulation
{synonym}

Use <circulation> to summarize how much a <velocity field> runs along an oriented <closed curve> $C$, such as a <circle> around a <fluid vortex>. Its definition is the <line integral>
$$
\Gamma_C=\oint_C\mathbf u\cdot d\mathbf l,
$$
where $d\mathbf l$ is tangent to $C$ in the chosen direction. Reversing that direction reverses the sign of <circulation>. Its units are $\mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}$, because <velocity> is multiplied by a length. Counterclockwise <circulation> in the horizontal plane is positive when the chosen <unit normal vector> is $+\mathbf e_z$, according to the <right-hand rule>.

For <solid-body rotation> $\mathbf u=(-\Omega y,\Omega x,0)$, a counterclockwise <circle> of radius $R$ has constant tangential <velocity> $\Omega R$. Multiplying by its circumference gives
$$
\Gamma_C=2\pi\Omega R^2.
$$
For the potential <line vortex> $\mathbf u=K\mathbf e_\theta/r$, the same <circle> instead gives $\Gamma_C=(K/R)(2\pi R)=2\pi K$, independent of $R$. Stipulate $\Omega=1\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$, $R=1\ \mathrm m$ and $K=1\ \mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}$: both <velocity fields> have $\Gamma_C=2\pi\ \mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}\approx6.283185\ \mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}$, although their <vorticities> in the fluid are $2\mathbf e_z\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$ and zero respectively. On a <circle> of radius $2\ \mathrm m$, their <circulations> are $8\pi\ \mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}$ and $2\pi\ \mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}$. The comparison plot in <vorticity> shows this difference in radius dependence.

The bridge between <circulation> and <vorticity> is <Stokes theorem>:
$$
\Gamma_{\partial S}=\int_S\boldsymbol\omega\cdot\mathbf n\,dS.
$$
Here the <velocity field> must be continuously differentiable on a neighborhood of the entire spanning surface $S$ inside the fluid, and its boundary orientation must match the <unit normal vector> $\mathbf n$. For <solid-body rotation>, the disk is admissible and its <surface integral> is $2\Omega\pi R^2$. For the potential <line vortex>, the origin is singular and excluded from the fluid: a disk crossing it is not admissible. On an <annulus>, <Stokes theorem> instead gives $\Gamma_{R_{\rm out}}-\Gamma_{R_{\rm in}}=0$, with both <circulations> here measured counterclockwise; the induced inner boundary direction is clockwise. Nonzero <circulation> therefore does not imply nonzero local <curl> in the available fluid. https://davidtong.org/pdfs/teaching/fluid-mechanics/fluids1.pdf[David Tong, Fluid Mechanics, section 2.2, circular-flow example]{ref}

This is useful because <Stokes theorem> lets a local <vorticity> measurement determine a loop's <circulation> when a suitable surface exists, while <Kelvin's circulation theorem> tracks <circulation> along a moving <material curve>. The beauty is the precise link between a local <derivative> and a global <line integral>, with the domain deciding which conclusions are valid.

= Kelvin's circulation theorem
{parent=Circulation (physics)}
{c}
{wiki}
{title2=$d\Gamma/dt=0$}

In an inviscid barotropic fluid subject to conservative body force, the <circulation> of a material loop is constant. For constant density, parameterize that loop by $\mathbf r(s,t)$ with $\mathbf r_t=\mathbf u$. Differentiating $\Gamma=\int\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf r_s\,ds$ gives an integral of $(D\mathbf u/Dt)\cdot d\mathbf r$ plus $\int\partial_s(|\mathbf u|^2/2)ds$. The latter vanishes on a closed loop, and the <Euler equations> make the former the integral of a gradient, also zero. For a barotropic density, $\nabla p/\rho$ is still a gradient of specific enthalpy. Constant circulation is consistent with changing local <vorticity> when a vortex tube changes its cross-sectional area.

= Irrotational circulation in an exterior domain
{parent=Kelvin's circulation theorem}

In smooth barotropic inviscid flow with conservative <body force>, <Kelvin's circulation theorem> preserves circulation of <material curves>. Initially zero <vorticity> therefore gives zero circulation on contractible loops for all times. A loop winding around an excluded obstacle can have a nonzero constant circulation: the planar field $\mathbf u=\Gamma\mathbf e_\theta/(2\pi r)$ is <irrotational> outside the origin but has circulation $\Gamma$ on a positively oriented enclosing loop.

= Irrotational flow
{parent=Vorticity}
{wiki}
{title2=$\nabla\times\mathbf u=\mathbf0$}

Use <irrotational flow> to identify where a <velocity potential> can describe the <velocity field> locally. An <irrotational flow> has $\boldsymbol\omega=\nabla\times\mathbf u=\mathbf0$ throughout the fluid region. For a continuously differentiable <velocity field>, the <Poincare lemma> supplies a <velocity potential> on sufficiently small neighborhoods. A global single-valued <velocity potential> requires zero <circulation> around every <closed curve>; a <simply connected domain> is a sufficient domain assumption.

The potential <line vortex> $\mathbf u=K\mathbf e_\theta/r$ is an <irrotational flow> on the punctured plane, or on an <annulus> $a<r<b$ with $a>0$, even though its <pathlines> are circular. Every sufficiently small loop bounding a disk in that fluid has zero <circulation>, by <Stokes theorem>. A loop winding once counterclockwise around the excluded origin has <circulation> $2\pi K$. Thus local <irrotational flow> and nonzero <circulation> around a hole are compatible: the enclosing disk passes outside the domain of the <velocity field>.

An <irrotational flow> is a kinematic condition on a <velocity field>, independent of whether a particular model neglects <viscosity> or assumes <incompressible flow>. Adding <incompressible flow> makes the local <velocity potential> satisfy the <Laplace equation>. This reduces the number of unknowns and makes <potential flow> a powerful starting point for flow around bodies and for <gravity waves>. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/2-016-hydrodynamics-13-012-fall-2005/c472432debcf6ee250209b68cf18cc12_2005reading4.pdf[A. H. Techet, MIT Hydrodynamics reading 4, pp. 1–2]{ref}

= Circular vortex-sheet mode
{parent=Fluid mechanics}

For a circular patch in solid-body rotation next to stationary fluid, a sinusoidal boundary displacement couples an interior $r^k$ potential to an exterior $r^{-k}$ potential. The tangential velocity jump makes the interface Kelvin--Helmholtz unstable.

\Include[gravity-wave]{parent=Fluid mechanics}

= Stream function
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

= Streamfunction
{synonym}

A two-dimensional incompressible velocity field can be written either as $u=\psi_y$, $v=-\psi_x$ or with both signs reversed. The continuity equation then holds identically.

= Sinusoidal cellular flow
{parent=Stream function}
{title2=$\psi_0=-\sin x\sin y$}

Using $\mathbf u=(-\psi_y,\psi_x)$, the displayed <stream function> gives $\mathbf u_0=(\sin x\cos y,-\cos x\sin y)$. Its <streamlines> are level sets of $\psi_0$. At $(n\pi,m\pi)$, the <Jacobian matrix> is $\operatorname{diag}((-1)^{n+m},-(-1)^{n+m})$, so the points are <saddle equilibria>. At $(\pi/2+n\pi,\pi/2+m\pi)$ the eigenvalues are $\pm i$, giving <center equilibria> surrounded by closed <streamlines>. The grid lines $x=n\pi$ and $y=m\pi$ form <heteroclinic orbits> between adjacent saddles; circulation alternates between cells.

= Melnikov splitting of a periodically perturbed cellular flow
{parent=Sinusoidal cellular flow}
{title2=$M(X_0,t_0)=\frac{\pi\omega}{\sinh(\pi\omega/2)}\sin[\omega(t_0-\log\tan(X_0/2))]$}

Perturb the <sinusoidal cellular flow> by $\epsilon\cos x\cos y\sin(\omega t)$ in its <stream function>. On $y=0$, parameterizing the unperturbed orbit with $q_0(0)=(X_0,0)$ gives $x(s)=2\arctan e^{s+a}$, $a=\log\tan(X_0/2)$. The <heteroclinic Melnikov function for a periodic planar flow> has integrand $\operatorname{sech}^2(t-t_0+a)\sin(\omega t)$. Its odd part vanishes and the Fourier transform of $\operatorname{sech}^2$ gives the displayed result. For each fixed nonzero frequency its simple zeros produce transverse intersections at sufficiently small perturbation, hence exchange of fluid lobes across the former cell boundary. The leading splitting is exponentially small at high frequency; that estimate is not uniform in frequency and perturbation size.

= Linear planar strain and rotation streamlines
{parent=Stream function}
{title2=$\psi=\alpha xy-\frac\beta2(x^2+y^2)$}

The incompressible planar field $(\alpha x-\beta y,\beta x-\alpha y)$ has the displayed <stream function> under $(u,v)=(\psi_y,-\psi_x)$ and vorticity $2\beta$. In rotated coordinates $s=(x+y)/\sqrt2$, $t=(x-y)/\sqrt2$, $\psi=((\alpha-\beta)s^2-(\alpha+\beta)t^2)/2$. For positive parameters its nonstationary <streamlines> are closed ellipses if $\alpha<\beta$, hyperbolas and straight separatrices if $\alpha>\beta$, and parallel straight lines with a stationary diagonal if $\alpha=\beta$.

= Axisymmetric hydrodynamic mass flux function
{parent=Stream function}
{title2=$\rho\mathbf u_p=\nabla\psi\times\nabla\phi$}

For a steady axisymmetric compressible <fluid flow>, <mass conservation> permits this representation. The mass flow through an annulus between two stream surfaces is $2\pi\Delta\psi$. In a steady adiabatic wind without magnetic forces, the <specific entropy>, <specific angular momentum> and <Bernoulli function> are locally functions of $\psi$ on connected regular stream surfaces.

= Stokes streamfunction
{c}
{parent=Stream function}
{title2=$\psi(r,z)$}

For axisymmetric incompressible flow without swirl, $u_r=-\psi_z/r$ and $u_z=\psi_r/r$. These identities enforce incompressibility, and $2\pi\psi$ measures axial volume flux from the symmetry axis when its additive constant is chosen there.

= Streamline classification of a planar linear saddle or centre
{parent=Stream function}

For $(u,v)=(y,ax)$, the stream function $\psi=(y^2-ax^2)/2$ gives hyperbolic streamlines and a saddle when $a>0$, while $a<0$ gives elliptical streamlines and a centre.

= Linear planar flow with strain and rotation
{parent=Streamline classification of a planar linear saddle or centre}
{title2=$\psi=\epsilon xy-\frac{\gamma}{2}(x^2+y^2)$}

The incompressible linear flow $(u,v)=(\epsilon x-\gamma y,\gamma x-\epsilon y)$ has <streamfunction> $\psi=\epsilon xy-\gamma(x^2+y^2)/2$. Its velocity matrix squares to $(\epsilon^2-\gamma^2)I$. Thus strain-dominated flow has saddle trajectories, rotation-dominated flow has closed elliptical trajectories, and equal nonzero strain and rotation gives <simple shear flow> along parallel lines with a stationary line. Pure rotation is counterclockwise for positive $\gamma$.

= Streamline
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

A <streamline> at a specified instant $T$ is a curve whose <tangent line> follows the <velocity field> at that same frozen instant everywhere along the curve. One convenient <integral curve> parameterization is
$$
\frac{d\boldsymbol\gamma}{ds}=\mathbf u(\boldsymbol\gamma(s),T),\qquad T\text{ fixed}.
$$
Here $s$ is an auxiliary curve parameter, with units of seconds in this convention; advancing $s$ does not advance physical time. Any regular reparameterization preserves the tangent geometry. Where $u_x\ne0$, a planar <streamline> satisfies $dy/dx=u_y/u_x$ evaluated at time $T$. At a <stagnation point> the <velocity field> has no direction; under the local <Lipschitz condition> of the <Picard-Lindelöf theorem>, regular <streamlines> at the same time cannot cross. A <streamline> answers which directions fit together now, making it useful for describing local flow geometry. https://web.mit.edu/fluids-modules/www/potential_flows/LecturesHTML/lec02/tutorial/[MIT fluid mechanics modules, tutorial on streamlines, streaklines and pathlines]{ref}

In the analytical <velocity field> $(U,at)$ introduced under <pathline>, the <streamline> through the origin at time $T$ is
$$
y=\frac{aT}{U}x.
$$
At $T=2\ \mathrm s$ this is $y=2x$ when both coordinates are expressed in metres. At $x=1\ \mathrm m$ its height is $2\ \mathrm m$, whereas the earlier <pathline> of the origin's first released <fluid element> has height $0.5\ \mathrm m$. Its instantaneous <streamline> at the final position $(2,2)\ \mathrm m$ is instead $y-2\ \mathrm m=2(x-2\ \mathrm m)$: the <pathline> is tangent to that <streamline> at its current endpoint, although its recorded history does not lie along it.

\Image[stream-path-streak-lines-three-curves.png]
{title=Analytical comparison of a frozen-time streamline, one pathline and a streakline}

The green <streamline> uses the <velocity field> at $T=2\ \mathrm s$ throughout; the orange <pathline> uses times $0\leq t\leq2\ \mathrm s$; the purple <streakline> uses different release times at the single observation time $T=2\ \mathrm s$. The grey arrows all show the instantaneous <velocity> direction at $T$. These are exact analytical curves with chosen units, not measured dye shapes.

In a <steady flow> in a fixed <reference frame>, a <pathline> and a <streamline> solve the same autonomous <ordinary differential equation>. Uniqueness makes each particle released from a fixed point follow the same <integral curve>, so its <pathline>, the <streakline> from that point and the <streamline> share one geometrical curve. Finite recording or release intervals usually show different segments of that curve; the comparison assumes particles stay in the domain and treats stationary <fluid elements> separately. Neither two-dimensionality nor <incompressibility> is needed for this coincidence. Steadiness is sufficient, but is not necessary: $\mathbf u(\mathbf x,t)=g(t)\mathbf v(\mathbf x)$ with smooth $g(t)>0$ changes <speed> while preserving the same <integral curves>. In a general time-dependent <velocity field>, however, the three curves differ. Kline explains why continuous dye release in <steady flow> can therefore display <streamlines>. https://web.mit.edu/hml/ncfmf/05FV.pdf[S. J. Kline, Flow Visualization film notes, section II]{ref}

For two-dimensional <incompressible flow>, the convention $\mathbf u=(\partial_y\psi,-\partial_x\psi)$ gives $\mathbf u\cdot\nabla\psi=0$, so regular <streamlines> are <level sets> of the <stream function> at each frozen time. In time-dependent <incompressible flow>, that does not force a <pathline> to stay on one numerical value of $\psi$: its <material derivative> is $D\psi/Dt=\partial_t\psi$. The distinction is the difference between a geometric snapshot and a parcel's history.

= Stream tube
{parent=Streamline}

A <stream tube> is a narrow tube whose side boundary consists of <streamlines>. In steady flow no fluid crosses its sides, so continuity makes its <mass flux> $A\rho u$ constant. If flow follows a <magnetic field> with constant <magnetohydrodynamic mass loading>, its normal area obeys $A|\mathbf B|=\mathrm{constant}$.

= Velocity potential
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathbf u=\nabla\phi$}

Use a <velocity potential> to reconstruct an <irrotational flow> from one <scalar field>. A <velocity potential> $\phi(\mathbf x,t)$ satisfies
$$
\mathbf u=\nabla\phi.
$$
It has units $\mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}$ and is unchanged as a description of the <velocity field> when any function of time alone is added. For twice continuously differentiable $\phi$, the identity <curl of a gradient> proves zero <vorticity>. In <incompressible flow>, taking the <divergence> also gives $\nabla^2\phi=0$, the <Laplace equation>. This turns a three-component <velocity field> into a single <harmonic function> with appropriate <boundary conditions>. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/2-016-hydrodynamics-13-012-fall-2005/c472432debcf6ee250209b68cf18cc12_2005reading4.pdf[A. H. Techet, MIT Hydrodynamics reading 4, pp. 1–3]{ref}

For <uniform flow> $\mathbf u=(U,0,0)$, take $\phi=Ux$. With $U=2\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, the change from $x=0$ to $x=3\ \mathrm m$ is $\Delta\phi=6\ \mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}$. By the <fundamental theorem for line integrals>, the <line integral> of <velocity> between these points equals that change along any path in the domain; a <closed curve> gives zero <circulation>.

For the potential <line vortex>, choose a local angle branch in <polar coordinates> and set $\phi=K\theta$. Then $u_r=\partial_r\phi=0$ and $u_\theta=r^{-1}\partial_\theta\phi=K/r$. One counterclockwise turn changes the continued <velocity potential> by $2\pi K$, although the <velocity field> returns to its original value. With $K=1\ \mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}$, this change is $2\pi\ \mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}$, exactly the <circulation>. A full <annulus> therefore admits local angle branches and a multivalued <velocity potential>, but no global single-valued <velocity potential> when $K\ne0$. Cutting the <annulus> along a radial line allows a single-valued angle branch on the remaining domain. The obstruction is <path independence>, not a failure of the local <gradient> formula.

= Potential flow around a circular cylinder
{parent=Velocity potential}

Uniform irrotational flow of speed $U$ past a circular cylinder of radius $a$, with zero circulation, has <velocity potential>
$$
\phi=U\left(r+\frac{a^2}{r}\right)\cos\theta.
$$
On the cylinder, the tangential speed is $-2U\sin\theta$, so the <Bernoulli equation> gives
$$
p(a,\theta)=p_\infty+\frac12\rho U^2(1-4\sin^2\theta).
$$

= Potential flow around a circular cylinder with circulation
{parent=Potential flow around a circular cylinder}

Adding circulation $\kappa$ gives the possibly multivalued <velocity potential>
$$
\phi=U\left(r+\frac{a^2}{r}\right)\cos\theta+\frac\kappa{2\pi}\theta.
$$
The velocity remains single-valued, and on the cylinder its tangential component is $-2U\sin\theta+\kappa/(2\pi a)$.

= Kutta–Joukowski theorem
{c}
{parent=Potential flow around a circular cylinder with circulation}
{wiki}

For steady two-dimensional inviscid flow of density $\rho$, far-field velocity $\mathbf U$, and circulation $\kappa$, the force per unit span is perpendicular to the flow. With counterclockwise circulation taken positive and $\mathbf U=U\mathbf e_x$, it is $-\rho\kappa U\mathbf e_y$.

= Advective and pressure contributions to circulatory lift
{parent=Kutta–Joukowski theorem}
{title2=$\oint(\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf n)\mathbf u\,dl=\tfrac12\boldsymbol\Gamma\times\mathbf U$}

For <uniform flow> $\mathbf U$ plus a <line vortex> of counterclockwise <circulation> $\Gamma$, the outward advective <momentum flux> around a large circle is $\rho\boldsymbol\Gamma\times\mathbf U/2$. The <Bernoulli equation> supplies an equal <pressure> contribution. Their sum is the force on the fluid; the force on an obstacle per unit span is its negative, $\rho\mathbf U\times\boldsymbol\Gamma$. Retaining only the advective term loses half the lift and obscures its sign.

= Radially symmetric incompressible flow in a planar annulus
{parent=Velocity potential}

For liquid occupying $a(t)<r<b(t)$, radial symmetry and <incompressible flow> imply
$$
u_r(r,t)=\frac{a\dot a}{r},
\qquad
\phi(r,t)=a\dot a\log r+C(t).
$$
The <kinematic boundary condition> at the outer interface gives
$$
b^2-a^2=\text{constant},
$$
which expresses conservation of the liquid's area.

= Pressure in radially symmetric annular potential flow
{parent=Radially symmetric incompressible flow in a planar annulus}

If the pressure at $r=b(t)$ is $p_\infty$, the <unsteady Bernoulli equation> gives
$$
p(a,t)-p_\infty
=\rho\left[\frac d{dt}(a\dot a)\log\frac ba
-\frac{(a\dot a)^2}{2}\left(\frac1{a^2}-\frac1{b^2}\right)\right].
$$

= Small oscillation of a planar gas bubble in an annular liquid
{parent=Pressure in radially symmetric annular potential flow}

For $a=a_0(1+\epsilon)$, equilibrium outer radius $b_0$, and a bubble gas obeying $p_0\pi a^2=\text{constant}$, the <linearization> is
$$
\rho a_0^2\log\frac{b_0}{a_0}\,\ddot\epsilon
=-2p_\infty\epsilon.
$$
The bubble therefore undergoes <simple harmonic motion> with
$$
\omega^2=\frac{2p_\infty}{\rho a_0^2\log(b_0/a_0)}.
$$

= Plume
{disambiguate=fluid dynamics}
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

A <plume (fluid dynamics)> is a localized buoyancy-driven flow generated by a source of heat or another density-changing substance. A plume can be laminar or turbulent; the transport regime is an additional condition.

= Laminar plume
{parent=Plume (fluid dynamics)}

A laminar plume is a narrow buoyancy-driven flow in which viscous diffusion balances inertia across the plume.

= Integral momentum-flux balance for a two-dimensional plume
{parent=Laminar plume}

For a steady incompressible plume with localized vertical body force $b(x)\delta(y)$, decay of velocity and shear at transverse infinity gives
$$
\frac{d}{dx}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\rho u^2\,dy=b(x).
$$

= Similarity scaling of a two-dimensional laminar plume
{parent=Laminar plume}

If $b(x)=Bx^{-1/5}$, balancing the momentum flux and transverse viscosity gives
$$
\Delta\sim\left(\frac{\mu^2}{\rho B}\right)^{1/3}x^{2/5},
\qquad
U\sim\left(\frac{B^2}{\rho\mu}\right)^{1/3}x^{1/5}.
$$

= Similarity equation for a two-dimensional laminar plume
{parent=Similarity scaling of a two-dimensional laminar plume}

With $u=-\psi_y$, $v=\psi_x$, $\eta=y/\Delta$, and $\psi=U\Delta f(\eta)$, the plume boundary-layer equation reduces to
$$
f'''+\frac15(f')^2-\frac35ff''=\delta(\eta).
$$

= Euler equations for an inviscid fluid
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{c}
{wiki}

= Euler equations
{synonym}
{c}

= Euler momentum equation
{c}
{synonym}

= Euler equation for fluid motion
{c}
{synonym}

= Euler equation
{c}
{synonym}

Euler’s equations express momentum conservation in a fluid without viscosity.

= Linearized Euler equations
{parent=Euler equations for an inviscid fluid}

The linearized Euler equations retain only first-order perturbations about a specified inviscid fluid state. Together with the linearized <continuity equation> and a pressure-density constitutive relation, they describe small-amplitude <acoustic waves>. About a uniform resting state, $\rho_0\partial_t\mathbf u'=-\nabla p'$, $\partial_t\rho'+\rho_0\nabla\cdot\mathbf u'=0$, and an isentropic pressure perturbation has $p'=c_0^2\rho'$. Gradients and advection of a moving background must be retained when its variation is not negligible.

= Conservative energy flux of a polytropic ideal gas
{parent=Euler equations for an inviscid fluid}
{title2=$E=\tfrac12\rho u^2+\frac p{\gamma-1},\quad F_E=u_x(E+p)$}

For an unmagnetized <ideal gas> without gravity, the <pressure> equation gives internal-energy balance $\partial_te+\partial_x(u_xe)=-p\partial_xu_x$, where $e=p/(\gamma-1)$. The momentum equation gives kinetic-energy balance with source $-u_x\partial_xp$. Their sum is conservative with the displayed total-energy flux. Tangential <velocities> contribute to <kinetic energy> even when all derivatives are in the normal direction. Smooth <polytropic flow> advects its <entropy> parameter; a physical shock need not preserve that parameter across the discontinuity.

= Crocco form of the unsteady Euler equation
{c}
{parent=Euler equations for an inviscid fluid}
{title2=$\partial_t\mathbf u+\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf u+\nabla\varepsilon=T\nabla s$}

For a fixed-composition inviscid fluid, <specific enthalpy> satisfies $dw=Tds+dp/\rho$. Combining this identity with the <vorticity cross-product identity> gives the displayed equation, with $\boldsymbol\omega=\nabla\times\mathbf u$ and $\varepsilon=u^2/2+\Phi+w$. It exposes entropy gradients as the thermodynamic source of a mismatch between <Bernoulli function> gradients and the vorticity term. In a steady <adiabatic process>, its projection along a <streamline> makes $\varepsilon$ constant there.

= Yudovich characteristic flow
{c}
{parent=Euler equations for an inviscid fluid}
{title2=$\dot X(t)=u(t,X(t))$}

For a planar Euler velocity obtained from vorticity in $L^\infty_{\mathrm{loc}}(dt;L^1\cap L^\infty)$, the <planar vorticity velocity kernel> gives a locally integrable speed bound and <log-Lipschitz modulus>. Spatial mollification gives existence of global trajectories; the <Osgood uniqueness criterion> gives uniqueness and continuous dependence. Measurability in time and an initial trace are needed. This establishes the flow for a fixed Euler solution; nonlinear uniqueness of the Euler solution itself requires further comparison arguments.

= Bernoulli's principle
{parent=Euler equations for an inviscid fluid}
{c}
{wiki}
{title2=$p/\rho+|\mathbf u|^2/2+\Phi=B$}

= Bernoulli principle
{c}
{synonym}
