The <Bernoulli principle> relates <pressure>, <kinetic energy> per unit volume and <Newtonian gravitational potential> along a <streamline> of <steady flow> with <inviscid flow>. For constant <mass density>, the <Bernoulli equation> gives $p/\rho+u^2/2+\Phi$ constant along that <streamline>. Constancy across different <streamlines> requires further hypotheses, such as <irrotational flow>.

Precisely, suppose a <fluid> of constant positive <mass density> obeys the <Euler equations for an inviscid fluid>, its <velocity field> and <pressure> are steady, and the body <force> per unit <mass> is $-\nabla\Phi$. Then
$$
B=\frac{p}{\rho}+\frac{|\mathbf u|^2}{2}+\Phi,\qquad \mathbf u\cdot\nabla B=0.
$$
Every term has units of <energy> per unit <mass>. For a uniform gravitational field, <Newtonian gravitational potential> is $\Phi=gz$, up to an additive constant. The <pressure> term represents work by surrounding <fluid>, so acceleration can exchange <pressure> work with <kinetic energy>. Dotting the steady <Euler equations> with $\mathbf u$ gives the displayed <directional derivative>: this is why constancy follows a <streamline>. https://www.damtp.cam.ac.uk/user/mem2/FLUIDS-IB/dyn.pdf[Cambridge, Fluid Dynamics IB, Dynamics, section 2.4]{ref}

Continue the horizontal water constriction example from <incompressible flow>. Assume negligible viscous losses, <steady flow> and approximately <uniform flow> at both measurement sections, so the <streamline> speeds there equal the mean speeds $0.20$ and $0.80\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. Equal height cancels the <Newtonian gravitational potential> terms. The ideal <pressure> drop is
$$
p_1-p_2=\frac{\rho}{2}(U_2^2-U_1^2)=\frac{1000}{2}(0.80^2-0.20^2)=300\,\mathrm{Pa}.
$$
This is the <Venturi effect> in its ideal limit: the gain in <kinetic energy> density is supplied by <pressure> work. Cambridge's treatment uses this relation to connect a measured <pressure> difference to <volumetric flow rate>. https://www.damtp.cam.ac.uk/user/mem2/FLUIDS-IB/dyn.pdf[Cambridge, Fluid Dynamics IB, Dynamics, section 2.5]{ref}

The $300\,\mathrm{Pa}$ result is conditional, rather than a prediction of the actual viscous pipe. <Mass conservation> fixes $Q$ and the ratio of mean speeds even with <viscosity>, but nonuniform <velocity profiles> require an integrated <energy> balance, and wall friction dissipates mechanical <energy>. A pump would supply additional work. In particular, constant mean speed in a straight pipe does not imply constant <pressure>: <Poiseuille flow> needs a continuing <pressure gradient> to overcome <viscosity>. https://uclnatsci.github.io/2021/Electromagnetism-Fluids-and-Waves/fluids/bernoulli.html[UCL, Bernoulli's equation, assumptions and boundary limitations]{ref}

= Venturi effect
{c}
{parent=Bernoulli's principle}
{wiki}

In ideal steady flow, a contraction of a pipe increases the speed and lowers the pressure. For constant <mass density>, equal body-force potential at the ends and approximately uniform endpoint speeds, <conservation of mass> and the <Bernoulli equation> give $\dot M=S_1S_2\sqrt{2\rho(p_1-p_2)/(S_1^2-S_2^2)}$ for flow from a wider to a narrower section. Elevation changes contribute a body-force potential difference, and nonuniform profiles or viscous losses require additional information.

// Target: probability-and-statistics.bigb

= Axisymmetric inviscid flow between moving parallel plates
{parent=Euler equations for an inviscid fluid}

For plates $z=\pm h(t)$, incompressibility, impermeability and regularity on the axis determine a z-independent radial velocity $u_r=-r\dot h/(2h)$ and vertical velocity $u_z=z\dot h/h$. A possible azimuthal velocity requires additional rotational data; continuity alone does not determine it. For a smooth regular initial profile $U(r)$ independent of $z$, its azimuthal Euler equation transports it as $u_\theta(r,t)=\lambda U(\lambda r)$, where $\lambda=\sqrt{h/h_0}$. If the rotation is solid-body with angular velocity $\Omega(t)$, the <vorticity equation> gives $\Omega=\Omega_0h/h_0$. Along a particle path, $r^2h$ and $r^2\Omega$ are constant, illustrating <conservation of angular momentum>.

= Radial velocity
{title2=$u_r$}
{parent=Euler equations for an inviscid fluid}

Radial velocity is the component of a velocity field along the line from a chosen centre to the point of observation.

= Unsteady Bernoulli equation
{parent=Euler equations for an inviscid fluid}
{c}

For time-dependent <irrotational flow> with $u=\nabla\phi$, constant <mass density>, and no body force, the <Euler equations for an inviscid fluid> integrate to
$$
\partial_t\phi+\frac12|\nabla\phi|^2+\frac p\rho=C(t).
$$
A time-dependent change of gauge in $\phi$ may set $C(t)=0$.

This is the unsteady potential-flow version of <Bernoulli's principle>, with a time-dependent potential gauge.

= Constant-tension balloon discharge
{parent=Unsteady Bernoulli equation}
{title2=$R^3\ddot R+2R^2\dot R^2=-K,\quad K=\gamma a^2/(2\rho L)$}

A spherical balloon feeding an inviscid uniform tube plug, with entrance excess <pressure> $2\gamma/R$, satisfies $L\dot u=2\gamma/(\rho R)$ and $4\pi R^2\dot R=-\pi a^2u$. Eliminating $u$ yields the displayed equation. Its <first integral> is $R^4\dot R^2+KR^2=C$. From rest at radius $R_0$, $C=KR_0^2$; integration gives $t=R_0^2K^{-1/2}(\pi/4-\theta/2+\sin(2\theta)/4)$, where $\theta=\arcsin(R/R_0)$. The ideal emptying time is $\pi R_0^2/(4\sqrt K)$. Independently prescribed initial flow changes $C$ and therefore this time.

= Inviscid startup in a pressure-driven tube
{parent=Unsteady Bernoulli equation}
{title2=$U(t)=\sqrt{2P/\rho}\tanh\left(\frac tL\sqrt{\frac P{2\rho}}\right)$}

For a slender tube of length $L$ supplied from a large reservoir with excess pressure $P$, negligible gravity and entrance inertia, the <unsteady Bernoulli equation> gives $L\dot U+U^2/2=P/\rho$. A stationary initial liquid column has the displayed speed and <volume flux> $\pi a^2U$ for tube radius $a$. The inertial term controls the early linear acceleration; the kinetic-energy term sets the terminal inviscid speed. This is a leading slender-tube model while the reservoir remains large and the tube full.

= Linearly elastic balloon discharge
{parent=Unsteady Bernoulli equation}
{title2=$\Omega^2=K\pi a^2/(\rho L)$}

An inviscid uniform tube plug driven by entrance excess pressure $KV$ obeys $\rho L\dot u=KV$ and $\dot V=-\pi a^2u$. From rest, the first zero of $V$ occurs after a quarter harmonic period, independent of its initial volume. The maximum speed is proportional to that initial volume. Entrance losses, a reservoir kinetic head or independently prescribed initial flow change this ideal model.

= Clebsch-potential variational derivation of incompressible Euler flow
{parent=Euler equations for an inviscid fluid}
{c}

For $\mathbf u=\nabla\phi+\beta\nabla\alpha$, the density
$$
\mathcal L=-\beta\alpha_t-\frac12|\mathbf u|^2
$$
gives incompressibility and material conservation of $\alpha$ and $\beta$. Differentiating the Clebsch representation then yields the Euler momentum equation with
$$
p=-\frac12|\mathbf u|^2-\phi_t-\beta\alpha_t.
$$

= Bernoulli equation
{parent=Euler equations for an inviscid fluid}
{c}

= Bernoulli's equation
{c}
{synonym}

For steady inviscid flow, pressure, kinetic energy density, and conservative potential are constant along a streamline.

= Bernoulli invariant
{c}
{parent=Bernoulli equation}
{title2=$b=u^2/2+\Phi+w$}

In a steady adiabatic inviscid fluid with a conservative body force, the <Bernoulli invariant> is the displayed specific energy, constant along each <streamline>. Dotting the <Euler equations> with velocity and using the <specific enthalpy> identity gives $\mathbf u\cdot\nabla b=0$. In <ideal magnetohydrodynamics>, magnetic work need not vanish; the <planar ideal-MHD Bernoulli invariant> includes the corresponding electromagnetic correction.

= Hydraulic control over a smooth hump
{parent=Bernoulli equation}
{title2=$D+h+Q^2/(2gh^2)=\text{constant}$}

A slowly varying, steady, inviscid channel flow has constant discharge $Q=uh$ per unit width and the displayed <Bernoulli equation>. The bed-height function of depth has its maximum at $h_c=(Q^2/g)^{1/3}$, where the <Froude number> is one. At a nondegenerate critical crest, a differentiable profile changes between the <subcritical flow> and <supercritical flow> branches. With subcritical upstream squared Froude number $F<1$ and upstream depth $h_1$, the shock-free supercritical downstream depth is $h_1(F+\sqrt{F^2+8F})/4$.

= Bernoulli function for planar constant-vorticity flow
{parent=Bernoulli equation}
{c}
{title2=$|\mathbf u|^2/2+\omega\psi+p/\rho=C$}

With the <streamfunction> convention $(u,v)=(\psi_y,-\psi_x)$, constant scalar <vorticity> gives $\mathbf u\times\boldsymbol\omega=-\omega\nabla\psi$. For steady inviscid constant-density flow without body force, this integrates to
$$
\tfrac12|\mathbf u|^2+\omega\psi+p/\rho=C
$$
throughout a connected fluid region. Reversing the streamfunction convention changes the sign of its term; the declared convention matters.

= Steady rotating-frame Bernoulli integral
{parent=Bernoulli equation}
{title2=$\tfrac12|\mathbf v|^2+p/\rho+\Phi-\tfrac12\Omega^2r_\perp^2$}

For <steady flow> of constant-density fluid in a uniformly rotating frame with conservative body forces, the displayed quantity is constant along each relative <streamline>. The <centrifugal potential> contributes the negative quadratic term, and the <Coriolis force> contributes no work because it is perpendicular to the relative velocity. The result follows by dotting the rotating <Euler equations for an inviscid fluid> with that velocity.

= Bernoulli function
{parent=Bernoulli equation}
{c}
{wiki}

The Bernoulli function combines pressure, kinetic energy density, and conservative potential.

= Bernoulli equation over topography
{parent=Bernoulli equation}
{c}

For steady potential flow over small hills, linearized Bernoulli pressure combines a hydrostatic term $-\rho g\eta$ with a dynamic term $-\rho U\phi_x$.

= Draining time of a uniform tank through a siphon
{parent=Bernoulli equation}

If a tank and siphon tube have areas $A$ and $a$, the tube outlet is $H$ below an inlet initially submerged by $h_0$, and both free boundaries are at <atmospheric pressure>, continuity and Bernoulli's equation give
$$
t=\sqrt{2}\left(\frac{A^2}{a^2}-1\right)^{1/2}
\frac{\sqrt{H+h_0}-\sqrt H}{\sqrt g}
$$
for the free surface to reach the inlet.

= Integral momentum equation
{parent=Euler equations for an inviscid fluid}
{wiki}

The integral momentum equation balances momentum flux, pressure, body force, and forces on a control volume.

= Holding force on a contracting nozzle
{parent=Integral momentum equation}
{title2=$F=\rho Q^2(A_0/A_1-1)^2/(2A_0)$}

For steady ideal flow through a horizontal contracting nozzle, <Bernoulli equation> gives inlet gauge pressure $p_0=\rho Q^2(A_1^{-2}-A_0^{-2})/2$. The <integral momentum equation> gives the fluid's force on the nozzle as $p_0A_0-\rho Q^2(A_1^{-1}-A_0^{-1})$ in the outlet direction. Simplification yields the displayed nonnegative magnitude. The external holding force has equal magnitude and the opposite direction.

= Force on a pipe junction
{parent=Integral momentum equation}
{wiki}

The force on a pipe junction follows by balancing inlet and outlet momentum fluxes and pressure forces.

= Conservation of mass in a pipe
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

For steady incompressible pipe flow, volume flux equals cross-sectional area times mean speed and is conserved through a junction.

\Include[viscous-fluid-flow]{parent=Fluid mechanics}

= Surface tension
{title2=$\gamma$}
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

<Surface tension> $\gamma$ is the tensile <force> per unit length across a line drawn in a <liquid> interface. For a clean interface with <isotropy>, it has the same value in every tangential direction: each side pulls the other along the surface, perpendicular to the line. A straight segment of length $\ell$ therefore transmits a <force> of magnitude $\gamma\ell$ from one interface. Here we assume constant $\gamma>0$, with fixed <temperature> and composition. The same coefficient measures the reversible <work> required to create <surface area>:
$$
F_{\text{one interface}}=\gamma\ell,\qquad dE_{\mathrm s}=\gamma\,dA,\qquad
[\gamma]=\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}}=\mathrm{J\,m^{-2}}.
$$
The equality of units follows from $1\,\mathrm J=1\,\mathrm{N\,m}$; the equality of the mechanical coefficients follows from the moving-rim argument below. https://openstax.org/books/college-physics-2e/pages/11-8-cohesion-and-adhesion-in-liquids-surface-tension-and-capillary-action[OpenStax, College Physics 2e, §11.8: surface tension and the sliding wire]{ref} https://arxiv.org/html/2011.11403v2[Tomas Bohr and Bernhard Scheichl, Surface Tension and Energy Conservation in a Moving Fluid, equations (5) and (13)]{ref}

For a concrete example, take an ideal flat soap film spanning a rectangular wire frame with a straight sliding rim of length $\ell$. Pull the rim outwards in the plane of the film, perpendicular to the rim. Each of the film's two <liquid>–<gas> interfaces pulls the rim back towards the film with <force> $\gamma\ell$, so the outward holding <force> is $F=2\gamma\ell$. The two faces are two interfaces, each with its own <surface tension>. With the assumed toy value $\gamma=0.072\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}}$ and $\ell=20\,\mathrm{mm}=0.020\,\mathrm m$, the required <force> is $2.88\,\mathrm{mN}$; one interface would contribute $1.44\,\mathrm{mN}$. This value of $\gamma$ is a stipulated model parameter, rather than a measured value for a particular soap solution.

\Image[surface-tension-film-force.png]
{title=Two film faces double the force on a sliding rim}
{description=The plan view shows the outward pull and the inward <surface tension> <force> on a sliding rim; the two interfaces overlap in this view. The graph compares one and two interfaces at the same assumed constant $\gamma=0.072\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}}$, with rim length in millimetres and <force> in millinewtons.}

Why does an interface resist being enlarged? Its <molecules> have a different environment from those in the bulk <liquid>; changing that environment has an <energy> cost. In the constant-<surface tension> model, reducing <surface area> lowers <surface energy>. This connects an easily measured rim <force> to the shape of a droplet, the <Young–Laplace equation> for its <pressure>, and the restoring mechanism of <capillary waves>. It is useful because one coefficient links <forces>, <energy> and geometry. https://davidtong.org/pdfs/teaching/fluid-mechanics/fluids3.pdf[David Tong, Fluid Mechanics, §4.1.3, pp. 121–123]{ref}

The moving-rim argument also explains the factor of two without memorizing a special formula. An outward displacement $dx$ creates <surface area> $\ell\,dx$ on each face, hence $dA_{\mathrm{total}}=2\ell\,dx$. Slow pulling without <viscous dissipation> requires <work> $F\,dx=\gamma\,dA_{\mathrm{total}}$, giving $F=2\gamma\ell$. For one interface, the same argument gives $F=\gamma\ell$. A <surface tension> gradient instead produces <Marangoni stress>; the constant-$\gamma$ assumption excludes that additional effect.

= Surface energy
{parent=Surface tension}
{title2=$E_{\mathrm s}=\gamma A$}

For a <liquid> interface with constant <surface tension> $\gamma$, the mechanical <surface energy> is the <energy> associated with its <surface area> $A$:
$$
E_{\mathrm s}=\gamma A+C,\qquad
E_{\mathrm s}(A_2)-E_{\mathrm s}(A_1)=\gamma(A_2-A_1).
$$
The additive constant $C$ does not affect the <work> needed to change <surface area>. For several interfaces with the same <surface tension>, $A$ means their total <surface area>; the two faces of a thin soap film must both be counted. https://arxiv.org/html/2011.11403v2[Tomas Bohr and Bernhard Scheichl, Surface Tension and Energy Conservation in a Moving Fluid, equation (13)]{ref}

With assumed $\gamma=0.072\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}}$, creating $1\,\mathrm{cm^2}=10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2}$ of one interface requires $7.2\,\mathrm{\mu J}$ of reversible <work>. In the sliding-rim example illustrated under <surface tension>, moving a $20\,\mathrm{mm}$ rim outwards by $1\,\mathrm{mm}$ creates $4.0\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{m^2}$ across the two faces and requires $2.88\,\mathrm{\mu J}$. Independently, multiplying its $2.88\,\mathrm{mN}$ holding <force> by the displacement gives the same <work>.

This makes the preference for a round isolated droplet precise. At fixed <volume> $V$, the three-dimensional <isoperimetric inequality> gives
$$
A^3\geq36\pi V^2,
$$
with equality for a <sphere>. Thus a <sphere> minimizes <surface energy> among sufficiently regular closed shapes enclosing the same <volume>, when <surface tension> is uniform and other <energy> contributions are negligible. This is an application of the <isoperimetric inequality>, rather than a proof of it. Walls, <wetting>, and <gravity> can change the shape by adding constraints or other <energy> contributions. https://math.hmc.edu/funfacts/isoperimetric-inequality/[Francis E. Su et al., Isoperimetric Inequality, Math Fun Facts]{ref}

The differential rule $dE_{\mathrm s}=\gamma\,dA$ integrates directly to $E_{\mathrm s}=\gamma A+C$ because <surface tension> is constant. At fixed <liquid> <volume>, decreasing <surface area> can drive <viscous fluid flow>; the released <surface energy> can supply <viscous dissipation>. This mechanical bookkeeping is useful for predicting which deformations can release <energy>. If <surface tension> depends on <temperature> or composition, distinguish this mechanical area-<work> relation from the complete interfacial <thermodynamic free energy> and <internal energy>.

= Wetting
{parent=Surface tension}
{wiki}

= Contact line
{parent=Wetting}

The line where the interfaces of three phases meet, for example where a liquid film meets a solid and the surrounding gas. Continuum assumptions at this line can differ from the bulk <lubrication approximation>.

= Bond number
{c}
{parent=Surface tension}
{title2=$\mathrm{Bo}=\Delta\rho\,g\ell^2/\gamma$}
{wiki}

= Eotvos number
{c}
{synonym}

The Bond number compares hydrostatic stress over a length $\ell$ with capillary stress. For a gas bubble of radius $a$ and negligible density, $\mathrm{Bo}=\rho ga^2/\gamma$. Small Bond number is the usual sufficient criterion for capillarity to resist gravity-scale deformations. For the clean-bubble terminal speed, the <capillary number> is $\mathrm{Ca}=\mu U/\gamma=\mathrm{Bo}/3$.

= Oil lens capillary balance
{parent=Surface tension}
{title2=$h^2=2\Gamma/((\rho_w-\rho_o)g)$}

For a broad, thin oil lens below a flat ice-water interface, define the effective interfacial energy $\Gamma=\gamma_{io}+\gamma_{ow}-\gamma_{iw}$ from the relevant <surface tensions>. At fixed oil volume $\mathcal V=Ah$, the energy is $E=\Gamma\mathcal V/h+\frac12(\rho_w-\rho_o)g\mathcal Vh$. Setting its <derivative> to zero gives $h^2=2\Gamma/[(\rho_w-\rho_o)g]$ for $\Gamma>0$, and the positive <second derivative> proves a minimum. A contact angle measured through the oil gives $\Gamma=\gamma_{ow}(1-\cos\vartheta)$. Oil-ice tension alone does not determine this effective quantity. Complete wetting or rough underside pockets invalidate this finite, flat-lens approximation. With densities $1025$ and $850\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, a lens below $1\,\mathrm{cm}$ requires $0<\Gamma<0.08584\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}}$.

= Contact angle
{parent=Surface tension}
{title2=$\theta_c$}

The contact angle is measured through a liquid between its interface and a solid substrate at their contact line. In a gently sloping two-dimensional film on a horizontal substrate, its small-angle value is the limiting magnitude of the height slope. A zero contact angle means a tangential approach to the substrate; it does not imply bounded curvature, as the limiting <zero-flux thermocapillary film profiles> illustrate.

= Capillary number
{parent=Surface tension}
{title2=$\mathrm{Ca}=\mu U/\gamma$}
{wiki}

The capillary number compares viscous stresses with <surface tension>. Its velocity and viscosity conventions must be specified; a porous-medium displacement can use the <Darcy velocity> and the viscosity of the displaced fluid.

= Capillary pressure
{title2=$p_c=\gamma\kappa$}
{parent=Surface tension}
{wiki}

Capillary pressure is the pressure jump produced by a curved interface. For constant surface tension it is proportional to the interface curvature according to the <Young–Laplace equation>.

= Surfactant
{parent=Surface tension}
{wiki}

A surfactant is an interfacially active substance that changes surface tension. A concentration gradient therefore generates tangential <Marangoni effect>[Marangoni stress].

= Surfactant transport with exchange relaxation
{parent=Surfactant}
{title2=$D_t C=-C\nabla_s\cdot\mathbf u_s-Cu_n\kappa+D_s\Delta_sC-k(C-C_0)$}

The <material derivative> of surface concentration combines tangential and normal area change, <surface diffusion>, and relaxation towards a reservoir concentration $C_0$. The rate $k$ represents exchange; setting $k=0$ recovers transport without exchange. The concentration is measured per interfacial area, so stretching dilutes it.

= Translating surfactant-coated bubble
{parent=Surfactant transport with exchange relaxation}
{title2=$\lambda=\gamma_1C_0a/[3\mu(ka^2+2D_s)]$}

A spherical inviscid bubble moving slowly through a viscous liquid has a dipolar <surfactant> perturbation. The associated <Marangoni stress> reduces the tangential velocity from its clean-bubble value towards zero. In the linear regime, with $\mathbf u_s=A(I-\mathbf n\mathbf n)\mathbf U$, the consistent <Newtonian fluid stress tensor> gives $A=-1/[2(1+2\lambda)]$.

= Normal stress balance on a translating bubble
{parent=Translating surfactant-coated bubble}

The dipolar normal <traction> is balanced by buoyant <hydrostatic pressure> and the concentration-dependent normal capillary stress, in addition to the constant gas pressure and equilibrium Laplace pressure. A degree-one shape perturbation translates a sphere rather than deforming it; the balance fixes the terminal speed. A uniform gas pressure alone cannot balance this mode.

= Dipolar surfactant distribution
{parent=Translating surfactant-coated bubble}
{title2=$C'=B\mathbf U\cdot\mathbf n$}

Linear steady transport on a translating sphere gives $B=2AC_0a/(ka^2+2D_s)$. The components of its unit <normal vector> are degree-one <spherical harmonics>. The condition $2|A|Ua/(ka^2+2D_s)\ll1$ ensures the concentration perturbation is small relative to $C_0$.

= Surfactant stabilization of film rupture
{parent=Surfactant}

Outward interfacial flow from a thinning region dilutes surfactant there and raises its <surface tension>. The resulting <Marangoni effect>[Marangoni stress] pulls fluid back toward the thinning region, opposes further stretching, and slows rupture.

= Strong-surfactant long-wave rupture maximum
{parent=Surfactant stabilization of film rupture}
{title2=$K_m^2\sim1/(2\Gamma),\quad s_{\max}\sim3V^2/(4\mu\gamma h_0^5)$}

For strong <Marangoni stress> and large $\Gamma=\gamma h_0^2/(3V)$, the unstable band has $K\ll1$. Its rate is asymptotically $[3V/(\mu h_0^3)]K^2(1-\Gamma K^2)/3$. Maximizing this quadratic in $K^2$ gives the displayed finite-wavelength maximum. The growth timescale is not alone a bubble lifetime: initial disturbance size and thickness evolution also enter.

= Insoluble surfactant
{parent=Surfactant}

An insoluble surfactant remains on an interface. Without surface diffusion, its material concentration changes only through the local surface-area dilation rate.

= Surface diffusion
{parent=Insoluble surfactant}
{title2=$D_s\Delta_s C$}
{wiki}

Surface diffusion transports particles along an interface down a <concentration> gradient. On an isotropic smooth interface with constant diffusivity $D_s$, its flux is $-D_s\nabla_sC$ and its concentration source is $D_s\Delta_sC$, using the <surface gradient> and <surface Laplacian>.

= Conservation of insoluble surfactant on a moving interface
{parent=Insoluble surfactant}

For concentration $C$ per unit <surface area>, conservation on a moving material interface gives
$$
\frac{DC}{Dt}=-C\left[\nabla_s\cdot\mathbf u_s+u_n\nabla_s\cdot\mathbf n\right]+D_s\Delta_sC.
$$
The <material derivative> follows the surface particles. The two dilution terms are tangential expansion and curvature-induced area change from normal velocity $u_n$; the last term is <surface diffusion>.

= Quadrupolar surfactant distribution on a spherical interface
{parent=Conservation of insoluble surfactant on a moving interface}

For radius $a$ and tangential velocity $\mathbf u_s=\mathbf I_s\mathbf A\mathbf x$ with symmetric traceless $\mathbf A$, the <surface divergence> is $-3\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf A\mathbf n$. At small surface <Péclet number> $a^2|\mathbf A|/D_s$, neglecting advection of the concentration perturbation gives
$$
C-C_0=\frac{C_0a^2}{2D_s}\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf A\mathbf n.
$$
The result follows because this quadratic is a degree-two <spherical harmonic> with <surface Laplacian> $-6/a^2$ times itself and zero spherical mean.

= Marangoni effect
{c}
{parent=Surface tension}
{wiki}

= Marangoni stress
{c}
{synonym}

The Marangoni effect is interfacial flow driven by a surface-tension gradient. Tangential stress balances the surface gradient $\nabla_s\gamma$.

= Thermocapillary migration of an insulating bubble
{parent=Marangoni effect}
{title2=$\mathbf U=-a\gamma_T\mathbf H/(2\mu)$}

For a clean spherical gas bubble with negligible internal <dynamic viscosity> and <thermal conductivity>, a weak temperature gradient produces the displayed migration <velocity>, where $\gamma_T=d\gamma/dT$. The exterior <Papkovich–Neuber representation> can use only the scalar potential $\chi=-a^3\mathbf U\cdot\mathbf r/(2r^3)$. Its surface normal velocity equals $\mathbf U\cdot\mathbf n$, and its tangential <traction> balances minus the surface <surface tension> gradient from the <temperature dipole around an insulating sphere>. For the usual $\gamma_T<0$ the bubble moves toward warmer liquid. Small <Reynolds number>, <Péclet number>, <capillary number>, and fractional surface-tension variation justify the approximation; <surfactants> can oppose the temperature-driven <Marangoni stress>.

= Interfacial stress balance with variable surface tension
{parent=Marangoni effect}

Across a fluid interface, the jump in normal traction is the surface tension times curvature, while the tangential traction jump is the surface gradient of surface tension. For one common normal convention,
$$
(\boldsymbol\sigma_+-\boldsymbol\sigma_-)\mathbin\cdot\mathbf n
=\gamma\kappa\mathbf n-\nabla_s\gamma.
$$

= Marangoni immobilization of a bubble in straining flow
{c}
{parent=Interfacial stress balance with variable surface tension}

For an almost spherical inviscid bubble in exterior <Stokes flow> $\mathbf E\mathbf x$, let $\gamma=\gamma_0-\gamma_1(C-C_0)$ and $K=C_0a^2/(2D_s)$. The <quadrupolar surfactant distribution on a spherical interface> makes the tangential velocity $\mathbf u_s=\alpha\mathbf I_s\mathbf E\mathbf x$ satisfy
$$
\alpha=\frac5{5+2M},\qquad M=\frac{K\gamma_1}{\mu a}.
$$
The <Marangoni stress> approaches the stress needed to suppress surface motion as $M$ grows. The quadrupolar shape perturbation $r=a(1+\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf D\mathbf n)$ is
$$
\mathbf D=\frac{5\mu a}{\gamma_0}\frac{2+M}{5+2M}\mathbf E.
$$
These expressions require small deformation and small surface <Péclet number>; large $M$ alone does not justify a small concentration perturbation.

= Chemophoresis
{parent=Marangoni effect}

Chemophoresis is the motion of a particle or droplet driven by a gradient in chemical concentration. For a surfactant-coated bubble, adsorption converts the chemical gradient into a <Marangoni stress>.

= Rayleigh–Plateau instability
{c}
{parent=Surface tension}
{wiki}

The Rayleigh–Plateau instability makes a sufficiently long cylindrical fluid thread unstable to axisymmetric radius perturbations. Surface tension lowers area by amplifying wavelengths longer than the circumference-scale threshold.

= Capillary instability of an annular liquid lining
{parent=Rayleigh–Plateau instability}
{title2=$\beta=\frac{\sigma h_0^3}{3\mu}k^2(a^{-2}-k^2)$}

A thin liquid lining inside a rigid cylindrical tube has <pressure> perturbation $p_1=-\sigma(a^{-2}h_1+h_{1,xx})$ at leading order in $h_0/a$. <Lubrication theory> gives flux $q=-h_0^3p_x/(3\mu)$, so <mass conservation> gives the displayed growth rate. Long waves with $0<k^2a^2<1$ grow, while short waves are stabilized by <curvature>. The maximum occurs at $k^2a^2=1/2$.

= Surfactant stabilization of an annular liquid lining
{parent=Capillary instability of an annular liquid lining}
{title2=$\beta^2+q[\alpha+\lambda(q-1)]\beta+\alpha\lambda q^2(q-1)/4=0$}

For <insoluble surfactant> with $\sigma=\sigma_0-A\Gamma$ and positive base tension, let $q=k^2a^2$, $\lambda=h_0^3(\sigma_0-A\Gamma_0)/(3\mu a^4)$ and $\alpha=h_0A\Gamma_0/(\mu a^2)$. The coupled film-height and advected-<surfactant> amplitudes give the displayed growth equation. The unstable band remains $0<q<1$. Weak <surfactant> elasticity gives $\beta_{\max}=\lambda/4-3\alpha/8+O(\alpha^2/\lambda)$; strong elasticity gives $\beta_{\max}\sim\lambda/16$. The <Marangoni stress> reduces tangential surface motion; strong elasticity reduces lubrication mobility to the value for an effectively immobile interface.

= Young–Laplace equation
{c}
{parent=Surface tension}
{wiki}
{title2=$\Delta p=\gamma\kappa$}

The <Young–Laplace equation> relates the <pressure> jump across a stationary interface to its <curvature> and <surface tension>. Choose a unit <normal vector> $\mathbf n$ pointing from the inside region to the outside region, and define $\kappa=\nabla_s\cdot\mathbf n$ to be positive for a convex <sphere>. In mechanical <equilibrium>, with constant <surface tension> and no additional normal <viscous stresses>,
$$
\Delta p=p_{\mathrm{in}}-p_{\mathrm{out}}
=\gamma\kappa=\gamma(k_1+k_2).
$$
Here $k_1,k_2$ are the signed <principal curvatures> in this convention. Their sum is twice the corresponding signed <mean curvature>; stating the convention prevents a factor-of-two ambiguity. The <Young–Laplace equation> is a local <normal stress> balance for one interface. https://arxiv.org/html/2011.11403v2[Tomas Bohr and Bernhard Scheichl, Surface Tension and Energy Conservation in a Moving Fluid, equations (8)–(9)]{ref}

A spherical <liquid> droplet of <radius> $R$ in a <gas> has one interface, with $k_1=k_2=1/R$. An ideal thin soap bubble has two <liquid>–<gas> interfaces. Adding their <pressure> jumps gives
$$
\Delta p_{\mathrm{droplet}}=\frac{2\gamma}{R},\qquad
\Delta p_{\mathrm{soap\ bubble}}\simeq\frac{4\gamma}{R}.
$$
For the soap bubble, $\Delta p$ now means the inner <gas> <pressure> minus the outer <gas> <pressure>. The second formula assumes equal <surface tensions> on both faces and film thickness negligible relative to $R$, so their radii can be identified. Retaining distinct radii gives $2\gamma/R_{\mathrm{inner}}+2\gamma/R_{\mathrm{outer}}$. A <gas> bubble inside bulk <liquid> has only one interface and therefore uses $2\gamma/R$. https://openstax.org/books/college-physics-2e/pages/11-8-cohesion-and-adhesion-in-liquids-surface-tension-and-capillary-action[OpenStax, College Physics 2e, §11.8: pressure in a soap bubble]{ref}

With the assumed constant toy <surface tension> $\gamma=0.072\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}}$, a droplet with $R=1\,\mathrm{mm}$ has excess <pressure> $144\,\mathrm{Pa}$; at $R=0.5\,\mathrm{mm}$ it has $288\,\mathrm{Pa}$. A thin soap bubble assigned the same $\gamma$ and $R=1\,\mathrm{mm}$ has excess <pressure> $288\,\mathrm{Pa}$. These are differences from the surrounding <pressure>, rather than absolute <pressures>. Halving the <radius> doubles the <capillary pressure>; doubling the interface count also doubles it at fixed <radius> and <surface tension>.

\Image[surface-tension-droplet-pressure.png]
{title=Smaller droplets need larger excess pressure; two bubble interfaces double it}
{description=Left: a virtual outward displacement $dR$ of a <sphere> balances <pressure> <work> against increased <surface energy>. Right: excess <pressure> in pascals versus positive <radius> in millimetres for one and two interfaces, using the assumed constant toy $\gamma=0.072\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}}$.}

The spherical case has a short <work> argument. A virtual increase in <radius> gives $dV=4\pi R^2\,dR$ and $dA=8\pi R\,dR$. At mechanical <equilibrium>, the net <pressure> <work> balances the increase in <surface energy>:
$$
\underbrace{\Delta p\,4\pi R^2\,dR}_{\text{pressure work}}
=\underbrace{\gamma\,8\pi R\,dR}_{\text{surface energy increase}}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad \Delta p=\frac{2\gamma}{R}.
$$
For a thin soap bubble, both interfaces gain approximately the same <surface area>, doubling the right-hand side and giving $4\gamma/R$. This is a virtual <work> balance between neighboring spherical configurations, rather than an assumption that a fixed-<volume> droplet actually expands. The general <Young–Laplace equation> replaces the spherical area-to-<volume> ratio by local <curvature>, making geometry directly measurable through <pressure>.

= Gibbs--Thomson relation
{c}
{parent=Young–Laplace equation}
{wiki}

= Gibbs-Thomson effect
{c}
{synonym}

The Gibbs--Thomson relation shifts the equilibrium chemical potential or composition at a curved interface by an amount proportional to its mean curvature. Small droplets therefore have a larger equilibrium solubility than flat interfaces.

= Curvature-induced melting-temperature depression
{parent=Gibbs--Thomson relation}
{title2=$T_I=T_m-\gamma T_m\mathcal K/(\rho L)$}

Curvature depresses the equilibrium <melting> <temperature> of solid convex into liquid. Here $\mathcal K$ is the sum of <principal curvatures>, equal to twice a common convention for <mean curvature>, and $T_m$ is absolute <temperature>. The coefficient uses <solid-liquid surface energy>, <mass density> and <latent heat>. This stabilizes fine corrugations of a <solidification> front.

= Capillary length
{title2=$\ell_c=\sqrt{\gamma/(\rho g)}$}
{parent=Surface tension}
{wiki}

The <capillary length> measures when <gravity> and <surface tension> are comparably important in shaping a <liquid> interface. For a <liquid> of uniform <mass density> $\rho$, beneath a <gas> whose <mass density> is negligible, with uniform <gravitational acceleration> $g>0$ and constant <surface tension> $\gamma>0$,
$$
\ell_c=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma}{\rho g}}.
$$
A deformation of height $a$ and horizontal length scale $L$ has <hydrostatic pressure> variation of order $\rho ga$ and small-slope <capillary pressure> of order $\gamma a/L^2$. Equating these gives $L\sim\ell_c$: <surface tension> smooths small features, whereas <gravity> matters for broad ones. For two resting <fluids>, replace $\rho$ by their positive <mass density> difference $\Delta\rho$ in this static balance. This change alone does not give the two-fluid <wave> dynamics, because both <fluids> can have inertia. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-357-interfacial-phenomena-fall-2010/04759cfc15c79928ca9f72a18b3864dd_MIT18_357F10_lec_all.pdf[John W. M. Bush, Interfacial Phenomena, chapters 5–6: curvature and hydrostatic pressure]{ref}

With the stipulated illustrative parameters $\gamma=0.072\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}}$, $\rho=1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, and $g=9.81\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$, the <capillary length> is $\ell_c=2.71\,\mathrm{mm}$. These parameters define the ideal calculations below; they are not a measurement of a particular sample.

\Image[capillary-waves-crossover.png]
{title=The <gravity-capillary wave> crossover occurs at <wavelength> $2\pi\ell_c$, where <phase speed> is smallest}
{description=Calculated <phase velocity> and <group velocity> magnitudes, and the ratio of the <surface tension> and <gravity> terms in the deep-water <dispersion relation>, for the stipulated $\gamma$, $\rho$ and $g$. The <capillary length> is $2.71\,\mathrm{mm}$, while the equality <wavelength> is $17.02\,\mathrm{mm}$. Both panels show ideal theory, without experimental data.}

For a <sinusoid> with <wavenumber> $k>0$, the relevant horizontal scale is $L=1/k$, while its <wavelength> is $\lambda=2\pi/k$. Thus the <Bond number> for this scale and the reciprocal restoring-term ratio are
$$
\mathrm{Bo}_{1/k}=\frac{\rho g}{\gamma k^2}
=\frac{1}{(k\ell_c)^2}
=\left(\frac{\lambda}{2\pi\ell_c}\right)^2,
\qquad
\frac{\text{capillary term}}{\text{gravity term}}
=(k\ell_c)^2.
$$
Equality is at $k_c=1/\ell_c$, hence $\lambda_c=2\pi\ell_c=17.02\,\mathrm{mm}$ for these parameters. Using $L=\lambda$ instead produces a different numerical <Bond number>; state the length convention. The <capillary length> itself is not the equality <wavelength>. This distinction helps choose whether a measured ripple should be modeled as a <surface gravity wave>, a nearly pure <capillary wave>, or a <gravity-capillary wave>. https://davidtong.org/pdfs/teaching/fluid-mechanics/fluids3.pdf[David Tong, Fluid Mechanics, §4.1.3, equations (4.23)–(4.24) and the wavelength factor $2\pi$]{ref}

= Dynamic meniscus
{parent=Surface tension}
{wiki}

A dynamic meniscus is a curved transition region joining a moving thin film to a bulk fluid reservoir.

= Capillary wave
{parent=Surface tension}
{wiki}

A <capillary wave> is a <wave> along a <fluid> interface whose restoring stress is predominantly supplied by <surface tension>. A curved <free surface> requires a <pressure> jump by the <Young–Laplace equation>; increasing its corrugation also costs <surface energy>. In the ideal limit, inertia carries the interface through its flat position and the resulting <oscillation> propagates. When <gravity> also contributes appreciably, use the combined <gravity-capillary wave> model. Within the <capillary wave> regime, shortening a ripple makes it travel faster. https://davidtong.org/pdfs/teaching/fluid-mechanics/fluids3.pdf[David Tong, Fluid Mechanics, §4.1.3: the capillary restoring pressure]{ref}

The <laser-diffraction measurement of capillary waves> below turns this mechanism into a test: set the <frequency>, measure the <wavelength> independently with <light>, and ask whether the <dispersion relation> follows. The theory is useful both for predicting how ripple <wave packets> separate and for inferring <surface tension> from their motion.

= Gravity-capillary wave
{parent=Capillary wave}
{title2=$\omega^2=gk+(\gamma/\rho)k^3$}

= Capillary-gravity wave
{synonym}

A <gravity-capillary wave> has restoring contributions from both <gravity> and <surface tension>. Consider an initially resting, horizontally unbounded <liquid> layer of constant depth $h$, with uniform <mass density> $\rho$, <incompressible flow>, <inviscid flow> and <irrotational flow>. The upper <gas> exerts constant <pressure> and has negligible inertia. Assume a clean interface with constant <surface tension> $\gamma$: no <surfactant> stresses, imposed current or wind. The <wave amplitude> $a$ must satisfy $ka\ll1$ and $a\ll h$, so <linearized boundary conditions> apply. Deep water means $kh\gg1$, rather than a particular depth in metres. https://www.ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-354j-nonlinear-dynamics-ii-continuum-systems-spring-2015/6163837c5efa5d6ed969e535ec8f890c_MIT18_354JS15_lectureNotes.pdf[Brenner, Peacock, Stocker, Reis and Dunkel, MIT Nonlinear Dynamics II, §23.1, pp. 105–106]{ref}

Let $z=0$ be the undisturbed <free surface>, with <liquid> below it. <Potential flow> gives a <velocity potential> $\phi$ with $\mathbf u=\nabla\phi$, and <incompressible flow> gives the <Laplace equation>. The linearized <kinematic boundary condition for a free-surface graph> and the <unsteady Bernoulli equation>, including <gravity> and the <Young–Laplace equation>, give
$$
\phi_{xx}+\phi_{zz}=0\quad(z<0),
\qquad
\eta_t=\phi_z,
\qquad
\phi_t+g\eta-\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\eta_{xx}=0\quad(z=0).
$$
Here $\eta(x,t)$ is the interface displacement; the signed small-slope <pressure> jump is $p_{\mathrm{liquid}}-p_{\mathrm{gas}}=-\gamma\eta_{xx}$. A <wave crest> has $\eta_{xx}<0$, so its <capillary pressure> is positive. Choose a right-travelling <Fourier mode> of positive <wavenumber> $k$ and <angular frequency> $\omega$:
$$
\eta=a\cos(kx-\omega t),
\qquad
\phi=\frac{a\omega}{k}e^{kz}\sin(kx-\omega t).
$$
The factor $e^{kz}$ decays into the deep <liquid>, satisfies the <Laplace equation>, and makes $\phi_z=\eta_t$ at $z=0$. Substituting in the dynamic <boundary condition> leaves
$$
-\frac{a\omega^2}{k}+ga+\frac{\gamma}{\rho}k^2a=0,
\qquad
\boxed{\omega^2=gk+\frac{\gamma}{\rho}k^3}.
$$
This derivation displays the two restoring contributions with the same sign. <Dimensional analysis> also checks the result: $\gamma/\rho$ has units $\mathrm{m^3\,s^{-2}}$, so both $gk$ and $(\gamma/\rho)k^3$ have units $\mathrm{s^{-2}}$. https://www.ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-354j-nonlinear-dynamics-ii-continuum-systems-spring-2015/6163837c5efa5d6ed969e535ec8f890c_MIT18_354JS15_lectureNotes.pdf[MIT Nonlinear Dynamics II, §23.1, equations (547)–(551): boundary conditions and dispersion]{ref}

At finite depth, a flat impermeable bottom at $z=-h$ requires $\phi_z=0$. Replacing $e^{kz}$ by the normalized vertical factor $\cosh(k(z+h))/\cosh(kh)$ makes the surface ratio $\phi_z/\phi=k\tanh(kh)$. Eliminating the mode amplitudes then gives
$$
\omega^2=\left(gk+\frac{\gamma}{\rho}k^3\right)\tanh(kh).
$$
Thus the same model supplies its own depth check: use the deep-water formula only when $\tanh(kh)$ is sufficiently close to one for the required accuracy. For example, $kh=3$ reduces $\omega$ by about $0.248\%$ relative to deep water, while $kh=1$ reduces it by about $12.7\%$. These percentages are calculations from this factor. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-357-interfacial-phenomena-fall-2010/04759cfc15c79928ca9f72a18b3864dd_MIT18_357F10_lec_all.pdf[John W. M. Bush, Interfacial Phenomena, chapter 19, equation (19.4)]{ref}

In deep water the <phase velocity> magnitude follows a <wave crest>, while the <group velocity> magnitude follows the <wave envelope> of a narrow <wave packet>. Differentiating the positive-frequency <dispersion relation> gives
$$
c_p=\frac{\omega}{k}=\sqrt{\frac{g}{k}+\frac{\gamma k}{\rho}},
\qquad
c_g=\frac{d\omega}{dk}
=\frac{g+3\gamma k^2/\rho}{2\sqrt{gk+\gamma k^3/\rho}},
\qquad
\frac{c_g}{c_p}=\frac{1+3(k\ell_c)^2}{2\left(1+(k\ell_c)^2\right)}.
$$
For $k\ell_c\ll1$, the <surface gravity wave> limit has $c_g\simeq c_p/2$. For $k\ell_c\gg1$, the <deep-water capillary-wave dispersion relation> has $c_g\simeq3c_p/2$: a <wave crest> slips backward relative to the faster <wave envelope>, even while both advance. Neither speed is the oscillating <fluid element>'s own <velocity>. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-357-interfacial-phenomena-fall-2010/04759cfc15c79928ca9f72a18b3864dd_MIT18_357F10_lec_all.pdf[John W. M. Bush, Interfacial Phenomena, chapter 19, equation (19.5) and the two group-speed limits]{ref}

There is a particularly neat coincidence at the crossover. Differentiate $c_p^2=g/k+\gamma k/\rho$: its unique minimum is at $k_c=\sqrt{\rho g/\gamma}=1/\ell_c$, exactly where the restoring terms are equal. Therefore
$$
c_{p,\min}=\left(\frac{4g\gamma}{\rho}\right)^{1/4},
\qquad c_g(k_c)=c_p(k_c).
$$
For the stipulated parameters in <capillary length>, the minimum is $0.231\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. At $\lambda=3\,\mathrm{mm}$, direct substitution gives $f=\omega/(2\pi)\simeq131\,\mathrm{Hz}$, $c_p\simeq0.394\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ and $c_g\simeq0.580\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. This short <wavelength> is capillary dominated, but its <group velocity> is still slightly below the pure-capillary ratio $3c_p/2$ because <gravity> has not vanished.

= Laser-diffraction measurement of capillary waves
{parent=Capillary wave}

Zhu, Miao, Xu and Cao's 2007 experiment drove a triangular metal frame electromagnetically with a sinusoidal generator. In a roughly $3\,\mathrm{cm}$-deep, temperature-regulated pool, it produced nearly plane <capillary waves>. A $632.8\,\mathrm{nm}$ <laser> illuminated the surface obliquely; the moving ripples acted as a reflection <diffraction grating>. One split beam monitored laser stability, while a <CCD> recorded <diffraction orders> on a screen $6.76\,\mathrm m$ away. An air-supported optical table reduced ambient vibration. https://www.eg.bucknell.edu/~phys310/skills/papers/paperX_full.pdf[Zhu et al., Measurement of the dispersion relation of capillary waves by laser diffraction, American Journal of Physics 75 (2007), §II.A, p. 896; DOI 10.1119/1.2750379]{ref}

\Image[capillary-waves-diffraction.png]
{title=Drive a <capillary wave> at known <frequency> and measure its <wavelength> by <laser> <diffraction>}
{description=An original schematic of excitation and optical detection, with ray angles and surface displacement exaggerated for readability. The right panel calculates the deep-water <dispersion relation> for the stipulated parameters in <capillary length>; the $100$–$290\,\mathrm{Hz}$ band identifies a drive range to compare with the documented experiment. No diffraction intensities, measured points or fitted <surface tensions> are plotted.}

The <frequency> sweep was $100$–$290\,\mathrm{Hz}$ in $10\,\mathrm{Hz}$ steps, using distilled <water> and kerosene at $20\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$. For each setting they averaged at least three measurements of first-order spot separation. Known optical geometry and the <grating equation> yielded $k=2\pi/\lambda$; generator readings yielded $\omega=2\pi f$. Agreement with the predicted log–log slope $3/2$ tests the capillary law, and its intercept gives <surface tension> if <mass density> is known. The authors identify surface contamination as a major error source; noncontact optical detection reduces additional disturbance. https://www.eg.bucknell.edu/~phys310/skills/papers/paperX_full.pdf[Zhu et al., §§II.B–III, equations (6)–(8), pp. 897–898]{ref}

The optical measurement can be understood from the <grating equation>. Let $\lambda_L$ be the <laser> <wavelength>, $\alpha$ the incident grazing <angle> above the mean surface, and $\beta_m$ the outgoing grazing <angle> of <diffraction order> $m$, with the sign of $m$ chosen so that
$$
\lambda\left(\cos\alpha-\cos\beta_m\right)=m\lambda_L.
$$
Thus calibrated spot positions determine outgoing <angles>, which determine the ripple <wavelength> $\lambda$, independently of the mechanical <dispersion relation>. This optical geometry treats the surface as a reflection <diffraction grating> at the <laser> <wavelength>. https://web.mit.edu/8.13/www/JLExperiments/JLExp001.pdf[MIT Department of Physics, Introductory Optics, §III.1, equation (4), p. 3: grazing-angle reflection grating]{ref}

The inferred <surface tension> in the full deep-water model is
$$
\gamma=\rho\frac{\omega^2-gk}{k^3}.
$$
This formula makes useful experimental controls explicit. Measure <mass density> at the sample's <temperature>; check depth with $kh$; keep the driven <wave amplitude> small enough that $ka\ll1$; and distinguish $f$ from <angular frequency> $\omega$. For the stipulated parameters, the $100$–$290\,\mathrm{Hz}$ window corresponds to calculated <wavelengths> about $3.62$–$1.76\,\mathrm{mm}$ and $kh\simeq52$–$107$ at $h=30\,\mathrm{mm}$. Depth corrections are negligible here, but the <gravity> contribution is about $4.5\%$–$1.1\%$ of the capillary contribution. These are ideal predictions, not digitized results from the experiment.

Omitting <gravity> would infer $\gamma_{\mathrm{cap}}=\rho\omega^2/k^3=\gamma+\rho g/k^2$, biasing <surface tension> upward. In the capillary limit, the differential estimate $d\gamma/\gamma\simeq d\rho/\rho+2\,d\omega/\omega-3\,dk/k$ also shows why careful spot-spacing calibration matters. A clean surface and weak <viscosity> are modeling assumptions: appreciable <damping>, <surfactant> stresses or large <wave amplitudes> call for additional physics. The experiment tests the short-wave regime; its drive window does not establish the crossover or the long-wave <surface gravity wave> limit.

= Deep-water capillary-wave dispersion relation
{title2=$\omega^2=(T/\rho)|k|^3$}
{parent=Capillary wave}

For a capillary wave on deep water with surface tension $T$ and density $\rho$, the dispersion relation is
$$
\omega(k)=S|k|^{3/2},
\qquad
S=\sqrt{T/\rho}.
$$
Its group velocity is $(3S/2)|k|^{1/2}$ in the direction of the wavevector.

= Surface response to a localized impulsive velocity
{parent=Deep-water capillary-wave dispersion relation}

If an initially flat one-dimensional surface receives velocity $-W$ on $|x|<\epsilon$ and zero velocity elsewhere, its linear capillary-wave response is
$$
\eta(x,t)=-\frac{2W}{\pi S}
\int_0^\infty
\frac{\sin(k\epsilon)\sin(Sk^{3/2}t)\cos(kx)}{k^{5/2}}\,dk.
$$

= Stationary-phase wake of an impulsive capillary wave
{parent=Surface response to a localized impulsive velocity}

Along $x=Vt>0$, if $\epsilon V^2/S^2\ll1$, the stationary wavenumber $k_0=4V^2/(9S^2)$ obeys $k_0\epsilon\ll1$. The <one-dimensional stationary-phase formula> then gives
$$
\eta(x,t)\sim
\frac{W\epsilon S t^2}{x^{5/2}}
\frac9{2\sqrt\pi}
\sin\left(\frac{4x^3}{27S^2t^2}-\frac\pi4\right).
$$
Only the integrated initial velocity $-2W\epsilon$ survives in this long-wave far field.

= Diffusion-limited relaxation of an interface
{parent=Capillary wave}

For a conserved scalar order parameter, a sinusoidal interface perturbation of wavenumber $q$ creates a harmonic chemical-potential field extending a distance $|q|^{-1}$ into each phase. Surface tension then produces a relaxation rate proportional to $-|q|^3$.

= Body force
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

A <body force> acts throughout a body's or <fluid>'s <volume>, rather than only through its surface. <Gravity> is the example used in <hydrostatic pressure>: a <gravitational field> $\mathbf g$ gives <force> per unit <volume> $\rho\mathbf g$, where $\rho$ is the <mass density>. More generally, a <body force> per unit <mass> $\mathbf f$ contributes $\rho\mathbf f$ to <momentum conservation>. <Forces> from <electromagnetism> are another example. Surface <pressure> <forces> produce <buoyancy> on an immersed body, while its <body force> from <gravity> acts downward.

= Elastic force dipole
{parent=Body force}
{title2=$f_i=-D e_i e_j\partial_j\delta(\mathbf x-\mathbf y)$}

Opposite forces separated along the unit direction $\mathbf e$, with fixed force times separation $D$, have this distributional <body force> limit. Its net force is zero. A symmetric <seismic moment tensor> is a sum of such dipoles along its principal directions.

= Hydrostatic pressure
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{title2=$dp/dz=-\rho_fg$}
{wiki}

<Hydrostatic pressure> is the <pressure> distribution in a resting <fluid>, balancing its <body force> from <gravity>. Take $z$ upward and a uniform downward <gravitational field> $\mathbf g=-g\mathbf e_z$, where $g>0$. The vertical <pressure gradient> satisfies
$$
\frac{dp}{dz}=-\rho_f g.
$$
A short derivation balances the vertical <forces> on a thin horizontal layer of <area> $A$ and thickness $\Delta z$: $p(z)A-p(z+\Delta z)A-\rho_fgA\Delta z=0$. Dividing by $A\Delta z$ and taking the <derivative> gives the equation above. For constant <mass density> $\rho_f$, <integration> from the surface at $z_s$ gives
$$
p(z)=p_{\mathrm{surface}}+\rho_fg(z_s-z)
=p_{\mathrm{surface}}+\rho_fgh,\qquad h=z_s-z.
$$
Thus <pressure> increases with depth $h$. For the stipulated <water> with <mass density> $1000\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$ and <gravitational acceleration> $9.81\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$, depths $0.20\ \mathrm m$ and $0.30\ \mathrm m$ give <gauge pressures> $1962\ \mathrm{Pa}$ and $2943\ \mathrm{Pa}$. Their difference produces the <buoyancy> of the example <cube>. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_40.html#Ch40-S1[Feynman Lectures, II §40-1: hydrostatics]{ref}

= Gauge pressure
{parent=Hydrostatic pressure}
{title2=$p_g=p-p_{\mathrm{atmosphere}}$}

<Gauge pressure> is <pressure> measured relative to the surrounding <atmospheric pressure>; absolute <pressure> is instead measured relative to an ideal <vacuum>. At an open surface $p_{\mathrm{surface}}=p_{\mathrm{atmosphere}}$, so the constant-<mass density> <hydrostatic pressure> law becomes $p_g=\rho_fgh$. Here $p_g=0$ at the surface denotes equality with <atmospheric pressure>. It does not denote zero absolute <pressure>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/14-2-measuring-pressure[OpenStax, University Physics, §14.2: gauge and absolute pressure]{ref}

= Hydrostatic approximation
{parent=Hydrostatic pressure}
{wiki}

The hydrostatic approximation neglects vertical acceleration relative to gravity and vertical pressure gradients. It reduces vertical momentum balance to $p_z=-\rho g$.

= Hydrostatic balance
{parent=Hydrostatic approximation}
{title2=$p_z=-\rho g$}

Vertical <pressure gradient> balances gravitational force per unit volume. Subtracting the resting hydrostatic state and using <kinematic pressure> gives $p'_z=b'$ for a <Boussinesq approximation> buoyancy anomaly. The approximation neglects vertical <acceleration>; this diagnostic relation is what couples <buoyancy perturbation> to the vertical pressure profile.

= Volumetric flow rate
{title2=$Q$}
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

= Volume flux
{synonym}

The volumetric flow rate is the volume of fluid crossing a section per unit time, $Q=\int_A\mathbf u\mathbin\cdot\mathbf n\,dA$.

= Mass flow rate
{parent=Volumetric flow rate}
{title2=$\dot M=\int_S\rho\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf n\,dS$}
{wiki}

The mass flow rate through an oriented cross-section is the integral of <mass density> times normal <velocity>. For constant density it is density times the <volumetric flow rate>. In a steady pipe without leaks, <conservation of mass> makes this rate independent of cross-section.

= Volume flux per unit width
{title2=$Q$}
{parent=Volumetric flow rate}

For a flow invariant in one horizontal direction, the <volume flux> per unit width is the <velocity> integrated across the flow depth. It has dimensions of area per unit time.

= Discharge coefficient
{title2=$C_d$}
{parent=Volumetric flow rate}
{wiki}

A discharge coefficient multiplies an ideal inviscid opening-flow rate to represent contraction and dissipative losses. Its value depends on the opening geometry and flow regime.

= Potential flow
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

Potential flow is irrotational flow represented as the gradient of a scalar potential.

= Corner sink in a semi-infinite channel
{parent=Potential flow}
{title2=$\psi=-my-\frac{2m}{\pi}\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{e^{-n\pi x}\sin(n\pi y)}n$}

For unit channel width, <incompressible flow> towards a corner withdrawal point has $\psi=0$ on the lower wall and $\psi=-m$ on the upper and left walls, with $\psi\to-my$ far downstream. For <irrotational flow>, <separation of variables> gives the displayed harmonic correction: the Fourier sine coefficients of $-m(1-y)$ are $-2m/(n\pi)$. Near the corner, $\psi\sim-2m\theta/\pi$ and radial velocity is $-2m/(\pi r)$, whose integrated inward flux across a quarter-circle is $m$.

= Rankine half-body
{c}
{parent=Potential flow}
{title2=$b=Q/(2U)$}

The region bounded by the dividing <streamlines> of a two-dimensional <point source> superposed on uniform flow. Its stagnation point is $-Q/(2\pi U)$ upstream and its far downstream width is $Q/U$. The boundary encloses fluid originating at the source rather than a rigid obstacle; the upstream stagnation point is reached only asymptotically by the upstream source trajectory.

= Radial source flow
{parent=Potential flow}
{title2=$u_r=Q/(2\pi r)$}

A two-dimensional incompressible line source with area flux $Q$ has radial <velocity> $Q/(2\pi r)$, obtained from flux conservation around a circle. It is irrotational away from the source, with potential $(Q/(2\pi))\ln r$. For a circular expanding interface, $Q=2\pi aa_t$ and $u_r=aa_t/r$, whose radial strain is $-aa_t/r^2$. That strain supplies a normal viscous stress even though the <velocity> is a potential flow.

= Potential dipole
{parent=Potential flow}
{title2=$\phi=\mathbf d\cdot\mathbf x/r^3$}

A potential dipole in three dimensions is the <potential flow> $\mathbf u=\nabla\phi$ with $\phi=\mathbf d\cdot\mathbf x/r^3$. Away from the origin its potential is a <harmonic function>, so it is also a constant-<pressure> <Stokes flow>. Its <velocity> decays as $r^{-3}$ and its <vorticity> is zero. It is distinct from a <stresslet>, whose leading <velocity> decays as $r^{-2}$.

= Point source
{parent=Potential flow}

A two-dimensional point source of strength $q$ has velocity potential $\phi=q\log r/(2\pi)$ and outward velocity $q\mathbf e_r/(2\pi r)$. Every closed contour enclosing it has volume flux $q$.

= Source dipole
{parent=Point source}
{title2=$\mathbf u=[3(\mathbf M\cdot\mathbf r)\mathbf r/r^5-\mathbf M/r^3]/(4\pi)$}

= Source doublet
{synonym}

A source dipole is the limit of an equal source and sink brought together with their source-strength times separation vector fixed at $\mathbf M$. Differentiating the <three-dimensional point source> field gives $\mathbf u=-(\mathbf M\cdot\nabla)[\mathbf r/(4\pi r^3)]$. It has <velocity potential> $-\mathbf M\cdot\mathbf r/(4\pi r^3)$, zero total <volume flux>, and $r^{-3}$ far-field decay. Unlike a <stresslet>, it is an irrotational potential singularity rather than a <force-dipole flow>.

= Three-dimensional point source
{parent=Point source}
{title2=$\mathbf u=Q\mathbf r/(4\pi r^3)$}

A point injection of volume at rate $Q$ produces the displayed radial <potential flow> in three dimensions. Its <velocity potential> is $-Q/(4\pi r)$, and every enclosing <sphere> has outward <volume flux> $Q$. The <divergence> vanishes away from the source and is $Q$ times the three-dimensional <Dirac delta> distribution at it. The field is also a <Stokes flow> with constant <pressure> away from its singularity.

= Hydrodynamic attraction of a plane wall to a point source
{parent=Point source}
{title2=$F=\rho Q^2/(16\pi a^2)$}

An incompressible <point source> of volume <flux> $Q$ at height $a$ above an impermeable plane has <velocity potential> $\phi=-Q(1/r_++1/r_-)/(4\pi)$, with an equal image source below the plane. On the wall, $u_s=Qs/[2\pi(s^2+a^2)^{3/2}]$. The <Bernoulli equation> gives $p-p_\infty=-\rho Q^2s^2/[8\pi^2(s^2+a^2)^3]$. Integrating the <pressure> deficit over the plane gives the displayed attraction force relative to ambient <pressure>. If source strength instead denotes the coefficient $Q/(4\pi)$ of radial inverse-square velocity, the force is $\pi\rho m^2/a^2$.

= Sink flow in a sector
{parent=Potential flow}

A line sink of strength $\alpha Q$ at the vertex of a sector $0<\theta<\alpha$ produces the irrotational outer flow
$$
u_r=-\frac Qr,
\qquad u_\theta=0,
\qquad \psi=-Q\theta.
$$
It satisfies no penetration at the radial walls but violates no slip there, so viscous boundary layers form along both walls.

= Similarity solution for a sink-flow boundary layer
{parent=Sink flow in a sector}

Near a radial wall, let $x$ measure distance from the sink along the wall and $y$ point into the fluid. With
$$
\delta=x\sqrt{\frac\nu Q},
\qquad
\eta=\frac y\delta,
\qquad
\psi=\sqrt{\nu Q}\,f(\eta),
$$
the <Prandtl boundary-layer equation> reduces to
$$
f'''=1-(f')^2,
\qquad
f(0)=f'(0)=0,
\qquad
f'(\infty)=-1.
$$
One physical solution has
$$
f'(\eta)=\frac{5-\cosh(\sqrt2\eta+c)}{1+\cosh(\sqrt2\eta+c)},
\qquad
c=\operatorname{arcosh}5.
$$

= Potential flow around a translating sphere
{parent=Potential flow}

For a sphere of radius $a$ translating at speed $U$ along the polar axis through fluid at rest at infinity, the laboratory-frame potential is
$$
\phi=-\frac{Ua^3}{2r^2}\cos\theta.
$$
It gives $u_r=Ua^3\cos\theta/r^3$ and $u_\theta=Ua^3\sin\theta/(2r^3)$.

= Spherically symmetric incompressible radial flow
{parent=Potential flow}

For radial flow outside a sphere, <incompressible flow> makes
$$
Q(t)=4\pi r^2u_r(r,t)
$$
independent of radius. Its velocity potential is $\phi=-Q/(4\pi r)$.

= Rayleigh equation for an inviscid spherical bubble
{c}
{parent=Spherically symmetric incompressible radial flow}
{title2=$R\ddot R+3\dot R^2/2=(p_g-p_\infty)/\rho$}

In radial <potential flow> outside a spherical bubble, <incompressibility> and the moving-boundary condition give $u_r=R^2\dot R/r^2$ and $\phi=-R^2\dot R/r$. The <unsteady Bernoulli equation>, with its gauge fixed at infinity, yields the displayed radius equation when surface tension and viscosity are neglected. Pressure continuity at the interface identifies the liquid pressure with the gas pressure $p_g$. The vacuum special case is <Rayleigh collapse of a spherical cavity>.

= Pressure-work energy balance for a spherical bubble
{parent=Rayleigh equation for an inviscid spherical bubble}
{title2=$K=2\pi\rho R^3\dot R^2,\quad\dot K=(p_b-p_\infty)\dot V$}

For spherical expansion in an unbounded <incompressible flow>, continuity gives $u_r=R^2\dot R/r^2$. Integrating the <kinetic energy> outside the bubble gives $K=2\pi\rho R^3\dot R^2$. The <Rayleigh equation for an inviscid spherical bubble> then yields $\dot K=(p_b-p_\infty)\dot V$, where $V=4\pi R^3/3$. With $p_b=p_\infty V_0/V$ and constant $p_\infty$, integration gives $K-K_0=p_\infty[V_0\log(V/V_0)-V+V_0]$. This neglects <viscosity>, <surface tension> and external body-force differences, which contribute additional terms in other bubble models.

= First integral of polytropic spherical-bubble motion
{parent=Rayleigh equation for an inviscid spherical bubble}
{title2=$R^3\dot R^2=\frac2\rho\int_{R_0}^R s^2[p_g(s)-p_\infty]ds$}

For gas pressure depending only on radius and initial speed zero, multiplying the <Rayleigh equation for an inviscid spherical bubble> by $2R^2\dot R$ integrates to the displayed identity. A polytropic pressure $p_g=K/R^{3\kappa}$ gives elementary power integrals when $\kappa\ne1$, and a logarithm when $\kappa=1$. Turning radii are the positive zeros of this first integral. At $\kappa=2$ and $p_g(R_0)=p_\infty/2$, they are $R_0$ and $R_0/2^{1/3}$; the inward and outward accelerations at them have opposite signs, giving bounded undamped oscillation in the <ideal> model.

= Rayleigh-Plesset equation
{c}
{parent=Spherically symmetric incompressible radial flow}
{title2=$R\ddot R+\tfrac32\dot R^2=(p_B-p_\infty-2\sigma/R-4\mu\dot R/R)/\rho$}
{wiki=Rayleigh–Plesset_equation}

The <Rayleigh-Plesset equation> balances the radial inertia of an incompressible liquid around a spherical bubble against internal and ambient <pressure>, <surface tension> and <viscous stress>. Removing the last two effects and setting the bubble pressure to zero gives the following vacuum-collapse case.

= Rayleigh collapse of a spherical cavity
{parent=Rayleigh-Plesset equation}
{c}

A vacuum cavity of radius $a(t)$ in an infinite inviscid incompressible fluid of density $\rho$ and far-field pressure $p_0$ obeys
$$
a\ddot a+\frac32\dot a^2=-\frac{p_0}{\rho}.
$$
If $a(0)=a_0$ and $\dot a(0)=0$, its collapsing branch satisfies
$$
\dot a=-\sqrt{\frac{2p_0}{3\rho}
\left(\frac{a_0^3}{a^3}-1\right)}.
$$

= Squeezing wedge flow
{parent=Potential flow}
{wiki}

Inviscid flow between closing hinged plates is a quadratic potential flow that expels fluid radially.

= Free surface
{title2=$z=\eta$}
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

A free surface is a fluid boundary whose position is part of the solution and whose traction is prescribed by the adjoining medium. Its motion obeys a <kinematic boundary condition>, while its pressure or stress supplies a dynamic boundary condition.

= Surface gravity wave
{parent=Free surface}

A surface gravity wave is a free-surface oscillation restored by gravity. In inviscid deep water its angular frequency and horizontal wavenumber satisfy $\omega^2=gk$.

= Surface-wave breaking
{parent=Surface gravity wave}

A <surface gravity wave> breaks when its smooth free-surface motion becomes unstable and overturns or forms a strongly dissipative front. In the nondispersive <shallow-water approximation>, intersecting compressive <characteristic curves> signal failure of a smooth <simple wave>; a <hydraulic bore> replaces it at the integral-model level. Dispersion can instead produce an oscillatory bore, while physical breaking involves turbulence and energy loss beyond the inviscid equations.

= Normal modes of surface gravity waves in a rectangular tank
{parent=Surface gravity wave}
{title2=$\omega_{mn}^2=gk_{mn}\tanh(k_{mn}h)$}

For a tank with side lengths $a,b$, depth $h$, impermeable walls and no surface tension, the small-amplitude surface modes have horizontal factors $\cos(m\pi x/a)\cos(n\pi y/b)$ and $k_{mn}^2=(m\pi/a)^2+(n\pi/b)^2$. The velocity potential has vertical factor $\cosh(k_{mn}(z+h))$. Combining the linearized kinematic and dynamic conditions on the undisturbed surface gives $\omega_{mn}^2=gk_{mn}\tanh(k_{mn}h)$. The uniform $m=n=0$ elevation is excluded when the total water volume is fixed.

= Power-law near-shore ray asymptotics
{parent=Surface gravity wave}
{title2=$y-y_0\sim -K\sqrt{g\alpha}x^{1+p/2}/[\omega(1+p/2)]$}

For depth $h=\alpha x^p$ with $p>0$, stationary <surface gravity waves> conserve frequency $\omega$ and alongshore wavenumber $K$. Near shore their dispersion becomes $\omega^2\sim g\kappa^2h$, so $\kappa\sim\omega x^{-p/2}/\sqrt{g\alpha}$. The shoreward ray has $y'\sim-K\sqrt{g\alpha}x^{p/2}/\omega$, giving the displayed integrated scaling. Rays approach normally, while equal-phase crests approach parallel to the shore and their shore-normal spacing shrinks as $x^{p/2}$. These are asymptotics within the ray approximation; for $p<2$, its slow-variation assumption eventually fails arbitrarily close to the endpoint.

= Wave fetch
{parent=Surface gravity wave}

= Open-water fetch
{synonym}

Wave fetch is the distance over which wind acts on an uninterrupted water surface to generate waves. Growth also depends on wind speed and duration. A <polynya> can provide a local fetch inside otherwise extensive <sea ice>.

= Radiation stress
{parent=Surface gravity wave}
{title2=$S_{xx}=\mathcal E(2c_g/c_p-1/2)$}
{wiki}

= Wave radiation stress
{synonym}

The excess depth-integrated, time-averaged <momentum flux> of a <surface gravity wave> is its radiation stress. In deep water $c_g/c_p=1/2$, so $S_{xx}=\mathcal E/2=\rho_wga^2/4$. The force per transverse width on a reflecting object is the incident plus reflected minus transmitted radiation stress. Reflection increases force because the reflected wave carries momentum in the opposite direction.

= Surface-gravity-wave energy
{parent=Surface gravity wave}
{title2=$\mathcal E=\rho_wga^2/2$}

A linear <deep-water gravity wave> of surface-displacement amplitude $a$ has equal mean <kinetic energy> and <potential energy>, and total mean <energy> per horizontal area $\mathcal E=\rho_wga^2/2$. Its energy transport speed is the <group velocity>. This amplitude is half the crest-to-trough height.

= Wave steepness
{parent=Surface gravity wave}
{title2=$ka$}

The dimensionless product of surface-wave amplitude and <wavenumber>, $ka$, measures orbital displacement relative to the wavelength. Small <wave steepness> permits a <Taylor expansion> of the velocity sampled by moving parcels. For a <deep-water gravity wave>, orbital amplitudes and the local steepness decrease exponentially with depth.

= Airy wave theory
{c}
{parent=Surface gravity wave}
{wiki}

<Airy wave theory> linearizes the motion of small-amplitude <surface gravity waves> in an inviscid, incompressible, irrotational fluid. At depth $h$, its dispersion relation is $\omega^2=gk\tanh(kh)$; deep water is the limit $kh\to\infty$.

= Deep-water gravity wave
{parent=Airy wave theory}

A small-amplitude deep-water wave with surface elevation $\eta=Ae^{i(kx-\omega t)}$ has dispersion relation $\omega^2=gk$ and a velocity potential that decays as $e^{kz}$ below the surface.

= Dispersive swell source inversion
{parent=Deep-water gravity wave}
{title2=$t=t_0+4\pi L/(gT)$}

For an impulsive distant source of <deep-water gravity waves>, a component of period $T$ travels at <group velocity> $gT/(4\pi)$. Its arrival at distance $L$ obeys the displayed law, so inverse period increases linearly with arrival time. Two arrivals determine both $L$ and $t_0$. Finite source duration, currents, refraction and passage through <sea ice> can invalidate the simple inversion.

= Viscous decay of a deep-water gravity wave
{parent=Deep-water gravity wave}

For small kinematic viscosity $\nu$, the mean viscous dissipation per unit horizontal area is $2\rho\nu gk^2|A|^2$. Since the mean wave energy is $\rho g|A|^2/2$, the amplitude obeys
$$
\frac{d|A|}{dt}=-2\nu k^2|A|,
\qquad
|A(t)|=|A(0)|e^{-2\nu k^2t}.
$$

= Linearized free-surface boundary conditions
{parent=Deep-water gravity wave}

For small-amplitude potential flow beneath a mean surface $z=0$, the linearized kinematic and dynamic conditions are
$$
\eta_t=\phi_z,
\qquad
\phi_t+g\eta=-\frac{p}{\rho}
\qquad(z=0).
$$

= Rectangular standing surface-gravity mode
{parent=Linearized free-surface boundary conditions}

In a rectangular box with Neumann side walls, a horizontal mode
$$
\cos\frac{m\pi x}{L_x}\cos\frac{n\pi y}{L_y}
$$
has horizontal wavenumber $k=[(m\pi/L_x)^2+(n\pi/L_y)^2]^{1/2}$, depth dependence $e^{kz}$ in infinitely deep fluid, and natural frequency $\Omega=\sqrt{gk}$.

= Resonance of a pressure-forced rectangular surface-gravity mode
{parent=Rectangular standing surface-gravity mode}

Pressure forcing of one rectangular free-surface mode reduces its amplitude to
$$
H''+gkH=-\frac{kp_0}{\rho}\cos(\omega t).
$$
At $\omega=\sqrt{gk}$, the inviscid undamped response contains $t\sin(\omega t)$ and grows without bound in the linear model.

= Streamfunction in polar coordinates
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

For planar incompressible flow, a streamfunction satisfies
$$
u_r=\frac1r\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\theta},
\qquad
u_\theta=-\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial r}.
$$

= Stagnation point
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

A stagnation point of a fluid flow is a point at which the <velocity field> vanishes. For a two-dimensional <streamfunction in polar coordinates>[streamfunction], it is a critical point satisfying both first spatial derivatives of the streamfunction equal to zero.

= Incompressible flow
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}
{title2=$\nabla\cdot\mathbf u=0$}

= Incompressibility
{synonym}

= Incompressibility condition
{synonym}

An <incompressible flow> preserves the <volume> of each moving <fluid element>. Its local condition is zero <velocity divergence>, $\nabla\cdot\mathbf u=0$. Combined with <mass conservation>, it says that each parcel's <mass density> stays unchanged as it moves. This does not require every parcel to have the same <mass density>: an initially nonuniform distribution can be transported without compression. Nor does <incompressibility> require a constant <velocity> or constant <pressure>.

For positive <mass density>, the <continuity equation> makes the equivalence precise:
$$
\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t}+\nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf u)=0,
\qquad
\frac{D\rho}{Dt}+\rho\nabla\cdot\mathbf u=0,
\qquad
\frac{D}{Dt}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+\mathbf u\cdot\nabla.
$$
The <material derivative> follows a <fluid element>. Thus $D\rho/Dt=0$ if and only if the <velocity field> is a <divergence-free vector field>. The distinction between following a parcel and observing a fixed position prevents confusing <incompressibility> with an unchanging <density> field. https://uclnatsci.github.io/2021/Electromagnetism-Fluids-and-Waves/fluids/conservation.html[UCL, Conservation law derivations, sections 30.1–30.2]{ref}

For a rigid pipe with impermeable walls, no leaks and <steady flow>, <mass conservation> at constant <mass density> implies a constant <volumetric flow rate>:
$$
Q=\int_A\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf n\,dA=A\overline U,
\qquad A_1\overline U_1=A_2\overline U_2.
$$
Here $\mathbf n$ points downstream, $A$ is the section's <area> and $\overline U$ is the mean normal <velocity>, not necessarily the centreline speed. No assumption of <inviscid flow> or a uniform <velocity profile> is needed. If unequal amounts entered and left a fixed segment, its <mass> would accumulate, contradicting <steady flow>.

For a worked example, take horizontal water flow with stipulated <mass density> $1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, inlet radius $R_1=0.010\,\mathrm m$, outlet radius $R_2=0.005\,\mathrm m$ and inlet mean <velocity> $\overline U_1=0.20\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. The circular <areas> give
$$
Q=\pi R_1^2\overline U_1=2\pi\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{m^3\,s^{-1}},
\qquad
\overline U_2=\frac{Q}{\pi R_2^2}=0.80\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}.
$$
The radius halves, the <area> falls to one quarter, and the mean <velocity> quadruples. The <mass flow rate> is $\rho Q=0.02\pi\,\mathrm{kg\,s^{-1}}$. The same amount of water passes both sections each second; narrowing changes its speed rather than compressing it. The <Reynolds number> and <Bernoulli's principle> discussions use this same example to examine viscous effects and the additional assumptions needed to calculate <pressure>.

\Image[fluid-flow-constriction.png]
{title=Continuity fixes the mean speeds; Bernoulli's principle gives a conditional ideal pressure drop}
{description=The pipe outline shows the radius ratio. Arrows represent section means, not a computed velocity field. The bars show the constant $Q$ and the ideal exchange between pressure and kinetic energy density at equal height; $p_2$ is the arbitrary pressure reference.}

= Rectilinear flow
{parent=Incompressible flow}

A <rectilinear flow> has velocity everywhere parallel to a fixed direction and independent of the coordinate in that direction, for example $U(y,z)\widehat x$. Its convective acceleration vanishes. A steady incompressible viscous rectilinear flow obeys the linear transverse Poisson equation $\nu\Delta U=\rho^{-1}dp/dx$, rather than an inertia-viscosity balance. This explains Reynolds-number independence of the spatial equation with fixed scaled forcing and boundary data.

= Streamfunction advection bracket
{parent=Incompressible flow}
{title2=$J(f,g)=f_zg_x-f_xg_z$}

For the two-dimensional <streamfunction> convention $u=\psi_z$, $w=-\psi_x$, the advective derivative of $\theta$ is $u\theta_x+w\theta_z=J(\psi,\theta)$. This antisymmetric <Jacobian determinant> bracket organizes nonlinear interactions in convection and vorticity equations.

= Cartesian streamfunction
{title2=$u=\psi_y,\ v=-\psi_x$}
{parent=Incompressible flow}

For a two-dimensional <incompressible flow>, a Cartesian streamfunction $\psi$ defines $u=\partial_y\psi$ and $v=-\partial_x\psi$. The continuity equation $u_x+v_y=0$ then holds identically.

= Divergence-free vector field
{parent=Incompressible flow}

A divergence-free vector field satisfies $\nabla\mathbin\cdot u=0$. It preserves volume under its flow, and its transport operator is skew-symmetric when boundary fluxes vanish.

= Streakline
{parent=Fluid mechanics}
{wiki}

A <streakline> at observation time $T$ is the set of present positions of all <fluid elements> that passed through one fixed release point $\mathbf a$ during a specified history of injection. For continuous release over $\tau_0\leq\tau\leq T$, solve a different <initial value problem> for each release time $\tau$:
$$
\frac{\partial\mathbf X}{\partial t}(t;\tau,\mathbf a)=\mathbf u(\mathbf X(t;\tau,\mathbf a),t),\qquad \mathbf X(\tau;\tau,\mathbf a)=\mathbf a,\qquad \mathcal S_T=\{\mathbf X(T;\tau,\mathbf a):\tau_0\leq\tau\leq T\}.
$$
Thus a <pathline> varies $t$ for one particle; a <streakline> varies $\tau$ while holding $T$ fixed. Interrupted release gives only the corresponding subsets of the <streakline>. A moving nozzle requires a time-dependent release position instead, so the standard fixed-point definition should not silently be applied to it. Continuous release makes <streaklines> easy to observe and useful for showing where material from one source has reached. https://web.mit.edu/13.021/demos/lectures/lecture2.pdf[MIT Marine Hydrodynamics, lecture 2, section 1.2]{ref}

In the analytical <velocity field> $(U,at)$ of <pathline>, a particle released at the origin at time $\tau$ follows
$$
x(t;\tau)=U(t-\tau),\qquad y(t;\tau)=\frac a2(t^2-\tau^2),\qquad t\geq\tau.
$$
Observe all particles together at time $T$, after continuous release from $\tau=0$:
$$
x=U(T-\tau),\qquad y=\frac a2(T^2-\tau^2),\qquad y=\frac{aT}{U}x-\frac{a}{2U^2}x^2,\qquad 0\leq x\leq UT.
$$
For $U=1\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, $a=1\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$ and $T=2\ \mathrm s$, the <streakline> is the downward-curving <parabola> $y=2x-x^2/2$ in metre coordinates. Releases at $\tau=0,1,2\ \mathrm s$ occupy $(2,2)$, $(1,1.5)$ and $(0,0)\ \mathrm m$, respectively. At $x=1\ \mathrm m$, the <streakline> lies above the <pathline> $y=x^2/2$ and below the origin's <streamline> $y=2x$. All particles nevertheless have the same present <velocity> $(1,2)\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. The <streakline> slope $dy/dx=a\tau/U$ generally differs from the present <velocity> slope $aT/U$; they agree at the nozzle. The oldest particle is the downstream endpoint, which explains why the <pathline> and <streakline> meet there without being the same curve.

\Image[stream-path-streak-lines-release-family.png]
{title=Analytical release families observed at one and two seconds}

Each panel fixes one observation time. Coloured dots are the present positions of particles with the indicated release times; dashed <pathlines> show those particles' earlier histories. The black <streakline> connects present positions of the continuously released family, including release times between the sampled dots. After $T=1\ \mathrm s$, particles already present keep moving and new ones enter at the origin, so the later <streakline> is not a frozen record of the earlier one. Injection beginning only at $\tau=1\ \mathrm s$ would show just $0\leq x\leq1\ \mathrm m$ of the $T=2\ \mathrm s$ curve. Both panels are diffusion-free analytical models, not experimental photographs.

Kline's contracting-channel demonstration switches from marking an element to continuous bubble production at a marking wire: the resulting trace is a <streakline>. The apparatus therefore changes the curve being displayed by changing the marking history, even with the same channel. A wire produces a family of traces; conceptually each fixed emitting location supplies its own <streakline>. Individual bubbles are markers rather than perfect <fluid elements>: Jean Hertzberg's teaching notes describe <hydrogen> production by <electrolysis> at a thin <cathode> wire and warn that larger bubbles have more <buoyancy>. https://web.mit.edu/hml/ncfmf/05FV.pdf[S. J. Kline, Flow Visualization film notes, figure 8]{ref} https://www.flowvis.org/Flow%20Vis%20Guide/particles-3-in-water/[Jean Hertzberg and Flow Vis course students, Particles 3: In Water]{ref}

A dye version uses a small fixed injection port upstream in a transparent water channel, a dilute premix of dye with the channel water, illumination and a fixed <optical camera>. Start continuous injection at a recorded time $\tau_0$ and take a short exposure at $T$: the visible filament approximates a <streakline>, not the accumulated <pathline> of one particle. Repeat with the same port and imaging geometry after changing the flow with time; compare the filament against local directions estimated from short displacements of sparse markers at $T$. Agreement in a <steady flow> and possible disagreement when the flow changes are the predictions being tested; no measured curves are claimed here. The protocol follows Hertzberg's advice to use small ports, little injected volume and a dilute premix with matched <density> and <viscosity>.
